Asahi Linux Progress Linux 7.3
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-07
05:20:18 UTC
14:20:18 KST

Progress Report: Linux 7.3
Well here we are again. Linux 7.3 is out and it’s been an eventful few months, so let’s get stuck in to another Asahi Linux progress report!
Upstreaming continues
As always, upstreaming work continues to progress. Aside from the usual patches for low-level SoC blocks, two major drivers made it on to the list this cycle: macaudio and avd. Getting macaudio ready for submission has been a challenge. The audio subsystem on Apple Silicon devices is rather special, and required quite a number of core ALSA changes to properly support it. Apple also uses special variants of speaker and headset amplifiers that were already supported upstream, and these drivers also needed some pretty significant modifications. We have previously discussed a lot of this work at length, and we are finally in a position where we can submit the main driver for review. The driver and Devicetree changes represent a hefty chunk of our total patchset, about 3100 lines.
AVD is a whole different beast. The AVD driver has spent barely 3 months in our downstream tree and is already on the list. This is our shortest turnaround time yet for a major SoC component and bodes well for a transition to upstream-first development some time in the future. This is fantastic news, as the AVD driver and related Devicetree changes are around 13000 lines of code.
Also making an appearance on the list this cycle are intial Devicetrees not only for M4 Pro and Max devices but also M5 thanks to Yureka’s tireless efforts. As usual these initial Devicetrees are barebones and only sufficient to boot the SoC to a TTY, however work is progressing on further enablement. Watch this space!
We have also begun shifting to an upstream-first approach where practical. Changes to the TI USB-PD and SPMI drivers required for new devices, as well as initial support for Thunderbolt, were all developed upstream-first. We are so thrilled to see our focus on upstreaming starting to pay off!
M3 goes GA
Last time, we announced initial support for M3 series machines via the EXPERT flag on the Asahi installer. We are pleased to announce that we received almost no issues from early adopters, so M3 support is now generally available without the EXPERT flag!
We have also made a number of improvements to M3 support, most notably enabling DCP. On top of performance and battery life improvements, this also allows us to enable sleep on M3 series devices and make the area around the notch useable on the MacBooks! Keep an eye out for m1n1 and kernel updates in the coming days.
Crowded House
Last time, we teased support for virtualising SPTM-enabled versions of macOS in m1n1, which enables reverse engineering on M4 series machines. M5 presents a new challenge, as the versions of macOS which support it all require the use of Exclaves.
Exclaves are small applications running in an isolated environment controlled by the Secure Kernel. On M5 series SoCs and above, macOS uses them for sensitive tasks such as drawing the “camera in use” indicator to the screen. XNU communicates with the Secure Kernel and its Exclaves via SPTM, using yet another IPC protocol called Tightbeam. Much as with SPTM, XNU will panic if it cannot find the running Exclaves. This is all starting to look very similar in concept to Arm’s own TrustZone feature.
While some Exclaves can be bypassed by changing some settings on the command line, we predict that this will not always be the case. As such, Sven started looking at how we could fit all of this in to m1n1. First line of investigation, try putting the Secure Kernel and Exclave userspace in another VM next to SPTM and macOS.
Oh, that worked, and we’re now up to spawning three VMs in EL1 under m1n1! Maybe we can start selling m1n1 to companies fleeing VMWare… With that, we can now virtualise and reverse engineer macOS on both M4 and M5 series machines.
Hello Triangle(s)!
While M3 support is now mostly at feature parity with M1 and M2, the very obvious exception to this is the GPU. Apple’s GPU team seems to follow a tick-tock model, and the M3’s GPU was a very significant departure from the models found on M1 and M2 SoCs. We mentioned quite some time ago that the M3 GPU was being reverse engineered by a new group of contributors, however not much has been said since.
At XDC this year, Alyssa Millburn, Anna Maniscalco and TellowKrinkle gave a talk on their progress reverse engineering the M3 GPU. Work has progressed far enough for m1n1 to successfully initialise the GPU and for the team to draw a triangle! Fans of the “Think Different” sections of these progress reports will find the talk edifying; Apple have once again made a number of… interesting choices which will require significant engineering effort to integrate into upstream Mesa. It is well worth a watch if you’re interested in more detail.
A huge thanks goes out to everyone who has made this work possible. More progress is on the way, so stay tuned.
Kept you waiting, huh?
All the way back in January of 2024, we made the mistake of promising Thunderbolt support within the year. It’s a mistake we won’t quickly repeat. We’ve said almost nothing about Thunderbolt in the two years since, which begs the question: where is it?
Thunderbolt was originally developed by Apple and Intel, with Intel handling the design and fabrication of the hardware required. Since Intel made these controllers available to anyone who wanted them at a (mostly) reasonable price, no one else bothered to design their own. It is only very recently — largely coinciding with the transfer of the standard to the USB-IF — that companies like AMD have decided to roll their own controllers. Apple is of course no exception.
As part of their push for insourcing most silicon IP, Apple made the choice to design their own Thunderbolt controller before most others. Surprising no one, the controller is of course a Cortex-M3 running RTKit coupled with a bespoke Thunderbolt/USB4 router. There is one of these for each Thunderbolt-enabled USB-C port on all Apple Silicon Macs.
Since Intel supplies every other extant Thunderbolt controller on the market, the Linux kernel’s Thunderbolt subsystem is effectively designed around them. It makes a number of assumptions about the hardware topology of the running machine, including hardware capabilities and access patterns for certain registers and regions of shared memory. Apple’s controller breaks a lot of these assumptions. Great.
The Thunderbolt controller is also oddly coupled to PMGR. The reset line for each controller is asserted or deasserted by setting and clearing a set of bits in the middle of PMGR’s MMIO range. No other hardware block works quite like this, and it took some time to realise that these seemingly random writes to PMGR were not just noise.
Luckily for us, Sven was up to the task of dealing with all this nonsense. It just took slightly longer than expected! While the patchset to enable Thunderbolt was sent upstream first (another massive win for us!), we have also pulled it into our downstream kernel and are now shipping it to users!
But there is a catch. Currently, only USB3, XDomain and Thunderbolt Networking are supported. You can plug in USB devices or connect two Thunderbolt computers together and make them ping each other, but that’s about it. PCIe and DisplayPort tunnelling — required for most Thunderbolt docks — are not supported. Yet. Watch this space.
An enormous amount of gratitude is owed to Sven, who has stuck with this thankless chore for nearly 5 years in between all of his other incredible work.
“The Holy Grail, Doctor Jones”
Playing retail PC games on Asahi Linux is a bit of a pain point, to say the least. The software stack required to enable this is incredibly complex. We need to translate x86/amd64 binaries to arm64, and do so fast enough for low-latency gaming. In most cases, we also need to deal with the Windows API. On top of that, almost all games bake in an assumed 4 KiB memory page size to enable certain performance optimisation. Over the past few years, most of these problems have been solved:
- FEX has improved leaps and bounds. The performance and reliability of binary translation is hardly a consideration in most cases now;
- WINE is also fantastic now. Support for ARM64EC is mature and reliable, meaning that FEX now only has to translate the game binaries rather than WINE’s entire Windows API implementation; and
- muvm allows us to spin up a thin VM with DRM Native Context to present a 4 KiB memory page size to x86/amd64 applications.
When muvm was first developed, it was with the assumption that DRM Native Context would enable near-native GPU performance from inside the VM. Unfortunately, this assumption did not hold. The muvm performance penalty is quite significant for a number of reasons, about 20% depending on the workload. Engineering around the limitations inherent to using a VM also introduces issues; muvm cannot use Wayland, has slow I/O performance, and struggles with certain types of input devices.
This is one area where macOS has us completely licked. While the performance gap between FEX and Rosetta has narrowed, XNU allows userspace processes to map themselves at any page granularity, so all Rosetta needs to do is tell XNU to map 4 KiB pages for its child processes. Linux does not have this flexibility. All pages must be mapped at the same granularity as the kernel. While we did tease Windows games working outside of muvm last year, this relied on ugly hacks inside FEX which have since been removed in the interests of maintainability and translation correctness. Bummer. Even so, compatibility was hit-and-miss, with a lot of (mostly older) software still refusing to work. Double bummer.
Android is currently going through a transition from 4 KiB pages to 16 KiB pages for a variety of reasons. Google Play now requires apps to be built for 16 KiB systems, and any application updates which do not test successfully on such systems will not be approved for publication on the Play Store. What about applications which are no longer updated though? What about sideloaded enterprise slop apps?
Google engineers Kalesh Singh and Frederick Mayle gave a very interesting talk at Linux Plumbers Conference this year about exactly this. Their solution is simple: just fix Linux so that userspace can map 4 KiB pages!
Their addition to the Android kernel is called P3S, or Per Process Page Size. On ARM64 systems, pagetable granularity is controlled by bits set in the Translation Control Registers (TCR). P3S works by tracking the granularity of each process’s pagetables, and writing the appropriate bits to the TCR whenever a process with a different pagetable size is switched to. Because this is a single register write, and because this is a no-op for “native” page size processes, the performance penalty is effectively zero!
While this effort is primarily targeted at Android, the patchset mostly rebases cleanly on top of mainline Linux. What happens if we rebase it on top of our Asahi tree (and fix some bugs)? As it turns out:
Okay okay, Battlefront is a very simple Direct3D 9.0 game from 22 years ago. Not exactly an impressive demo, right? Well what about:
Wolfenstein: The New Order is an extremely well-optimised OpenGL title, and being 64-bit uses WINE’s ARM64EC pathways to massively reduce the amount of work FEX has to do. Again, not exactly an impressive demo if we’re judging based on technical difficulty.
Let’s find something else. Something… worse. Something 32-bit so that FEX and WINE have to work harder. Something that will exercise DXVK too. Something that is so comically buggy and poorly optimised that it doesn’t even work properly on real Windows machines, and relies on community patches and command line switches to start at all on modern computers:
That’s more like it! But we can do even worse. We can even run… Bethesda games!
Performance is of course still patchy. Both Battlefront and Wolfenstein run essentially flawlessly, with the latter hitting 144+ FPS on an M2 Max Mac Studio. GTA IV on the other hand is… not fantastic. Performance is still massively improved over using muvm, and eliminating the use of the VM in general should also eliminate a few classes of bugs. Debugging the GPU driver should also be much easier with this approach.
As always there is a catch, and unfortunately it’s a big one. The current solution is hard-coded for 16 KiB hosts and 4 KiB processes on those hosts. This obviously satisfies Google’s commercial requirements, but adding pagesize-specific kludges to the kernel’s core memory management code is not appropriate upstream. We are unsure if Google intends to generalise this approach and upstream it, or if this is going to stay an Android-only patchset. As such, we do not want to ship this in our downstream tree and have folks become reliant on it when there is this level of uncertainty.
It also requires userspace integration. P3S works by checking the binary metadata for its sections’ page alignments. This works for Google’s use case, as P3S is intended as a fallback mechanism for applications aligned to 4 KiB pages. Modern arm64 binaries are aligned to 64 KiB however, as this guarantees compatibility with systems of all native pagesizes. Thus, a shim is required to force P3S on. The shim triggers P3S, and any child processes will inherit its 4 KiB pagesize. It is not worth spending the effort on implementing this until we have some certainty around what Google intends to do with this patchset.
We have reached out to Google with some questions, and we hope to have more to say soon.
Mics, but faster
Our custom beamformer for the microphone array found in the MacBooks is written in Rust. Rust’s scientific computing ecosystem is not as mature as C++’s, which is a bit of a problem as beamforming requires realtime FFT and very heavy matrix operations to be done on buffers filled with audio samples. Mature scientific computing libraries have hand-optimised code, often using intrinsics tied to the target ISA. We were not doing this however, and relied on the compiler autovectorising code where it could to achieve our performance targets.
This fell apart when we eliminated the FFT-based analytic transform, replacing it with an implementation based on infinite impulse response (IIR) filters. This should be faster, however the compiler was failing to autovectorise the new implementation. Linkmauve kindly stepped in and added SIMD-optimised code for both arm64 and amd64. This, coupled with other performance optimisations courtesy of Frédéric Bour has resulted in Triforce becoming almost twice as fast, all while using less CPU time!
Keeping up
Fedora Linux 45 was released on 3 November, and we shipped Fedora Asahi Remix on the same day. This is the first time we have been able to get a new version of Fedora Asahi Remix out on the same day as upstream!
Learning from history
We take great pride in our work, and have always done all of our development in the open. We communicate with our users, our followers and our financial sponsors as transparently as is humanly possible. Our finances are in the open, our development branches are in the open, and we report our progress regularly and candidly in the open. Our IRC channels and Matrix rooms are safe spaces, open to anyone with even a passing interest in Asahi Linux or hacking on Apple Silicon.
We do all of this because we firmly believe in Free and Open Source Software, not just as a means to an end but as the end in and of itself. The modern digital world is only possible because of the Free Software movement, and it is more important now than it ever has been that we do all we can to protect the Free Software ecosystem and uphold the ideals upon which it is built.
It may surprise some to learn that WINE was originally made available under the MIT licence. The 90s and 2000s were a very different time in the Free Software ecosystem, and permissively licenced projects were viewed by tech companies as free real estate to pilfer and exploit. WINE was not immune to this, and many monetised proprietary forks of WINE were marketed and sold by various bad actors. The WINE development team grew increasingly concerned that these proprietary forks, while well within their rights to exist, would eventually cause so much fragmentation in the ecosystem that WINE itself would become unviable. In 2002, WINE was relicenced under the LGPL to prevent further proprietary forks from being created, and to encourage direct upstream contribution.
When Asahi Linux was first started, it was with the intent that we develop a Free Software ecosystem for the Apple Silicon platform. To facilitate and encourage the particiation of our friends at the BSDs and other Free Software operating systems, we endeavoured to licence as much of our code as possible under the permissive MIT licence.
Unfortunately, this is no longer viable. The malicious practices of the 90s and 2000s have reared their ugly heads once again, this time driven in large part by both the insatiable appetite of frontier AI labs for high-quality training data, and militant animosity toward the Free Software community.
Some time ago, we were made aware of multiple attempts to exploit our permissive licensing to hijack our work, with a direct view to replacing the project and aggressively monetising Linux on Apple Silicon. In some extreme cases, in-development feature code was being lifted straight out of our personal forks and published without attribution or lazily rewritten with LLM assistance to obfuscate its origin. The MIT licence explicitly allows this, and has no carveouts to protect against bad faith actors.
As such, we made the difficult decision to begin the process of transitioning all of our repositories to the GNU General Public License version 2 or later. Going forward all Asahi Linux code will be licenced under strong copyleft licences only. Those who do not learn from history are doomed to repeat it.
Until next time!
As always, we would like to sincerely thank our supporters on OpenCollective and GitHub Sponsors. It is only through your generous backing that we are able to dedicate the time and resources to continuing our work reverse engineering the platform and bringing to it a world-class, polished, and battle-tested Linux desktop experience. You mean the world to us.

====================================================================================================

Mature scientific computing libraries have hand-optimised code, often using intrinsics tied to the target ISA. We were not doing this however, and relied on the compiler autovectorising code where it could to achieve our performance targets.
This fell apart when we eliminated the FFT-based analytic transform, replacing it with an implementation based on infinite impulse response (IIR) filters. This should be faster, however the compiler was failing to autovectorise the new implementation. Linkmauve kindly stepped in and added SIMD-optimised code for both arm64 and amd64. This, coupled with other performance optimisations courtesy of Frédéric Bour has resulted in Triforce becoming almost twice as fast, all while using less CPU time!
Keeping up
Fedora Linux 45 was released on 3 November, and we shipped Fedora Asahi Remix on the same day. This is the first time we have been able to get a new version of Fedora Asahi Remix out on the same day as upstream!
Learning from history
We take great pride in our work, and have always done all of our development in the open. We communicate with our users, our followers and our financial sponsors as transparently as is humanly possible. Our finances are in the open, our development branches are in the open, and we report our progress regularly and candidly in the open. Our IRC channels and Matrix rooms are safe spaces, open to anyone with even a passing interest in Asahi Linux or hacking on Apple Silicon.
We do all of this because we firmly believe in Free and Open Source Software, not just as a means to an end but as the end in and of itself. The modern digital world is only possible because of the Free Software movement, and it is more important now than it ever has been that we do all we can to protect the Free Software ecosystem and uphold the ideals upon which it is built.
It may surprise some to learn that WINE was originally made available under the MIT licence. The 90s and 2000s were a very different time in the Free Software ecosystem, and permissively licenced projects were viewed by tech companies as free real estate to pilfer and exploit. WINE was not immune to this, and many monetised proprietary forks of WINE were marketed and sold by various bad actors. The WINE development team grew increasingly concerned that these proprietary forks, while well within their rights to exist, would eventually cause so much fragmentation in the ecosystem that WINE itself would become unviable. In 2002, WINE was relicenced under the LGPL to prevent further proprietary forks from being created, and to encourage direct upstream contribution.
When Asahi Linux was first started, it was with the intent that we develop a Free Software ecosystem for the Apple Silicon platform. To facilitate and encourage the particiation of our friends at the BSDs and other Free Software operating systems, we endeavoured to licence as much of our code as possible under the permissive MIT licence.
Unfortunately, this is no longer viable. The malicious practices of the 90s and 2000s have reared their ugly heads once again, this time driven in large part by both the insatiable appetite of frontier AI labs for high-quality training data, and militant animosity toward the Free Software community.
Some time ago, we were made aware of multiple attempts to exploit our permissive licensing to hijack our work, with a direct view to replacing the project and aggressively monetising Linux on Apple Silicon. In some extreme cases, in-development feature code was being lifted straight out of our personal forks and published without attribution or lazily rewritten with LLM assistance to obfuscate its origin. The MIT licence explicitly allows this, and has no carveouts to protect against bad faith actors.
As such, we made the difficult decision to begin the process of transitioning all of our repositories to the GNU General Public License version 2 or later. Going forward all Asahi Linux code will be licenced under strong copyleft licences only. Those who do not learn from history are doomed to repeat it.
Until next time!
As always, we would like to sincerely thank our supporters on OpenCollective and GitHub Sponsors. It is only through your generous backing that we are able to dedicate the time and resources to continuing our work reverse engineering the platform and bringing to it a world-class, polished, and battle-tested Linux desktop experience. You mean the world to us.

Sequoia shutting down its Scouts program marks the end of an era
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-07
05:01:54 UTC
14:01:54 KST

Sequoia this week announced a broad overhaul in a memo posted to X, a move that includes closing its London office and shutting down its scout program. Observers warn that it would be a mistake to read too much into the London closure, since the British capital has a thriving AI scene. On the other hand, the decision to end scouts says more about where venture investing is headed.
That’s partly because 17 years ago, Sequoia pioneered the scouts model, in which select founders and operators write checks on the firm’s behalf. Amid increased competition for deal flow, many VC firms followed suit. But a consensus now seems to be emerging that the model has reached its limits in its current form.
Not everyone was sold on the model in the first place. “They pioneered the scout program but [to be honest] it never made sense to me!” VC Sheel Mohnot wrote on X this week. “The scouts themselves don’t really care that much about the investments, you can still miss (like they did with Uber), not sure what it gets you. I suspect other firms will shutter theirs too,” he added.
To be clear, Sequoia didn’t make a case against scouts in the memo. Instead, it framed its decision as part of a broader need for more focus. But its goal of “putting more energy behind fewer people and things” is likely to resonate with others who’ve questioned the ‘spray and pray’ approach to scouting.
Rival firm Andreessen Horowitz (a16z), for instance, rolled out a global scout network aimed at spotting opportunities in the U.S. and also in smaller European hubs. But a16z partner Gabriel Vasquez told TechCrunch earlier this year that the firm had revamped its program to double down “on the best people.”
Under this new approach, Vasquez explained, scouts get more resources but are also expected to be more active, such as by co-hosting dinners with the firm. “They get more budget, but we have reduced the number,” he said.
This also seems to indicate that it is VC firms that have been driving an overhaul of the model. For founders, being a scout is attractive: it brings them closer to firms they may want checks from someday, or even a partner role with, and being seen as someone who can write a check can raise their standing among other founders.
Some scouts will undoubtedly mourn the end of Sequoia’s program. Among them is Station F boss Roxanne Varza, who wrote on LinkedIn that she was “sad” to hear that the program was getting cut off, and “hope[d] that other funds [w]on’t follow suit.”
Varza, a former Sequoia scout from 2020 to 2025, told TechCrunch that she had no information beyond what has been shared publicly, but observed that becoming a scout had often been a stepping stone for aspiring investment managers.
“I cannot name all of the incredible scouts that have gone on to join or build funds, but there are quite a few. And the scout programs may have helped them get there — allowed them to build an investment track record, allowed them to build relationships with various funds directly, and more,” she wrote to TechCrunch.
According to Varza, the program’s impact went beyond the individual level, instead helping the entire ecosystem become more mature. “I believe they have helped to foster the investment culture and early-stage investment landscape that we now see in Europe, for example,” Varza said.
While others may agree with Varza about the role these programs have played, they may still have run their course for two reasons. First, seasoned operators may get more financial upside as angels. When it’s their own money, they get to keep the returns, too. Second, leading VC firms are now big and well-known enough that the best founders tend to come to them, so they worry less about missing deals, except at the very earliest stages or in niches and geographies where they have less reach.
Still, the pull of those early and niche deals is why scouts programs are much more likely to get revamped than to disappear entirely. “While Sequoia may be cutting the program, venture partner-style deals are likely to continue,” said Varza in her email to TechCrunch. “Scouts was just a way to scale the format. I don’t imagine Sequoia would end all forms of venture partners, but they are full of surprises.”

From the front row to the hot seat — what the midterms mean for Big Tech
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-07
04:16:43 UTC
13:16:43 KST

Right before the midterm elections, OpenAI hired Jessica Schumer, daughter of Democratic Sen. Chuck Schumer. The company also said it would start calling its data centers “compute campuses.”
For the past two years, many of Silicon Valley’s biggest names have built closer ties with President Donald Trump and his administration. Several attended White House events, some companies scaled back diversity, equity and inclusion programs, and some executives donated millions to super PACs supporting his campaign and allied candidates. Administration policies also benefited the industry, including tax breaks, the pausing of some federal investigations, and looser AI regulation. Meanwhile, Meta faced lawsuits and billion-dollar fines, and OpenAI faced suits alleging its technology harmed users.
Now, with the midterms shifting the balance of power in Washington, those same companies are working to rebuild ties with Democrats, who have pledged to tighten oversight of AI. How far that gets them may depend less on image-polishing than on where the real fights are happening: in state capitols, in communities deciding whether to host data centers, and, as we’ve seen this past week, in the markets watching the AI boom.
J.B. Branch, director of federal AI governance and technology policy at Public Citizen, a nonprofit advocacy group that describes itself as nonpartisan, told me that the tech industry “realizes they have an optics problem.” He characterized moves like the Schumer hire and the rebranding as “PR washing.” He expects more of it, too, pointing to OpenAI’s partnership with the American Federation of Teachers, announced last year, as well as Anthropic’s newly launched Claude Corps and Meta’s America’s Workforce Academy. “I would imagine more of that sort of investment in these corporate, private-public partnerships,” he said.
These initiatives didn’t spring up overnight. Meta and other tech companies reportedly began looking for ways to strengthen their ties to Democrats as Democratic gains in the midterms looked more likely. Reached for comment about the midterm elections’ impact on Meta, a company spokesperson pointed TechCrunch to remarks that Meta President Dina Powell McCormick made this week at WSJ Tech. “You have to work with the government that the American people elect,” McCormick had said. “I have seen it across the board that you have to be bipartisan, and you want to be, because you want buy-in on the things that you’re trying to do.”
Not everyone expects them to work. Alice Marwick, director of research at the tech policy nonprofit Data & Society, told me she expects Big Tech to try to pivot back toward the middle, “if not the left. But it’s a little too late,” she said. “Tech companies should be asking themselves how their products can make people’s lives better, because right now, it doesn’t seem like they are.”
If Democrats follow through on pledges to tighten oversight of AI and Big Tech, the industry could face a tougher environment. Data center moratoriums are gaining traction, and a wave of AI regulation bills awaits drafting. At the same event, Powell McCormick said Meta wants to show that communities welcoming its investments will see “an enormous amount of benefit,” while those that don’t want them “will of course have the right to stop them.”
Still, the next two years could bring political gridlock in Washington. Aryan Ranjan, founder of Baraza, a political campaign management software company, told me most action will likely happen at the state level, since states have already sued tech companies and passed regulations. “A coalition built on newer, working-class voters is likely to push Democrats to frame AI around electricity bills, data-center siting and jobs rather than abstract AI safety,” he said of what to expect at the federal level.
Last December, the Trump administration signed an executive order creating the AI Litigation Task Force, a federal group meant to challenge state AI laws. The administration has argued that U.S. AI companies “must be free to innovate without cumbersome regulations.” Many states have nonetheless passed their own laws, including measures on chatbots and deepfakes.
It also remains to be seen how President Trump will respond, including whether he’ll sign more AI executive orders or veto bills that could be seen as anti-AI. Clearly, voters want stronger guardrails around the technology.
In a statement to TechCrunch, a White House official said: “American leadership in super intelligence is essential for our national security, economic prosperity, and the quality of life of the American people. President Trump is committed to ensuring America continues to lead the world in super intelligence while protecting American consumers. As the President has said, he’s willing to work with anyone to continue delivering victories for hardworking Americans.”
Branch argued that Trump’s political fortunes are now tied to the AI industry. On Wednesday, the day after the midterms, a trillion-dollar market sell-off spooked investors, some are whom are reportedly now reconsidering Anthropic’s reported trillion-dollar IPO. “There’s a very strong likelihood that there will be an AI bubble pop, and it’ll happen on Trump’s hands,” Branch said, predicting a possible market collapse. “Does he want to sink or swim along with the AI industry?”
For now, the rules companies face may vary by location. “For the next two years, a company’s AI rules may depend more on where it operates rather than on anything Washington passes,” Ranjan said.

Lighting the Skies
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-07
03:40:34 UTC
12:40:34 KST

lighting the skies
Someone stated in the early days of the war, a feeling that was reciprocated by the soldier in khaki and the sailor in blue, that a mechanical device would be the determining factor in ending the world conflict, favorably, of course, for the United States and her allies. 1
One of my common topics is the history of nuclear weapons. The "atom bomb" is a rich vein of history because its impact has been as much cultural as technical. Dramatic depictions like Christopher Nolan's Oppenheimer have recently enforced, perhaps even revived, the perception of nuclear weapons as an innovation that forever changed warfare. More quietly, historians and strategists tend to wonder if that is still true today, or if it was ever as true as was supposed—the conventional firebombing of Tokyo probably claimed as many victims as the nuclear attacks on Hiroshima and Nagasaki, for example. Besides, it's not unusual to hear people overstate the significance of nuclear weapons to WWII in a way that is obvious with a brief inspection of the timeline. The technology became available only very late in the war, and for that reason if no other, it must have had less of an impact on the outcome than other revolutionary wartime developments like radar.
Why is it that radar, a complex technology that fundamentally changed the landscape of aerial combat, seems so pedestrian? Perhaps because the technology has far more non-military applications, and so is more familiar to our everyday lives. Perhaps because the development of radar was scattered between countries and institutions without the involvement of such intriguing figureheads as J. Robert Oppenheimer (although one wonders if this is because we have not looked as close; certainly radar figures like Henry Tizard had colorful lives as well). Perhaps because radar just isn't as exciting: it does not put on anything like the show of a nuclear detonation.
Today, we consider another such step in military technology. This is an invention that forever changed the battlefield, and it's one that does put on a show. In fact, that has become the technology's main purpose. From the 1870 siege of Paris to the modern used car lot, searchlights have marked many of history's great conflicts—and great deals.
The concept of electric light has been known since the very early 19th century. An electric arc lamp, or at least one of their ancestors, was demonstrated in 1806. Because of the widely known story of Thomas Edison's incandescent lamp, most people know that electric light had a very difficult journey from idea to practical device. That path was not as simple as what we are taught in schools: besides the several other inventors with a good claim to the lightbulb, incandescent lights were just one of a few technologies that developed in parallel.
Before controlled incandescence, the principal form of electric light was still an arc. Arc lamps also did not fall to incandescent bulbs as quickly as you might think. Arc lamps remained the preferred choice for very bright, high-CRI lighting into the mid-20th century, and reached what will likely remain their ultimate form in the late 1960s, as the 15 kW Xenon arc lamps used by IMAX projectors. Along the way, arc lamps displaced many of the early forms of artificial lights: Oxy-hydrogen candles, known as "limelights," fell out of theatrical use. "Moonlight towers" began the displacement of gas lights for street lighting.
And the military—the military readily adopted this new form of reliable, practical, powerful lighting.
Calling In Light
The effect of artificial lighting on military tactics could fill a book. I will focus on one narrow part of this story, but it will be helpful to set the stage with a few of the earlier developments. For much of the pre-electricity era of military history, artificial lighting was mostly a disadvantage. Fires, torches, and other practical sources of light were bright enough to reveal one's location but not bright enough to search anyone else out. Ironically, the main interest that military researchers had in light was often to suppress or conceal it.
The chemical light technology developed for theaters and displays had promise, though. One of the first recorded uses of a powerful light for a military venture (and, as far as I can tell, the very first recorded use of a searchlight) came in the context of the American Civil War. The Union Navy sought to economically attack the Confederacy by maintaining a persistent blockade on southern ports; the Confederacy sought to maintain trade, often by sending out "blockade runners" under cover of night. A Union ship, perhaps frustrated by firing aimlessly into the dark, improvised: a theatrical light was mounted to a crude reflector, forming the first naval searchlight. It wasn't a very good one, it was dim and unfocused and the sailors struggled even to keep it lit under maritime conditions. It was good enough, though. By first following the sound, and then casting the light, the Union ship was able to engage the Confederate vessel even on a moonless night. Amusingly, while this incident is widely described in histories of military signals and lights, I have found none that state the outcome. Perhaps it was unfavorable to the victors.
Electric arc lamps were far more suitable to military use: simpler to operate (although not as simple as we might today expect), more flexible in their placement, and by the end of the Civil War brighter. The 1870 Siege of Paris, part of the Franco-Prussian War, was a bellwether in the history of military aviation. Cut off on the ground, the French made extensive use of balloons (a technology they had pioneered in the 18th century) to move people and mail. In the process, several records for aerial travel were set. The French enjoyed air superiority and had little need for air defense, but the situation on the ground was much more troublesome. Prussian forces mounted lengthy artillery bombardments, causing devastation limited mostly by the range of their cannons. For the soldiers manning Paris's fortifications, it was important not only to keep attackers outside of the city, but to keep them as far away as possible.
Paris, not for nothing known as the "City of Light," had seen demonstrations of arc lamps back to 1850. The invention of the arc regulator (which advanced the carbon rods to keep the arc intact) and improved batteries made the lights more practical by 1870, and during the early days of the siege "electricians" were hastily recruited to the city's defense. They manned a variety of arc lamps installed around the city, limiting the cover of darkness for nighttime assaults. At the summit of Montmartre hill, a position with a clear view over one of the most vulnerable parts of the city's defense, they installed one of the most impressive lights ever built. Not only was it larger than the others, it could operate for longer: the power supply was not a battery, but a dynamo powered by a steam engine. Besides that leap into modern design, the Montmartre light also presents an early example of "tipping and cueing" in intelligence gathering. French intelligence agents, having caught wind of a surprise attack, sent a message to Montmartre where the light's skilled operators aimed it by heading and elevation before applying power to catch the enemy in quite a compromising position.
A decade later, the military searchlight had become a standard technology. The Royal Navy regularly equipped ships with dynamo-powered, carbon-arc searchlights starting around 1882. They found that, besides their role in aiming weapons, searchlights could be something of a weapon on their own. Aiming searchlights back at coast defense artillery positions not only exposed them to incoming fire, it also dazzled the gunners and frustrated their efforts to accurately aim at the ships. This principle was memorably demonstrated by a 1903 US joint maneuver exercise, in which the Navy's Atlantic Fleet mounted a simulated attack on Long Island Sound. All together, on cue, the ships trained their searchlights on the main defensive battery at Fort Michie. The soldiers at the fort didn't manage a single shot, they were so blinded by the lights—and limited in their ability to even move around the battlements, since they could so plainly be seen (and fired upon).
As the military developed searchlights, they were quickly applied to civilian use. Limited availability of electricity, and limited need for such powerful lighting, meant that searchlights wouldn't yet become part of the urban landscape. They were reserved for special events, especially those with an emphasis on technical demonstrations. Of course, such events were bounteous around the turn of the century in the form of various world fairs and expositions. Perhaps the first use of searchlights as a device for entertainment came at the 1893 Chicago World's Fair, which was extensively lit by electricity. A total of 17,000 horsepower of steam engines (so, yes, presumably steam horsepower, a curiously different unit from the one we are familiar with today) powered thousands of arc lights.
Most of these were for illuminating the fairgrounds and its many monuments, but some were installed for the sake of light itself. On the roof of the Liberal Arts Building, a searchlight claimed to be the most powerful yet made could light a newspaper well enough to comfortably read at a distance of eight miles. Of course, it was more than just a book light—it was a way to attract attention. Unlike the military context, civilian searchlights were deployed specifically to be seen. Rooftop searchlights became a common feature of large expositions, and by lazily searching the sky they established an atmosphere while guiding curious onlookers to the site of the event.
Even this early, it was known that searchlights could be a powerful form of advertising. At the Chicago World's Fair, the upstart Cloud Projector Company demonstrated a searchlight with a lens system that could focus a slide onto the cloud layer. It was literally a cloud projector, in the modern sense, and giant words floating ethereally in the sky announced the fair's upcoming special events.
In 1901, the Pan-American Exposition was underway at Niagara. Among the attractions was a demonstration of electrical arcs produced by a 40 kV transformer recently installed at the Niagara Falls power station. Alongside that light show, a second, more controlled form of arc was displayed: searchlights. These were described as an application of military technology, and specifically of military signaling technology. Searchlights were installed atop the "Electric Tower" at the exposition grounds, and at a viewing tower erected at Niagara Falls proper. At night, the two lights—22 miles apart—were each plainly visible from the other tower. The military was, of course, well aware of this potential use and as soon as searchlights had been installed on Navy ships they came into use as a means of communication.
Extensive newspaper coverage of the Exposition means that we know more details of these machines. The searchlights used for the tower demonstrations had 30" reflectors, and consumed 80 amps at about 50 volts for 4 kW give or take. This was indeed a very bright light for the time, and at the Exposition, the searchlights were swung around among various targets. "As the beams of the projectors sweep across country," The Virginian-Pilot wrote, "the residents of the villages and country places will enjoy the illumination." Excitement for the lights was such that newspapers throughout the region published Morse code tables, in the hope that anyone in the region could decipher news bulletins that were sent from the signal lights.
Watching the Skies
Electric light is far from the only invention to make a dual appeal to spectacle and the military. From the Wrights' first controlled flight in 1903, it only took about seven years for the Navy to envision the aircraft carrier. The 1911 Italo-Turkish war featured armed biplanes, and as the hostilities of the First World War opened the US Army flew airplanes into Mexico, in pursuit of Pancho Villa. In 1915, the Austro-Hungarian Aviation Troops bombarded the Serbian city of Kragujevac from biplanes. Serbian soldiers fired on them with small arms, but had no success. A private, though, got a lucky shot with an artillery gun. This was the first instance of ground-air combat, and the first use of air defense.
By the latter part of WWI, aircraft were in routine use as both bombers and fighters. One tended to lead to the other: our 1915 takedown notwithstanding, bombers and reconnaissance planes enjoyed almost complete freedom of the skies in the first few years of the war, until Germany's fighters presented meaningful resistance. Ground-based air defense lagged behind, but in an interesting turn of aviation history, the held-over use of tethered balloons as observation posts provided a key motivation for improved anti-air artillery. These balloons were conspicuous and almost defenseless, so ground-based escorts accompanied them.
Balloons—and in the case of the Germans, Zeppelins—remained an important part of WWI air units. Besides their ability to hang around for an extended period of time, airships could carry much larger payloads than early bombers. Zeppelins occasionally loomed over London, raining down the era's most sophisticated destruction, from 1915 to 1917. These lumbering vessels had their vulnerabilities, though. Big, slow, and flammable, they were easy to take out with artillery fire if you could spot them. German air raids were conducted at night, and by the end of the war Zeppelins were being painted a non-reflective black.
By 1916, a network of 258 searchlights across England hunted the skies for airships. There were as many anti-aircraft guns, using the pencil-beams of the searchlights as aiming guides. Air defense was so new at the time that little military doctrine had developed around it, and Britain's first searchlight units were found in the Royal Engineers. As in France, the searchlight operators were known as electricians, specifically the Electrical Engineers.
These units had actually formed prior to the outbreak of WWI, for the purpose of coastal defense. None other than Chief Scout Lieutenant-General Robert Stephenson Smyth Baden-Powell (yes, the 1st Baron Baden-Powell) had improvised the use of a chemical searchlight to keep ships away from a besieged town during the Second Boer War; the Electrical Engineers had relieved the use of chemical lights in South Africa when they arrived with arc lights. By 1914, the Electrical Engineers had six companies in London as part of the Territorial Army. With the outbreak of the World War they were immediately put into service, as were other Anti-Aircraft Searchlight companies across Britain. Soon, other companies were deployed closer to the front in Europe.
1910s carbon arc search lamps were not always easy to operate. Most military searchlights by that time were built on the back of trucks, with combustion engine dynamos as a power supply. A typical WWI searchlight unit had three lights, five trucks, and 28 soldiers. Searchlight units became a natural home for other air defense technologies then under development, so that by the end of the war some units had acoustic reflectors (for detecting aircraft by sound) as well.
One of the lessons learned during the First World War was that searchlights were quite flexible. Portable searchlights could be used for air-defense, or could be positioned on a hill to light the battlefield (and even dazzle attackers). A technique called "artificial moonlight" took advantage of the enormous output of carbon arc lamps by pointing a searchlight up at clouds and letting the clouds reflect the light back to earth. This dim illumination could help an infantry unit move at night; commanders had the moon on demand.
On the other hand, searchlights were vulnerable. Experiments in mounting searchlights on airships to light the ground were of limited success, the lights made the position of the airship obvious and it would quickly be shot down. Similarly, as air-ground combat techniques evolved, searchlights became the first target for incoming enemy aircraft. Destroying the searchlights meant that anti-air guns were firing blind.
Even after the First World War established searchlights as a defensive technology—their sweeping beams portending destruction—the public didn't lose its affection for a bright display. In an apparent homage to the use of arc lamps for both stage plays and film projectors, Hollywood's Egyptian Theater had searchlights atop its facade from its opening day. Perhaps inspired by the 1895 installation of an ex-Chicago World's Fair searchlight in Pasadena, the Egyptian Theater's lights announced premieres by sweeping the sky—promotional searchlights learned from air defense, and ironically, the post-WWI fashion for major events was to aim lights upwards, rather than downwards at terrain as had predominated before the war.
Grauman extended the concept to his other theaters, with both permanent and portable lights often used at the Chinese Theater. In 1930, for the opening of Howard Hughes's "Hell's Angels," 200 searchlights lined Hollywood Boulevard. The scene could have easily been mistaken for a major air raid, but instead it became part of Hollywood culture. Three years later, 20th Century Pictures adopted its memorable emblem, surrounded by beams of light.
Hollywood's searchlights spread beyond just theaters. In 1931, Fisher Motors De Soto-Plymouth on Hollywood Boulevard opened under sweeping lights. Auto dealerships always wanted attention, and searchlights were the perfect combination of electrical modernity, show business spectacle, and mystery.
Synchro Searchlights
Searchlight technology advanced incrementally between the First and Second World Wars. Carbon-arc lamps remained the underlying technology, but features like automatic carbon regulators (that "fed" the carbon rods towards the arc as they burned away) and foot switches for easy control became standard. A common searchlight at the beginning of WWII might range from 8 to 24 kW, and by the end of the war the largest units reached over 100 kW.
The greater innovation of that war was the integration of searchlights into electromechanical fire control systems. New electrical technology, specifically a clever device called a "synchro" or "selsyn," revolutionized searchlight units. A synchro is a way of electrically transmitting a position. Two devices, similar to AC motors, are coupled by a three-phase circuit such that rotating one will rotate the other in proportion. The two "ends" of the synchro will always have the same relative position, so that by putting a synchro transmitter in one place and a synchro receiver in another, you can remote control the position of a device like a searchlight.
Synchros were immediately applied to "distant electric control," still a common term in marine searchlights. One of the main problems with the searchlight has always been its tendency to attract enemy fire. Early searchlights often had two operators sitting on chairs to the back or side, cranking wheels for azimuth and elevation. These two operators were often killed early in the engagement. By the use of distant electric control, a searchlight's operators could hide some distance from the searchlight or even operate it from a fortified position like a bunker. This turned out to be a superior way of using a searchlight anyway: operators directly beside later searchlights were practically blinded by the light reflected back by dust and vapor in the air. An operator some distance to the side had a much easier time seeing what was at the end of the beam.
Many US air defense searchlights offered a backup, as well: a control crank at the end of a metal arm, called an extended hand control. Were the electrical control system to fail, a searchlight operator could at least stand ten or so feet off to the side and be that much further from incoming fire. Practical use of searchlights for air defense by WWII required adaptation to the better ground-attack capability of aircraft. Searchlights might be stored in a concrete bunker and rolled out on tracks for use, by soldiers who would quickly retreat from the searchlight to a hidden control point. If the searchlight was struck, the operators at least survived, and a well-equipped unit would have a second light ready to go. The purpose of the concrete searchlight shelters was mostly to ensure that a "spare" searchlight would survive the shrapnel from an attack on the one in use.
The synchro could also integrate searchlights with other air defense equipment. Early in WWII, radar had not yet been fielded, and acoustic locators remained the state of the art. But they were now part of a unified system: two operators on a set of acoustic listening horns would spin cranks to adjust horizontal and vertical position until the sound of an airplane's engines was loudest. By synchros, this position was transferred to a searchlight control station. These stations resembled a large pair of binoculars on a sort of articulated mount. The base of the control station had readouts for the azimuth and elevation from the acoustic locator, and the operator started by turning wheels to aim the remote searchlight where the locator indicated an aircraft should be present.
Once the aircraft was caught in the beam, the searchlight operator threw a transfer switch that placed the searchlight under control of the synchros in the articulations of the binocular mount. From this point on, they tracked the aircraft through the binoculars, and the searchlight followed automatically. The azimuth and elevation from the binoculars could be sent by yet other synchros to readouts at an anti-air control station or even directly to a firing computer that aimed guns.
All of this required adjusting for the different locations of each of these components. A combination of old fashioned fire control techniques (placing each station along a common base line) and analog computers did that trick, already well-established technologies since similar computations were needed to aim guns in consideration of gravity and wind.
Speaking of a common baseline, it is also worthwhile to discuss the use of searchlights in coast defense during WWII. For a period of the war, the US feared a naval attack, particularly on the West Coast. Similar systems were widely deployed for firing artillery at attacking ships. A set of observation stations, placed along a common baseline, took azimuth readings to enemy ships. Those readings were reported by messenger, and later transmitted by synchros, to a plotting room where a mechanical and later analog computer determined the position of the ship. Yet other computers, at fire control points, determined how to aim the guns.
Because this whole system relied on baseline stations visually spotting ships through theodolites, it was much less effective at night. Searchlights were added to many installations, remote controlled by synchros from the baseline stations. Like the azimuth-indicating spotting scopes, two searchlights trained on a ship from a well-surveyed baseline gave enough information to compute a firing solution.
Advancing technology obsoleted the whimsical acoustic locating horns. Infrared heat detectors, looking much like searchlights but operating more or less in reverse, were the first electronic means of detecting aircraft in the night. They soon gave way to the first radar sets. The SCR-268, introduced in 1940, was the US Army's first fielded radar system. Its huge beam antenna had three operators on a bench hanging from the back, spinning cranks to adjust the antenna position until oscilloscopes showed the strongest possible return. The SCR-268 was a drop-in replacement for the acoustic locators in searchlight units, but it was far more effective. Skilled radar operators could place an aircraft accurately enough that it was centered in the searchlight beam before the light was turned on. Besides giving enemy pilots quite a surprise, this gave them little time to evade or return fire before anti-aircraft guns were zeroed in from the searchlight.
The British developed a more refined version of the SCR-268 called the Searchlight Control Radar. It is notable for its far more compact design, small enough that its antennas were mounted directly to the searchlight under control. The result is almost cyberpunk, a 1.5 meter light bristling with antennas, operators hanging from the sides. The SLC is said to have raised the chances of spotting an inbound aircraft with the searchlight from 1% to 90%.
I put a lot of time into writing this, and I hope that you enjoy reading it. If you can spare a few dollars, consider supporting me on ko-fi. You'll receive an occasional extra, subscribers-only post, and defray the costs of providing artisanal, hand-built world wide web directly from Albuquerque, New Mexico.
Consider a typical World War II searchlight, the GE Model 1942, introduced the year that its model number suggests. This 60" searchlight came on a trailer, with a second trailer up to 600 feet away for the 12 kW diesel power plant and the control station up to 300 feet away. What we might now call accessory interfaces allowed the searchlight to receive synchro signals from a locator and send them to a firing computer. These searchlights still required quite a bit of maintenance, and they provided features to ease that work, like a work light inside the searchlight drum for replacing the carbon rods.
The Second World War also brought about a renaissance in aerial searchlights. Mostly abandoned in the First World War because of their tendency to attract fire, searchlights returned to aircraft for a decidedly nautical use. Submarines, all diesel-electric during WWII, typically surfaced at night to run their engines and charge their batteries. The first submarine-hunting aircraft used compact carbon arc searchlights to pick submarines out of the dark. The system was remarkably effective, and prompted a change in strategy for diesel submarines, which had to rely on their own anti-aircraft weapons rather than mere stealth.
Radar complemented the searchlight, but only for a short time. Radar locators for searchlights were basically an interim measure, making the best use of early radar sets that were sensitive enough to detect aircraft but lacked the spatial resolution to locate them precisely. Radar engineers, especially in Britain, made rapid improvements. For anti-submarine warfare, for example, shortwave radar systems were far more sensitive. It wasn't long before radar could directly guide anti-aircraft weapons, as well.
Military searchlights had a role to play all the way through the Korean War, but a much reduced one. Anti-aircraft detection and firing is now firmly the domain of electronic warfare: radio waves, not light beams, search our skies.
Surplus Light
The declining relevance of searchlights to the military proved an enormous boon to their civilian use. Searchlights were tremendously expensive, so promotional use had been limited to major events like world's fairs. During the war, on the other hand, the Army had built thousands of them. Afterwards, through the War Surplus Administration, it parted with most of them at bargain rates.
Entrepreneurial spirits, many of them veterans of the war, purchased secondhand air defense searchlights and offered them for advertising. Just about any auto dealership could afford to hire a searchlight for a sale, and many did. In California, it became traditional for dealerships to bring in searchlights to announce the new model year.
There is a bit of moth in every human being, operators of giant searchlights have found. Whenever a searchlight is probing into the night sky, motorists are apt to go blocks, even miles out of their way to track down the source. [^2]
Growing up in Portland, I remember seeing strange lights in the sky and searching for their origin. The searchlights utterly fascinated me, even if the source inevitably turned out to be another car lot. In New Mexico, promotional searchlights are rare, although not unheard of. The predominant weather may be a factor, since cloud cover makes searchlights more obvious. But it's mostly a matter of geography: the largest concentration of military searchlights was on the West Coast, and many overseas units were returned to West Coast ports. Los Angeles became the capital of the searchlight because they were cheapest there.
There are still WWII military surplus searchlights in promotional use, but far fewer than there once were. Parts are hard to come by, and the operation of carbon arc lamps is a less familiar skill. Most of these searchlights are eighty years old, and built to designs that would have been familiar one hundred years ago. Because of radar's dominance, the military has little need for such powerful lights and no longer buys them in quantity. Promotional searchlights have once again detached themselves from air defense, developing as a separate industry.
Modern searchlights use Xenon lamps, similar to carbon arcs in their use of an electrical arc as the light source, but far more refined. The arc is confined in an environment of Xenon gas, which practically eliminates electrode wear. Companies like Luminator make several-kW Xenon searchlights that can be mounted to aircraft, the main remaining use of searchlights for tactical purposes. Promotional systems have become much more specialized: Sky Tracker makes a popular system that mounts four 2 kW or 4 kW Xenon searchlights in the bed of a pickup truck. The four lights are attached to a common cam that rotates them in a "clover leaf" pattern, quite nonsensical for the historic application of air defense. Nonetheless, that clover leaf or vortex pattern is probably what most people now think of when searchlights are mentioned.
Fixed searchlights, of the type that graced so many World's Fairs, are now rare. The most famous in the US, if not the world, is the Luxor Sky Beam in Las Vegas. Often said to be the brightest light in the world, the tip of the Luxor pyramid contains 39 7 kW Xenon searchlights for a total of over a quarter of a megawatt, all fixed straight upwards. Even that light may be in its twilight years: since 2008, it has operated at only half power.
Today, concerns of light pollution, impact on aviation, and a general sense that auto dealerships should not lay claim to the sky have led to a decline in searchlight advertising. We are perhaps fortunate that the Cloud Projector Company's plans for on-cloud advertising didn't pan out; our present advertising industry is dystopian enough. Still, I am but a moth, or at least I have the impulses of one. There's nothing quite like a searchlight.
- "The Daily Item," Lynn, Massachusetts, May 7 1919 p. 9. [^2]: "The Plain Dealer," Cleveland, Ohio, July 11 1961 p. 46↩

Mexico's Senate Approves ISP Rules To Disconnect Persistent Online Pirates
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-07
03:30:00 UTC
12:30:00 KST

Mexico's Senate Approves ISP Rules To Disconnect Persistent Online Pirates (torrentfreak.com) 17
			
		 	
				An anonymous reader quotes a report from TorrentFreak: On Tuesday, Mexico's Senate approved (PDF) a reform of the Federal Copyright Law that ties ISP liability to having a policy to terminate repeat infringers. [This policy has to be published permanently on their website and in their terms of service, and it must include a basic way to notify users. In other words, what can be terminated is the Internet connection itself, not just an account. According to Mexican business outlet EL CEO, this means that access providers such as Telmex and Izzi will need such a policy.] The Senate approved the amendments with roughly two-thirds of the vote, as the opposition voted against. The Chamber of Deputies already approved the same text in October, so the decree now goes to President Claudia Sheinbaum. Her government proposed the changes in May to bring the law in line with the USMCA trade agreement between Mexico, the United States and Canada.
[...] Providers that don't comply risk facing liability. Under the new rules, any ISP that fails to meet its safe harbor conditions becomes liable for its users' infringements if it knowingly contributes to, induces, or causes the infringement. The same goes for an ISP that can control the infringing user and profits directly from the infringement. In both cases, the Mexican Institute of Industrial Property (IMPI) can fine the provider up to roughly 4.7 million pesos (~$250,000), depending on the infringement. The law doesn't explicitly define the term repeat infringer, nor does it say how many piracy accusations it takes before a subscriber can be cut off. According to Aurora Lopez-Portillo, director of legal services at IP firm ClarkeModet, each provider has to decide its own threshold. She told EL CEO that ISPs should also check whether the accusations actually hold up, while giving users a chance to respond, even though the law doesn't require that.
During Tuesday's Senate debate, some lawmakers in the opposition raised similar concerns. Senator Gabriel Elizondo warned that families can share a single connection, so the pirating activities of one person could leave an entire household offline. Previously, opposition lawmakers proposed requiring a court order before Internet service could be suspended, but that proposal was not adopted. [...] There, failing to meet the safe harbor conditions, including the repeat infringer policy, is the first requirement for liability. If an ISP also contributes to infringement with knowledge of it, or can control the infringer and directly profits from it, it can be held responsible. The new repeat infringer rules will take effect 180 days after their publication in Mexico's official gazette, which hasn't happened yet as far as we can see. How Mexican ISPs will define a repeat infringer, and how many accusations it takes, has yet to be seen.

The Military Triumphal Arch
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-07
03:07:51 UTC
12:07:51 KST

An American Vision
Finally Realized
A Monument To The American Spirit
For 250 years, Americans have triumphed over war, division, and hardship. Against impossible odds, our people have built a land of liberty where the individual citizen can thrive, prosper, and pursue happiness greater than any other place in the world.
The United States Military Triumphal Arch celebrates America's first 250 years and stands as a symbol of its next 250. It serves as a solemn entrance to Arlington National Cemetery, honoring the heroes who gave their lives for our freedom, and as a grand gateway to the capital of the nation that has been, and will forever be, the greatest force for freedom and goodness in the history of the world.
An American Vision
Finally Realized
A century ago, America envisioned a monument to reconciliation and national unity following the Civil War.
In 1924, the Arlington Memorial Bridge Commission proposed two monumental columns in Memorial Circle, crowned by figures of Victory. One represented the North. The other represented the South. But the columns were never built.
Today, President Donald J. Trump fulfills and expands on this historic civic vision: what began as a celebration of the reconciliation between North and South finds new expression as a celebration of the triumph of the American spirit throughout the full sweep of American history.
The Military Triumphal Arch realizes a century-old ambition for beautiful monumental architecture in Memorial Circle. While such a grand monument was planned and dreamed of by all of America, it was a Dream that never got built because of the onset of the Great Depression of 1929 — But it will get built now.
The Military Triumphal Arch will be an enduring symbol of American Freedom, American Strength, and American Unity. We will fulfill the aspirations of the Great American Patriots who came before us, and whose plans and visions will finally be realized with the start of construction in our 250th year.
A Tribute To Our Heroes
From the Lincoln Memorial, across Arlington Memorial Bridge, to the hallowed grounds of Arlington National Cemetery, an extraordinary ceremonial axis connects the ideals of the American Republic engraved in stone —with the heroes who fought and died to defend them.
The Military Triumphal Arch honors all who have served our country. It will inspire future generations to build an America worthy of their sacrifice.
View on map
“This will be the greatest and most beautiful triumphal arch, anywhere in the world.”
Donald J. Trump
45th & 47th President of the United States
The Future Begins
With a Step
Great nations remember. Great nations also imagine the possibilities of the future.
The Military Triumphal Arch will honor those who founded, built, defended, and transformed this country, while inspiring new generations to carry forward the American promise.
For centuries to come, Americans will walk beneath this monument with our nation’s history behind them and its future before them, remembering the extraordinary nation they inherited and celebrating the freedom they will pass on.

The AI sell-off compared to well-known stock market crashes

6 Nov 2026
The AI sell-off compared to well-known stock market crashes
The recent AI sell-off brought a lot of fearmongering headlines. Put next to past stock market crashes, it's only a mild correction for the S&P 500, 6% below its record. Tech fell harder, with the Nasdaq down 11% and chip stocks down 36%, as much as they fell during Covid (35%). We'll be tracking the sell-off here with daily data updates.
The S&P 500 at the close on 6 Nov 2026
- Below record
- −6%
- From its record close
- Thursday 5 Nov
- −4.4%
- Worst day since 4 Apr 2025
- Trading days since record
- 23
- Since 6 Oct 2026
- Slides that went past 15%
- 16 of 56
- Slides since 1928 that reached 6%
How far the S&P 500 fell below a record close
Each line runs from a record high to the low that followed. Eight of the 16 falls past 15% since 1928 are shown.
Daily closes to 6 Nov 2026
Four in five S&P 500 falls of 6% never reached −20%
All 56 falls of 6% or more from a record since 1928, one dot each at its lowest point.
Mild for the S&P 500, a crash for chip stocks
How far each index is below its record now, against the lows of its own best-known past falls. US chip stocks (the PHLX Semiconductor index) are 36% down from their 22 Jun record, as deep as their Covid fall.
Daily closes to 6 Nov 2026
After dot-com, the S&P 500 took 7 years to recover, chip stocks 18
Years from each index's record close to its first close back at or above it. Prices only: with dividends reinvested, most recoveries were shorter. These are worst cases, for someone who bought at the record.
About the data
- The S&P 500 is the price index, without dividends, from daily closes on Yahoo Finance. Before March 1957 the series is its predecessor, the S&P 90.
- A fall runs from a record close to the next record, so the 2011 fall sits inside the 2007 to 2013 one. Its depth is the lowest close before the next record.
- "AI sell-off" is our name for the slide since the 6 October 2026 record.
- The S&P 500 is not the same index it was in 1929 or 1973: technology is now about a third of it, far more than then, so a tech-led sell-off moves it more than it would have.
- "Still below 2000 high" means the Nasdaq and chip stocks had not got back to their 2000 records by 2008, so 2008 is part of their dot-com fall, not a fall of its own.
- How we collect and check the data behind Company Tracker: About.

Crusoe sells troubled Wyoming data center project to Google
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-07
01:40:28 UTC
10:40:28 KST

Crusoe, an eight-year-old, Denver-based data center developer recently valued at $30.9 billion, confirmed Friday it has sold an unfinished data center project in southeastern Wyoming to Google.
Google, which Crusoe had tried and failed to land as a customer for the project, is taking over the development after reportedly raising concerns about its cost and construction timeline under Crusoe’s management.
Crusoe initially announced the project last year with Blackstone-backed infrastructure company Tallgrass, planning to eventually scale it to 10 gigawatts of power capacity.
Google did not disclose how much it paid Crusoe for the unfinished project, but it announced plans to invest $17 billion to develop the data center campus.
The deal highlights how difficult it can be for independent data center developers to deliver massive projects on time and within budget, given the challenges of securing equipment and power. Growing opposition from local communities can further complicate development.
Despite taking over the Wyoming facility, Google hasn’t walked away from working with Crusoe entirely. Crusoe is developing another data center for Google near Amarillo, Texas.
The deal to sell the site to Google was first reported by Bloomberg in June, but the Wyoming setback didn’t stop Crusoe from raising a $3.9 billion round in September.
Crusoe makes money by leasing data center space to customers that bring their own GPUs, by renting out its own GPUs, and by selling compute power used to run AI models, known as inference.
Crusoe’s largest projects serve some of the biggest names in AI. Its campus in Abilene, Texas, is being built in phases for Oracle and OpenAI, and the company is also constructing a separate 900-megawatt campus next door for Microsoft, with its first building expected to come online in mid-2027. Meta is also a customer, and Bloomberg reported that the trading firm Jane Street signed a $13 billion, five-year cloud deal with Crusoe.
Crusoe was founded by Chase Lochmiller, now its CEO, and Cully Cavness, who started it as a cryptocurrency-mining business powered by natural gas that would otherwise have been burned off. In September, alongside its $3.9 billion funding round, the company added three board members, including JB Straubel, the Redwood Materials founder and Tesla board member who invested in Crusoe in 2021. Crusoe later became the first customer of Straubel’s energy-storage business.
