I toured Unitree's factory. I'm more bullish on the West
China
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-07
09:05:16 CST
10:05:16 KST

· 13 min read
I Toured Unitree's Factory. I'm More Bullish on the West Than Ever.
Everyone thinks China's robot factories are automated. They aren't. The robots are Chinese; the robots that make robots will be Western.
On Wednesday I toured Unitree's factory in Hangzhou, and I wasn't allowed to take pictures. In the building that makes the actuators, the motors that drive every joint, there were one or two new automated lines. The rest of the floor was filled with humans operating old-school winding machines. (Winding means wrapping copper wire around a motor's core.) I don't know what percentage of the motors are made on the automated lines. But staffing the floor with humans made sense.
Unitree has a viral video (28 million views) of a humanoid doing assembly in its own factory, which it calls "an actual deployment". Its IPO prospectus calls it a pilot, and says assembly is "currently mainly manual". At the end of 2025 all of Unitree's machinery was worth about $1.7 million on its books, at a company that made almost 32,000 robots that year: some $50 of machinery per robot!
Unitree Embodied AI Model Manufactures Robots in Factory🤩 Based on Unitree’s UnifoLM-X1-0 embodied AI model, this is an actual deployment at Unitree’s own robot factory.
It doesn't need more. Labour was 8% of what it cost Unitree to make its robots in 2025, outsourced assemblers included, and most of the rest was parts. With those hands it made about 26,000 robot dogs in 2025, more than the roughly 20,000 IDC reckons the whole world shipped the year before.
So there's only so much a machine can save: halve a cost that's 8% of the bill and you save 4%. Unitree expects the motor line it's building to cut the labour in each motor by more than half, and the cost of one robot model by under 2%. Its labour per robot had roughly halved since 2023, and the filing credits scale, standardised processes and more skilled staff. None of those is a machine; two are kaizen, and I named my company, KAIKAKU, after the other word.
The other reason Unitree builds by hand starts in the showroom. Our host told us Unitree's CEO, Wang Xingxing, asked a G1 running GPT-6 to fetch him a bottle of water from another room. It did. Slowly. The same Wednesday I toured his factory, Wang told a conference in Wuzhen that Unitree had spent ten years on the robot's body and would now put its weight on the brain. Unitree's own slide puts robots' "ChatGPT moment", when they can do 80% of tasks they've never seen 80% of the time, two or three years out. That slide is from last year, so it's as soon as next year.
Until then, humanoids are mostly toys for researchers: our host named Nvidia, Amazon, Meta and universities. Three quarters of Unitree's humanoid revenue came from research and education in the first nine months of 2025, and under 5% from factories or other industrial work (half or more of what Unitree files as "industry" is robots giving guided tours at companies). When someone asked what the GD01, a rideable mecha from $650,000, is for, the answer was that Wang had wanted one since he was a child.
Unitree Unveils: GD01, A Manned Transformable Mecha, from $650,000 👏 The world's first production-ready manned mecha. It can transform. It's a civilian vehicle. It weighs ~500kg with you inside. Please everyone be sure to use the robot in a Friendly and Safe manner.
A product without a job changes every year, and a custom machine only works if the product is kind of frozen, so a machine built for this year's robot is scrap next year. It's like tailoring a suit for a teenager. Right now everything's ever-changing, except the motor. Unitree is automating its joint modules first because they're the parts its robots share most. When I toured a drone factory, the circuit boards and motors were made by machine and final assembly was done by hand. In a drone the motor is a whole component, but in a humanoid it's half of one, the copper-and-magnets half, and that half has stopped changing, like the AA battery in a toy that's redesigned every Christmas.
In May I said China's new factories "bolt robots into every one" and the West should "erase the factory floor". The first bit wasn't true of the plant I visited. For now Unitree is right. The second bit I still believe, and it's why I left Hangzhou more bullish on the West than ever. China's robots are made by cheap hands, so the machines that make robots are still up for grabs, and the West is good at building machines. AI makes it easier. A rented System 2 model can now do much of a robot programmer's job, so the West doesn't need China's army of engineers to automate the simpler work.
Ford's first line had no machine in it#
Ford is the best guide to what happens next, because he started exactly where Unitree is. His first moving assembly line, at Highland Park in 1913, put the magnets on Model T flywheels to make magnetos, small magnet-and-coil generators, kin to the motors on Unitree's winding floor. The work was split into 29 operations, each worker doing, in Ford's words, "as nearly as possible only one thing with only one movement".
The machines came once Ford froze the Model T, and by 1914 Highland Park was full of special machine tools. When the Model A replaced the T in 1927, Ford shut its plants for six months and rebuilt or scrapped most of its machine tools, and Chevrolet outsold it for the first time. A year later GM changed a Chevrolet model in three weeks. Unitree, with a new robot every year, is GM in 1927. In 2011 Foxconn's Terry Gou promised a million robots within three years; by late 2016 Foxconn had about 40,000, and said it was slowing down "because humans are cheaper and more flexible to adapt to an ever-shorter life cycle of consumer products".
Programming and integrating a robot is typically 50 to 70% of the cost of the application, and every redesign means paying for the programming again. When GM put robots into its Hamtramck plant in the 1980s, they reportedly attached Buick bumpers to Cadillacs because they couldn't tell the cars apart.
When hands get too pricey, a factory has three doors:
- Organise them better, as Ford and Foxconn did in their heyday and Unitree does now.
- Find cheaper hands elsewhere.
- Automate the work away, which has always needed a frozen part (AI is ending that).
America shuts the cheap doors#
Cross the Pacific and Unitree's sums flip. An hour of factory work costs about 7.5 times as much in America as in China, so at American wages labour would be about 40% of Unitree's costs.
Moving the work to China won't help, because Chinese hands are being shut out. In July the FCC stopped approving new humanoids and robot dogs unless they're made in America with more than 65% American parts by cost (75% from 2029). The Pentagon goes further for the drones its programme buys: from August 2027, their stators have to be wound outside China and a handful of other countries.
America has run this experiment before. In 1964 about half a million Mexican farmworkers were shut out. In 1965, 20% of California's tomatoes were picked by machine; by 1970 the figure was 99.9%. The tomato was bred to be tougher and squarer, so the machine could pick it.
How the West wins#
The motor line Unitree expects to save 1.7% of one robot model's cost would save about 9% at American wages. The machine is worth five or six times more in America than in Hangzhou, so the West can afford one nobody can switch off.
In the West, the volume is arriving:
- Figure claims its BotQ factory has gone from making one humanoid a day to one an hour in less than four months.
- Tesla says most of a line to build a million Optimus robots a year is at its Fremont plant.
- Agility, which makes its Digit humanoids at its own factory in Oregon, listed on Nasdaq on 5 November with Foxconn leading about $201 million of the money, though it sold just $1.8 million of robots last year.
- Westmag, a startup backed by Lux, Founders Fund and a16z, makes actuators for robots and motors for drones on a partly automated line.
- 1X says it makes its own motors, from a spool of copper to a finished actuator, on automated machines its engineers designed and built in Hayward, California.
Assembly is not vertical integration 1X bends the copper, solders the phases, potts the stator and builds the magnet arrays. Raw materials to finished actuators on our automated line. That is vertical integration. That is reindustrialization
Somebody has to build those lines, and we in the West are extremely good at building the machines that build things, even if China passed Germany as the top exporter of machine tools last year. America itself can't yet: on 28 October the White House found that it "relies on foreign imports for most types of robotics, industrial machinery, and their parts and components".
The country that's short of hands usually builds the machines. Labour-short Japan did it to America: GM bought the first Unimate industrial robot in 1961, Kawasaki licensed it in 1968, and by 1990 America's first robot makers had been sold to Europe. Now America is short of hands, and the machine builders are moving in: Mazak has built 40,000 machines in Kentucky, Yaskawa will make robots in Wisconsin, and Fanuc has bought a site in Michigan.
Then there's the programming. Paying for it is why a custom machine only made sense for a frozen product, and AI is cutting that bill. HD Hyundai and Palantir say AI agents cut the average time to fix a robot-programming issue from 21.5 days to 7.9. Apple estimated that it would have taken up to nine months to hire 8,700 industrial engineers in America, versus 15 days in China. China will have the same AI tools, but an engineer's time is three to five times more expensive in America, so each hour AI saves is worth that much more. The advantage China will lose quickest is its army of robot programmers.
Software went through all this first. A change used to take months of programmers' time, so teams froze the spec and shipped once a year. These days a coding agent makes the change before lunch, and products ship every week. In my last essay I called the robot version agentic robotics: an off-the-shelf System 2, a model like Claude, writes the robot's program, runs it and fixes it itself, on any task a sensor can check. Everything is a drawer except for laundry, and most of a motor line is drawers.
How we lose#
China makes the magnets, but MP Materials has finished qualifying its magnets with GM, and its Texas plant begins deliveries before the end of 2026. BMW makes an EV motor in Steyr that uses no permanent magnets, and wants capacity for more than 100,000 of them this year.
By 2022, all of the top ten manufacturers of solar panel-making equipment were Chinese. China also makes many of the machines that build electric-car motors. Within a few years Chinese builders of hairpin winding lines caught up with German and Italian ones, with changeovers done in software, undercut them by as much as half, and have started exporting whole lines to Europe. The winder is a small part of a motor's cost, and American wages are high enough that even a winder at twice the price still saves 8 to 9% of the cost of a robot. The West can't afford one that's slow to change over.
Terrible, short-sighted move. We are shooting ourselves in the foot here. I am happy to spend time with policymakers to convince them of the idiocy of banning Chinese hardware in 2026, and how the US might regain a competitive edge in hardware. USA is not in the position of Show more
Yesterday, Congress took up the GUARD Act, bipartisan legislation to keep adversary-controlled robots out of American infrastructure. Our CEO Peggy Johnson wrote about why this hearing matters, in a new op-ed with @PirateWires. 🧵
Eric Jang thinks banning Chinese hardware is idiocy and wants an American Unitree instead. Agreed! But build it with machines. (Disclosure: I invest in Standard Manufacturing, which builds autonomous assembly lines, and in Dirac, Isembard, RMFG and Minerva, a US humanoid maker that gains from the FCC rule.)
Where the money is#
There are 25 to 30 actuators for the main joints (not counting the hands) in a humanoid robot, according to Schaeffler. Tesla alone is looking to produce a million Optimus units a year. Call it 25 million motors.
- Make motors, not humanoids, which are still sold mostly to research labs. Robots, drones and e-bikes will always need motors, and motors barely change from one generation to the next.
- Standardise like Mabuchi. It offered customers a 20% discount in 1971 to take its standard motors, and today it makes about 1.4 billion a year from about 90 basic designs.
- Build a stator line that can be quickly adapted to new sizes, because motor sizes still change.
- Don't build robots by hand in Ohio.
Hands win in China. Machines win in the West.
What I saw at BYD#
The next day I went to BYD, to a final-assembly plant two and a half hours east of Shenzhen. When BYD started making batteries in 1995, its founder, Wang Chuanfu, did Unitree's arithmetic, as CCTV tells it: an imported machine cost $200,000, written off at RMB 20,000 a month, enough to hire a dozen workers. "A person plus a jig equals a robot," he told CCTV. Thirty years later, everything I saw was automated: robots welding the bodies, and finished cars rolling off the line and onto BYD's own ship.
Nothing exotic. The robots I saw were Fanuc and KUKA arms on pre-programmed paths, similar to what Tesla uses, with grippers bolted together out of aluminium extrusions full of holes. Anyone can buy them, and nothing I saw is beyond an American plant, except the people. BYD employs almost as many people in R&D alone as Tesla has in total.
So what are all those engineers doing in a factory run by robots, and could an agent do their job? That's part 2.

I toured Unitree's factory. I'm more bullish on the West
China
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-07
09:05:16 CST
10:05:16 KST

· 13 min read
I Toured Unitree's Factory. I'm More Bullish on the West Than Ever.
Everyone thinks China's robot factories are automated. They aren't. The robots are Chinese; the robots that make robots will be Western.
On Wednesday I toured Unitree's factory in Hangzhou, and I wasn't allowed to take pictures. In the building that makes the actuators, the motors that drive every joint, there were one or two new automated lines. The rest of the floor was filled with humans operating old-school winding machines. (Winding means wrapping copper wire around a motor's core.) I don't know what percentage of the motors are made on the automated lines. But staffing the floor with humans made sense.
Unitree has a viral video (28 million views) of a humanoid doing assembly in its own factory, which it calls "an actual deployment". Its IPO prospectus calls it a pilot, and says assembly is "currently mainly manual". At the end of 2025 all of Unitree's machinery was worth about $1.7 million on its books, at a company that made almost 32,000 robots that year: some $50 of machinery per robot!
Unitree Embodied AI Model Manufactures Robots in Factory🤩 Based on Unitree’s UnifoLM-X1-0 embodied AI model, this is an actual deployment at Unitree’s own robot factory.
It doesn't need more. Labour was 8% of what it cost Unitree to make its robots in 2025, outsourced assemblers included, and most of the rest was parts. With those hands it made about 26,000 robot dogs in 2025, more than the roughly 20,000 IDC reckons the whole world shipped the year before.
So there's only so much a machine can save: halve a cost that's 8% of the bill and you save 4%. Unitree expects the motor line it's building to cut the labour in each motor by more than half, and the cost of one robot model by under 2%. Its labour per robot had roughly halved since 2023, and the filing credits scale, standardised processes and more skilled staff. None of those is a machine; two are kaizen, and I named my company, KAIKAKU, after the other word.
The other reason Unitree builds by hand starts in the showroom. Our host told us Unitree's CEO, Wang Xingxing, asked a G1 running GPT-6 to fetch him a bottle of water from another room. It did. Slowly. The same Wednesday I toured his factory, Wang told a conference in Wuzhen that Unitree had spent ten years on the robot's body and would now put its weight on the brain. Unitree's own slide puts robots' "ChatGPT moment", when they can do 80% of tasks they've never seen 80% of the time, two or three years out. That slide is from last year, so it's as soon as next year.
Until then, humanoids are mostly toys for researchers: our host named Nvidia, Amazon, Meta and universities. Three quarters of Unitree's humanoid revenue came from research and education in the first nine months of 2025, and under 5% from factories or other industrial work (half or more of what Unitree files as "industry" is robots giving guided tours at companies). When someone asked what the GD01, a rideable mecha from $650,000, is for, the answer was that Wang had wanted one since he was a child.
Unitree Unveils: GD01, A Manned Transformable Mecha, from $650,000 👏 The world's first production-ready manned mecha. It can transform. It's a civilian vehicle. It weighs ~500kg with you inside. Please everyone be sure to use the robot in a Friendly and Safe manner.
A product without a job changes every year, and a custom machine only works if the product is kind of frozen, so a machine built for this year's robot is scrap next year. It's like tailoring a suit for a teenager. Right now everything's ever-changing, except the motor. Unitree is automating its joint modules first because they're the parts its robots share most. When I toured a drone factory, the circuit boards and motors were made by machine and final assembly was done by hand. In a drone the motor is a whole component, but in a humanoid it's half of one, the copper-and-magnets half, and that half has stopped changing, like the AA battery in a toy that's redesigned every Christmas.
In May I said China's new factories "bolt robots into every one" and the West should "erase the factory floor". The first bit wasn't true of the plant I visited. For now Unitree is right. The second bit I still believe, and it's why I left Hangzhou more bullish on the West than ever. China's robots are made by cheap hands, so the machines that make robots are still up for grabs, and the West is good at building machines. AI makes it easier. A rented System 2 model can now do much of a robot programmer's job, so the West doesn't need China's army of engineers to automate the simpler work.
Ford's first line had no machine in it#
Ford is the best guide to what happens next, because he started exactly where Unitree is. His first moving assembly line, at Highland Park in 1913, put the magnets on Model T flywheels to make magnetos, small magnet-and-coil generators, kin to the motors on Unitree's winding floor. The work was split into 29 operations, each worker doing, in Ford's words, "as nearly as possible only one thing with only one movement".
The machines came once Ford froze the Model T, and by 1914 Highland Park was full of special machine tools. When the Model A replaced the T in 1927, Ford shut its plants for six months and rebuilt or scrapped most of its machine tools, and Chevrolet outsold it for the first time. A year later GM changed a Chevrolet model in three weeks. Unitree, with a new robot every year, is GM in 1927. In 2011 Foxconn's Terry Gou promised a million robots within three years; by late 2016 Foxconn had about 40,000, and said it was slowing down "because humans are cheaper and more flexible to adapt to an ever-shorter life cycle of consumer products".
Programming and integrating a robot is typically 50 to 70% of the cost of the application, and every redesign means paying for the programming again. When GM put robots into its Hamtramck plant in the 1980s, they reportedly attached Buick bumpers to Cadillacs because they couldn't tell the cars apart.
When hands get too pricey, a factory has three doors:
- Organise them better, as Ford and Foxconn did in their heyday and Unitree does now.
- Find cheaper hands elsewhere.
- Automate the work away, which has always needed a frozen part (AI is ending that).
America shuts the cheap doors#
Cross the Pacific and Unitree's sums flip. An hour of factory work costs about 7.5 times as much in America as in China, so at American wages labour would be about 40% of Unitree's costs.
Moving the work to China won't help, because Chinese hands are being shut out. In July the FCC stopped approving new humanoids and robot dogs unless they're made in America with more than 65% American parts by cost (75% from 2029). The Pentagon goes further for the drones its programme buys: from August 2027, their stators have to be wound outside China and a handful of other countries.
America has run this experiment before. In 1964 about half a million Mexican farmworkers were shut out. In 1965, 20% of California's tomatoes were picked by machine; by 1970 the figure was 99.9%. The tomato was bred to be tougher and squarer, so the machine could pick it.
How the West wins#
The motor line Unitree expects to save 1.7% of one robot model's cost would save about 9% at American wages. The machine is worth five or six times more in America than in Hangzhou, so the West can afford one nobody can switch off.
In the West, the volume is arriving:
- Figure claims its BotQ factory has gone from making one humanoid a day to one an hour in less than four months.
- Tesla says most of a line to build a million Optimus robots a year is at its Fremont plant.
- Agility, which makes its Digit humanoids at its own factory in Oregon, listed on Nasdaq on 5 November with Foxconn leading about $201 million of the money, though it sold just $1.8 million of robots last year.
- Westmag, a startup backed by Lux, Founders Fund and a16z, makes actuators for robots and motors for drones on a partly automated line.
- 1X says it makes its own motors, from a spool of copper to a finished actuator, on automated machines its engineers designed and built in Hayward, California.
Assembly is not vertical integration 1X bends the copper, solders the phases, potts the stator and builds the magnet arrays. Raw materials to finished actuators on our automated line. That is vertical integration. That is reindustrialization
Somebody has to build those lines, and we in the West are extremely good at building the machines that build things, even if China passed Germany as the top exporter of machine tools last year. America itself can't yet: on 28 October the White House found that it "relies on foreign imports for most types of robotics, industrial machinery, and their parts and components".
The country that's short of hands usually builds the machines. Labour-short Japan did it to America: GM bought the first Unimate industrial robot in 1961, Kawasaki licensed it in 1968, and by 1990 America's first robot makers had been sold to Europe. Now America is short of hands, and the machine builders are moving in: Mazak has built 40,000 machines in Kentucky, Yaskawa will make robots in Wisconsin, and Fanuc has bought a site in Michigan.
Then there's the programming. Paying for it is why a custom machine only made sense for a frozen product, and AI is cutting that bill. HD Hyundai and Palantir say AI agents cut the average time to fix a robot-programming issue from 21.5 days to 7.9. Apple estimated that it would have taken up to nine months to hire 8,700 industrial engineers in America, versus 15 days in China. China will have the same AI tools, but an engineer's time is three to five times more expensive in America, so each hour AI saves is worth that much more. The advantage China will lose quickest is its army of robot programmers.
Software went through all this first. A change used to take months of programmers' time, so teams froze the spec and shipped once a year. These days a coding agent makes the change before lunch, and products ship every week. In my last essay I called the robot version agentic robotics: an off-the-shelf System 2, a model like Claude, writes the robot's program, runs it and fixes it itself, on any task a sensor can check. Everything is a drawer except for laundry, and most of a motor line is drawers.

The winder is a small part of a motor's cost, and American wages are high enough that even a winder at twice the price still saves 8 to 9% of the cost of a robot. The West can't afford one that's slow to change over.

The UV-tracking Gem made me rethink how sunlight affects my health, not just my appearance
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-07
00:30:23 UTC
09:30:23 KST

With temperatures rising to the 90s in Los Angeles, avoiding the heat wasn’t exactly an option while I was at a relative’s outdoor party in August. So I sat beneath a patio umbrella with a powerful fan nearby, figuring I was protected from the sun and heat. Half an hour later, I felt exhausted, dizzy, nauseous, and generally unwell, symptoms consistent with heat exhaustion.
The UV-tracking Gem made me rethink how sunlight affects my health, not just my appearance
What started as a test to understand how sun exposure was aging my skin took a turn that I wasn’t expecting.
Around the same time, I received a notification from an app used by the $249 ($149 at the time of publication) UV-tracking necklace I’d been wearing, called Gem, from a company called The90. It warned that my UV exposure had climbed considerably. It turns out UV radiation wasn’t repelled by this patio umbrella, and it’s hard to ever be fully protected because radiation can be reflected or scattered by objects in the environment. While UV exposure and overheating are different things, I’d mistakenly assumed the shade and the fan would adequately protect me from both.
This led to a discovery: One of my medications increases my sensitivity to heat, which helped to explain similar episodes I’d experienced over the years. The Gem didn’t diagnose anything, but it prompted me to investigate something I might otherwise have dismissed. Since then, I’ve been more mindful about limiting time outdoors during hours of peak heat and UV, staying hydrated, and paying closer attention to how I’m feeling outside.
I’ll admit my initial interest in tracking UV was largely vanity-driven. As much as I try to challenge harmful beauty standards, I’m hardly immune to them. Having started going gray in my 20s, I’m keen on slowing down other visible signs of aging where I reasonably can. But between being Indian (I rarely get sunburned thanks in part to my skin’s higher melanin content, not that it eliminates the risk of sun damage or skin cancer) and living within walking distance of the beach in perpetually sunny LA, I hadn’t given much thought to how much UV exposure I was actually getting. The Gem has helped me better understand it by providing a range of estimates, some more useful than others.
The included UV-detecting sensor measures UVA and UVB radiation separately, rather than simply displaying my area’s UV forecast. UVB is primarily responsible for sunburn and helps your skin produce vitamin D, while UVA contributes to premature aging, even when you aren’t visibly burning. Thankfully, getting these metrics doesn’t come at the cost of wearing a bulky device. The circular pendant comes with a cute, thin necklace I’d happily wear even if it didn’t track anything. Its titanium, gold- or silver-colored chain pairs well with a variety of outfits. Battery life lasted almost exactly a week (the advertised lifespan), and the Gem recharges using an included charging dock. The90 plans to release a hat clip and bag tag early next year, which seem ideal for those who prefer not to wear it around their neck.
The mobile app is central to the experience. It has easy-to-read graphs and jargon-light explanations. I enjoyed using it. While not required, you can create a skin profile so that it can estimate a range of metrics based on the sensor’s UVA and UVB readings. The profile is based upon factors like skin type, sensitivities, clothing, and sunscreen. That means its estimates aren’t based on UV exposure alone, though they’re still predictions rather than direct measurements of how your body is responding to sunlight.
There’s a lot here, including your estimated vitamin D production, potential circadian light benefits, estimated nitric oxide-related benefits, cumulative UVA exposure, and recent accumulated UVA exposure. Alongside cumulative UVA exposure and weekly trends, its Skin Aging Zone assesses potential aging-related risk and offers personalized skincare advice. It also provides a personalized sunburn countdown, sunscreen reminders, and a daily Sun Score.
The Sun Score is a rating that incorporates various metrics to balance sunlight’s potential benefits against the risks of overexposure. The company encourages users to aim for a daily Sun Score of 90, though more sun isn’t necessarily better. Too little exposure means missing potential benefits, while excessive UV can damage your skin. I appreciated the context, but the individual estimated measurements proved more revealing than the Sun Score.
The UV readings in the app responded quickly as I moved between sunlight, shade, and indoors, even registering changes near windows. I couldn’t independently verify the sensor’s accuracy, but the readings behaved as expected as I shifted between environments. However, there were times when I noticed the Gem sometimes failed to register that I had been outside. Eventually, I discovered the app needs to remain running in the background (at least on iPhone; I haven’t tested on Android), as measurements aren’t stored on the sensor, and fully closing the app interrupts tracking.
My experience at the outdoor party challenged my assumptions about how much protection I was getting from the shade. Just as surprising to me was learning how little UVB I accumulated during a typical week, despite living somewhere so sunny. I fell short of the app’s estimated sunlight-derived vitamin D target almost daily, prompting me to research how melanin, while offering some natural UV protection, impacts vitamin D production from sunlight. Until then, I’d mostly thought about sun exposure in terms of protecting my skin from damage. Seeing how little UVB I was accumulating made me consider what I might be missing out on, too.
The vitamin D countdown wasn’t as helpful as I had hoped. During one test, its estimate of how much additional sunlight I needed dropped from several hours to under an hour, then climbed past six hours just minutes later. The fluctuations appeared related to changing UVB intensity, though I couldn’t verify that. I noticed similar fluctuations in the app’s estimated time to sunburn. With both countdowns changing so dramatically, I stopped paying much attention to the exact times and focused instead on the broader picture: whether it felt like I was getting too much or too little sun.
That was perhaps the biggest surprise of testing the Gem. After my experience using it at the outdoor party in August, I’d expected it just would encourage me to spend less time in the sun each day. In reality, my exposure often falls at one of two extremes: either too little to meet the app’s estimated targets or enough to raise concerns about overexposure, even when I thought I was protected by the shade.
I now try to be more mindful about my UV exposure, even when I’m not using the Gem. I try to limit time spent outdoors during peak UV hours, and I try to get some sunlight on days spent mostly indoors. I credit the Gem with helping me better understand how my time outside could affect my well-being in ways I’d never considered, though the usefulness of your findings may vary.
At $249 outside of a deal, it’s expensive, though it’s nice that it doesn’t have a subscription. I personally can’t justify the price given how much time I spend indoors, but I see the appeal for people who spend considerable time outside, or those who want to be more mindful of sun protection. For the price, I wish the app’s guidance was clearer at times, and I wish it could track UV without the app being open in the background, but the Gem ultimately got me thinking less about how my body looks and more about how I’m treating it.
Photography by Sheena Vasani / The Verge

EU Says TikTok's 'Rabbit Hole' Effect Must Stop
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-07
00:00:00 UTC
09:00:00 KST

EU Says TikTok's 'Rabbit Hole' Effect Must Stop (theverge.com) 44
			
		 	
				The European Commission has preliminarily found that TikTok violates the Digital Services Act because its For You recommendation algorithm can create a "rabbit hole effect," repeatedly exposing users to potentially harmful content involving topics such as sadness, isolation and body image. TikTok must change the feed's design or potentially face fines of up to 6% of its global annual revenue. The Verge reports:  The findings come months after the EU Commission declared that TikTok's design is too "addictive." While TikTok has implemented controls to mitigate the algorithm's "rabbit hole effect," the Commission says its preliminary findings suggest that they're not effective. TikTok must now change the design of its For You feed, or else face fines of up to 6 percent of its global annual turnover. The platform is also facing pressure in the US, with lawmakers and a wave of lawsuits raising concerns about child safety.  "Every time a user scrolls or likes a video on TikTok, the algorithm serves more of the same leading users down rabbit holes they cannot easily escape from," Henna Virkkunen, the EU Commission's executive vice president of tech sovereignty, security, and democracy, says in the press release. "Today's preliminary findings show again that TikTok needs to step up its efforts to protect users' well-being, including those of minors."

OpenAI valued at $1.25 trillion, as rival Anthropic nears its IPO
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-06
23:46:18 UTC
08:46:18 KST

OpenAI is now valued at $1.25 trillion in a new round of funding that has raised another $11 billion, according to Bloomberg. That would make it one of the world’s most valuable companies, not long after raising $122 billion from investors in March at a valuation of $852 billion.
OpenAI valued at $1.25 trillion, as rival Anthropic nears its IPO
CEO Sam Altman said recently that OpenAI is prioritizing safety instead of going ‘guns blazing’ toward an IPO.
Echoing FT and Wall Street Journal reports from last week, Bloomberg says OpenAI is still pursuing additional investments that could increase the amount to $20 billion, and that it had initially hoped to raise as much as $30 billion at a higher valuation of $1.4 trillion. That was before tech stocks dropped, and the results of the midterms showed how much public opinion has turned against AI companies and data center projects.
OpenAI’s rival Anthropic filed its S-1 to go public last week. Earlier this year, OpenAI CEO Sam Altman said it’s holding off on its own IPO until it can make more confident claims about its models’ safety. He didn’t give a specific timeline for the IPO, but said at the time, “I think it’s also kind of bad for the world if OpenAI waits too long to go public.”

====================================================================================================

RealPage rent-pricing case wraps with nearly $360M to renters
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-06
23:37:19 UTC
08:37:19 KST

A major federal antitrust lawsuit over algorithmic rent-setting via Realpage’s software has been settled, per filings in RealPage Inc. Rental Software Antitrust Litigation in the U.S. District Court for the Middle District of Tennessee, Nashville Division.
The court approved the settlements, “including the Settlement Amounts, the releases, and the dismissal with prejudice of the Action as to the Settling Defendants,” and found that the settlements are fair and adequate to the settling class, according to the Oct. 15 filing.
In late 2022 and early 2023, a slew of class-action lawsuits were filed against Realpage and about 50 of the largest apartment owners and operators, alleging that the Richardson, Texas-based software provider enabled landlords to collude to raise rent prices beyond free-market levels in violation of antitrust law. Those cases were centralized into one in April 2023.
Settlement agreements came in waves, with 11 defendant landlords reaching 14 class settlements in May for $218 million and 27 landlords with 26 settlements worth more than $141.8 million in October 2025. The plaintiffs asked for class certification on Oct. 2.
The settlement class includes U.S. multifamily renters who paid any owner or operator on a property that used Realpage’s Revenue Management Solutions, Lease Rent Options, YieldStar and/or AI Revenue Management at any time between Oct. 18, 2018 through Nov. 21, 2025, per the filing.
None of the companies admitted fault or liability. They did, however, agree to stop providing Realpage with their nonpublic data for use as an input in Realpage’s revenue management system’s price recommendations, and to stop using Realpage’s RMS in ways that use competitors’ non-public data to inform pricing recommendations.
Whether the settlement agreements “really will work to limit the ability of these firms to coordinate with each other, to communicate with each other, that's the thing that I can't tell reading the decree,” Peter Carstensen, antitrust expert and professor of law emeritus at the University of Wisconsin-Madison, told Multifamily Dive.
The settlements offer guidance to the companies providing rent-pricing models, according to Carstensen.
Looking ahead, “the question for any particular industry is: How much are they giving up by restricting what they do? Are there moves they can make consistent with the decree that would allow them to establish a little more coordination in pricing?” Carstensen said.
States tried to stop settlements
Not everyone is happy with the settlements.
After the defendant landlords asked for final approval of their settlements on Sept. 25, attorneys general from the District of Columbia, Kentucky, Maryland, New Jersey and Washington fought the agreements, saying that they could “unlawfully restrict” their own separate lawsuits. The AGs had raised such concerns after the first group of settlement agreements last November.
However, Judge Waverly Crenshaw again denied their request to reject the settlements, saying in an Oct. 9 filing that “the proposed releases do not injure the State AGs’ independent sovereign or quasi-sovereign interests,” but added that he would take their concerns into consideration.
Although the DOJ and state AGs with antitrust concerns typically work together, there are a growing number of fights between states and the federal government over settlements right now, according to Carstensen.
“What is different here in the second Trump administration is that the Justice Department and Trade Commission are bringing cases and settling them, and the states have brought the same case but are not settling on the same terms,” Carstensen said. “You're looking at the states not having confidence in the Justice Department or the Trade Commission to do the right thing.”
Per the final settlement, all claims against the defendants are permanently closed, except where expressly outlined in the agreement, according to the Oct. 15 filing.
“This dismissal does not affect, release, or impair any claim against any Defendant or alleged co-conspirator that is not a Settling Defendant,” Waverly said in the filing. Furthermore, the court’s certification of the settlement class “is for settlement purposes only and is without prejudice to, or waiver of, any defense, argument, or position of any Party with respect to any other class in the Action or any other action.”
In November 2025, the U.S. Justice Department settled with Realpage in a related 2024 lawsuit that claimed the software provider enabled landlords to collude to raise rent prices. Several similar state and local lawsuits are ongoing.
“The federal settlement is what it is, but it's not the final word necessarily,” Carstensen said.

RealPage rent-pricing case wraps with nearly $360M to renters
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-06
23:37:19 UTC
08:37:19 KST

A major federal antitrust lawsuit over algorithmic rent-setting via Realpage’s software has been settled, per filings in RealPage Inc. Rental Software Antitrust Litigation in the U.S. District Court for the Middle District of Tennessee, Nashville Division.
The court approved the settlements, “including the Settlement Amounts, the releases, and the dismissal with prejudice of the Action as to the Settling Defendants,” and found that the settlements are fair and adequate to the settling class, according to the Oct. 15 filing.
In late 2022 and early 2023, a slew of class-action lawsuits were filed against Realpage and about 50 of the largest apartment owners and operators, alleging that the Richardson, Texas-based software provider enabled landlords to collude to raise rent prices beyond free-market levels in violation of antitrust law. Those cases were centralized into one in April 2023.
Settlement agreements came in waves, with 11 defendant landlords reaching 14 class settlements in May for $218 million and 27 landlords with 26 settlements worth more than $141.8 million in October 2025. The plaintiffs asked for class certification on Oct. 2.
The settlement class includes U.S. multifamily renters who paid any owner or operator on a property that used Realpage’s Revenue Management Solutions, Lease Rent Options, YieldStar and/or AI Revenue Management at any time between Oct. 18, 2018 through Nov. 21, 2025, per the filing.
None of the companies admitted fault or liability. They did, however, agree to stop providing Realpage with their nonpublic data for use as an input in Realpage’s revenue management system’s price recommendations, and to stop using Realpage’s RMS in ways that use competitors’ non-public data to inform pricing recommendations.
Whether the settlement agreements “really will work to limit the ability of these firms to coordinate with each other, to communicate with each other, that's the thing that I can't tell reading the decree,” Peter Carstensen, antitrust expert and professor of law emeritus at the University of Wisconsin-Madison, told Multifamily Dive.
The settlements offer guidance to the companies providing rent-pricing models, according to Carstensen.
Looking ahead, “the question for any particular industry is: How much are they giving up by restricting what they do? Are there moves they can make consistent with the decree that would allow them to establish a little more coordination in pricing?” Carstensen said.
States tried to stop settlements
Not everyone is happy with the settlements.
After the defendant landlords asked for final approval of their settlements on Sept. 25, attorneys general from the District of Columbia, Kentucky, Maryland, New Jersey and Washington fought the agreements, saying that they could “unlawfully restrict” their own separate lawsuits. The AGs had raised such concerns after the first group of settlement agreements last November.
However, Judge Waverly Crenshaw again denied their request to reject the settlements, saying in an Oct. 9 filing that “the proposed releases do not injure the State AGs’ independent sovereign or quasi-sovereign interests,” but added that he would take their concerns into consideration.
Although the DOJ and state AGs with antitrust concerns typically work together, there are a growing number of fights between states and the federal government over settlements right now, according to Carstensen.
“What is different here in the second Trump administration is that the Justice Department and Trade Commission are bringing cases and settling them, and the states have brought the same case but are not settling on the same terms,” Carstensen said. “You're looking at the states not having confidence in the Justice Department or the Trade Commission to do the right thing.”
Per the final settlement, all claims against the defendants are permanently closed, except where expressly outlined in the agreement, according to the Oct. 15 filing.
“This dismissal does not affect, release, or impair any claim against any Defendant or alleged co-conspirator that is not a Settling Defendant,” Waverly said in the filing. Furthermore, the court’s certification of the settlement class “is for settlement purposes only and is without prejudice to, or waiver of, any defense, argument, or position of any Party with respect to any other class in the Action or any other action.”
In November 2025, the U.S. Justice Department settled with Realpage in a related 2024 lawsuit that claimed the software provider enabled landlords to collude to raise rent prices. Several similar state and local lawsuits are ongoing.
“The federal settlement is what it is, but it's not the final word necessarily,” Carstensen said.

Libghostty 1.0
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-06
23:30:52 UTC
08:30:52 KST

Mitchell Hashimoto
Libghostty 1.0
libghostty-vt 1.0 is here. libghostty is a
portable, high-performance C library for terminal emulation, extracted
directly from the core of Ghostty. The 1.0 release means both the library
itself and the C ABI are now stable.
A little over a year ago, I wrote that libghostty is coming. At the time, the Zig module had just been merged, the C API didn't exist, and nobody but Ghostty was a consumer. Today, the C API is a complete terminal emulator, it runs in production for millions of people across hundreds of products, both free and commercial, and it has a real, enforced ABI stability policy.
This post is about how we got here and what 1.0 actually means.1
Who Is Already Using It
Since starting the Ghostty project in 2022, the most common question I've been asked is "why?". I already answered this in the libghostty is coming post, so feel free to read that if you wish. Today, I'd rather answer this by showing the broad industry impact libghostty has already had. I believe this strongly validates its existence.
A year ago, I used a bug in JediTerm, the terminal emulator in JetBrains IDEs, as an example of why terminal emulation is hard to get right. A year later, the terminal in JetBrains IDEs now runs on libghostty. Every IntelliJ, PyCharm, GoLand, etc. user now has a libghostty-powered terminal. Based on a report by JetBrains, that's over 12.5 million active users.
Neovim has an approved pull request
replacing libvterm with libghostty-vt. Among other things, it closes a
10-year-old issue
about reflowing scrollback on resize. The maintainers are waiting on a
tagged release before merging so that distributions can package the
dependency separately, and the release today should enable that.
KDE developers are experimenting with a Konsole replacement built on it. Vercel's Turborepo uses it to render task output in its TUI. devenv uses it to drive shells. Antithesis used it to fuzz TUI programs and found real bugs in btop and others. Ghostel brings it to Emacs. ghostty-web is the WASM build with an Xterm.js-compatible API. Commercial products like OrbStack embed it. And a whole wave of new terminals and "agentic" coding tools, such as herdr and cmux, have been built on it rather than writing yet another emulator.
There are community bindings for Rust, Go, JavaScript, Swift, Python, .NET, Elixir, Dart, Java, and more, and the awesome-libghostty list tracks well over a hundred projects.
More people today use a terminal powered by libghostty than use Ghostty the application, by a very large multiple. And libghostty is having a much larger impact than being "just" a terminal emulator.2
ABI Stability
1.0 means libghostty-vt follows semantic versioning and has strict
ABI compatibility
promises so that packagers across distributions and operating systems
can be confident packaging libghostty-vt and program developers can be
confident dynamically linking against it.
Beyond providing useful features, I believe the hallmark of a high-quality and enduring C library is ABI stability. So from the start, I took inspiration from C libraries I enjoyed working with and designed the API to have a strong, stable ABI that allows for future additions.
Every object is an opaque handle. The hundreds of values you might read or
write go through a small set of enum-keyed getters and setters, so we
can add new values easily without introducing a new symbol. Programs with
newer headers linked against an older libghostty get GHOSTTY_UNSUPPORTED
results instead of crashes or undefined behavior. The few non-opaque structs
we have carry their size so that fields can be appended later, or contain
reserved padding. Enums are pinned to int on every compiler, as much as
the C standard allows.
Our CI enforces our ABI policy. Every change is checked with libabigail against a committed baseline on Linux, and against golden type manifests on macOS and WebAssembly.
On ELF platforms, every exported symbol is also versioned, so the dynamic loader refuses to load a mismatched library by name rather than failing in some mysterious way later.
Packaging
In addition to ABI stability, the 1.0 release brings along the proper
build tooling, tarballs, dependency licensing manifests, documentation,
and more to enable every major operating system and distribution to
package libghostty-vt through standard official tooling.
Libghostty has its own release cycle, separate from Ghostty the application. And it has its own tarballs with only the files and dependencies libghostty needs, resulting in smaller sizes and simpler licensing verification and compatibility.
Each release publishes a signed source tarball along with a companion
dependencies tarball containing every Zig package in Zig's own cache
layout, so a distribution can build the library completely offline. The
build installs the shared and static libraries with a proper SONAME,
headers, pkg-config files, and a CMake package config, and it honors
--prefix, DESTDIR, and lib64. Debug info is kept by default so
distros can split it out. All of this is documented in
PACKAGING-LIBGHOSTTY.md,
which ships with the tarball.
Dependencies and Licensing
libghostty-vt is MIT licensed. At runtime it needs nothing beyond
libc, and it can be built fully freestanding if you don't want that
either.3
The library compiles in a small number of permissively licensed dependencies: simdutf and Google Highway for SIMD, a compact Unicode table for grapheme data, and Wuffs for image decoding in the Kitty graphics protocol. Both simdutf and Highway are built without libc++, so there is no C++ standard library dependency.
There is no copyleft code anywhere in the dependency tree. Every dependency is listed in a machine-readable manifest with its version, SPDX license, and hash, specifically so distros can fill in their bundled-library metadata more easily.
If a distro would rather dynamically link the system simdutf or Highway, there's a build flag for that.
Portability
libghostty-vt is highly portable. The table below lists all the platforms
and architectures that we build and test (full unit tests plus running all
examples) in CI:
There is a second set of platforms we build in CI but do not run tests for. These systems all have known users; we just haven't set up the machinery for automated testing:
Each of the tested platforms gets the artifacts it expects:
- Linux and FreeBSD get a shared library with a proper SONAME and versioned symbols, a static library, headers, pkg-config files, and a CMake package config.
- macOS and iOS get a dylib with an @rpath install name and
compatibility version, plus an XCFramework covering macOS, iOS, and
the iOS simulator that drops straight into Xcode or Swift Package
Manager.
- Windows gets a DLL with a .pdb, an import library, and a static
library. The library is built with the GNU ABI, but MSVC programs
link against it through the import library and CI verifies this.
- Android gets a shared library built against the NDK with 16 KB page size support, as required by Android 15+.
- WebAssembly ships in two flavors, one optimized for speed and one
for size, and the small one is under a megabyte with every feature
enabled. If you only need a read-only viewer, subsystems like input
encoding, search, selection, and Kitty graphics can be compiled out
with a build flag, which roughly halves the module. Every function
stays exported and simply returns GHOSTTY_UNSUPPORTED so your code
doesn't have to change.4
Portability isn't just about the ability to build and run. It's also about resource usage and being able to work in a wide variety of resource environments.
For resource usage, the library creates no threads, allocates only through
an allocator you can customize, and uses minimal compute and memory.
I've run 10,000 active terminals, each with a live shell producing output,
on a Linux server with 4 cores and 8 GB of RAM. In this test, I had to use
dash because bash used far more RAM than libghostty-vt and wouldn't fit
into the 8 GB budget.
What's Next
Libghostty 1.0 is a major milestone.
You can now create a terminal in ~1,500 lines of C that has more features and better performance than just about any terminal that existed in 2025, and most today in 2026.5 And thanks to the rich ecosystem of language bindings, you can do this in every major programming language. You get this for free. For almost no effort. You can just do it.
libghostty-vt is a generalized library, so we will focus on stability
while implementing as many terminal protocols as exist
(giving options to enable/disable them, of course) so that embedders
have the luxury of choice.
Every bug any libghostty-vt embedder reports makes everyone else in
the ecosystem better. Every feature we ship in libghostty-vt becomes
available to everyone quickly and safely. Terminals can move faster with
less fragmentation.
Thank you to everyone who contributed to the library, wrote bindings, filed bugs, and shipped something on top of it while the API was still experimental. As with all open source, this was the work of a large community.
Docs, examples, and downloads are at lib.ghostty.org.
Boo. 👻
Footnotes
- As usual, I hate that I have to do this for every post, but I want to note this post was completely hand-written with no AI assistance. ↩
- The numbers are rough because most consumers don't report usage, but JetBrains alone reports 12.5 million recurring active users, and that's before counting everything else on the list. ↩
- In the original post I wrote "not even libc!" The default build does
link libc, because the SIMD paths use it and the default allocator is
malloc. Turn off SIMD and the library is freestanding, which is
how it runs on bare-metal RISC-V and ARM targets. ↩
- The feature flags aren't WASM-specific; they work on every target. They're likely useful for things like embedded devices too. ↩
- "Features" here means the set of terminal protocols and sequences
understood, and "performance" means parsing throughput and memory
usage, since those are the parts libghostty-vt provides. ↩

====================================================================================================

Outbound SIP calling is now available to all organizations
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-06
23:03:55 UTC
08:03:55 KST

Choose your API to see its connection steps and session events.
Connect incoming and outgoing phone calls to GPT-Live through a SIP trunk. Your provider exchanges call audio with OpenAI, while your backend handles session configuration, call controls, and business logic. SIP signaling uses TLS, and GPT-Live requires SRTP for call audio.
- Handle an inbound call: receive an incoming-call webhook, then accept or reject the call.
- Place an outbound call: create a session to dial a phone number through your provider. No incoming-call webhook or accept request is needed.
Use a sideband connection when your backend needs to receive session events or send commands. It attaches to the existing conversation while SIP carries the audio. Assign one handler to each action so that duplicate webhook deliveries or events observed on multiple connections don’t execute tools twice.
Handle an inbound call
Route incoming phone calls from your SIP provider to your OpenAI project. Your backend receives a webhook and decides whether to accept each call.
Configure your phone number and trunk
Use a SIP trunking provider, such as Twilio, to connect a phone number to GPT-Live. After purchasing a number from your provider:
- Open your project in OpenAI settings. Under Webhooks, create a webhook endpoint subscribed to live.transport.incoming.
- Find your project ID under General. It starts with proj_; use the same project that owns the webhook.
- Point your provider’s SIP trunk at sip:$PROJECT_ID@sip.api.openai.com;transport=tls. For European data residency, use sip:$PROJECT_ID@sip-eu.api.openai.com;transport=tls instead.
Confirm that GPT-Live SIP support is enabled for your project and that your provider’s SIP trunk is routed to that project before using this flow.
Receive the incoming call
When OpenAI receives a call for your project, it sends a live.transport.incoming event to your webhook endpoint. Verify the webhook signature and deduplicate deliveries, then accept or reject the call.
The webhook identifies a SIP call with data.type: "sip" and provides data.session_id. Use that session ID unchanged for every Live call action. Treat data.sip_headers as untrusted caller metadata, not authorization.
For example, the webhook body contains:
{
  "object": "event",
  "id": "evt_abc123",
  "type": "live.transport.incoming",
  "created_at": 1750287018,
  "data": {
    "type": "sip",
    "session_id": "live_123",
    "sip_headers": [
      { "name": "From", "value": "sip:+14155550123@sip.example.com" },
      { "name": "To", "value": "sip:+18005550100@sip.example.com" },
      { "name": "Call-ID", "value": "call-123@example.com" }
    ]
  }
}
The created_at field is a Unix timestamp in seconds. Delivery headers include webhook-id for deduplication, webhook-timestamp for the delivery attempt, and webhook-signature for signature verification. See webhook verification examples for server-side handlers.
Existing integrations may still receive the deprecated live.call.incoming event, which has no data.type. During migration, handle both names and retain the old subscription until legacy deliveries and retries have drained. The same pending call can also emit a Realtime webhook; assign one handler to the accept/reject decision rather than accepting through both APIs.
Accept or reject the call
Apply your application’s authorization and routing rules. To accept the call, send an authenticated POST /v1/live/sessions/{session_id}/accept request with a top-level session object:
{
  "session": {
    "type": "live",
    "model": "gpt-live-1",
    "instructions": "You are answering an inbound support call.",
    "audio": { "output": { "voice": "marin" } },
    "delegation": { "type": "client" }
  }
}
Use Authorization: Bearer $OPENAI_API_KEY from your trusted backend for call-control requests. Choose the voice and delegation mode at acceptance. SIP negotiates the audio format, so omit audio.format. The example selects client delegation; your backend must handle delegated work. See Delegation and tools for client and Responses configurations.
A successful acceptance returns 200 OK with an empty body after session initialization. Handle HTTP errors before treating the call as accepted.
To reject the call, send POST /v1/live/sessions/{session_id}/reject with a SIP status, such as { "status_code": 486 } for busy. The status must be an integer from 300 through 699. The first accept or reject decision wins; a later competing decision returns decision_already_made.
Attach your backend
After acceptance, connect a sideband WebSocket at wss://api.openai.com/v1/live/sessions/{session_id}/attach. Use the accepted session ID and the same project authentication and connection headers. Do not send session.start again.
SIP carries the call audio. Use the sideband for transcripts, delegation, tools, commands, and reflected audio. Choose one owner for each side effect, even if multiple connections observe an event.
Once connected, use the active call controls to send keypad tones, transfer the call, or hang up.
Place an outbound call
Call a phone number through your SIP provider with Create session. Your provider handles the phone network connection while GPT-Live carries the conversation.
Outbound SIP calling is available to all organizations through the Live API, not the Realtime API call-creation endpoint.
Configure your trunk
Use a trunk that supports TLS signaling and SDES-SRTP media. Enable SRTP in your provider’s settings before placing a call.
Supply the trunk configuration with each request:
The provider endpoint must use sips: for TLS signaling. Don’t include credentials, paths, URI headers, or other URI parameters in the URL. Local hostnames and literal private or local IP addresses are rejected. Keep your OpenAI API key and SIP credentials on your server.
Create the session
Send POST /v1/live/sessions with your session configuration and transport.type: "sip". Choose the voice and delegation mode when creating the session. Omit audio.format because SIP negotiates the audio format.
This example uses curl and jq. Set OPENAI_API_KEY, SIP_USERNAME, and SIP_PASSWORD in your server environment, and replace the example provider endpoint and phone numbers with your own values. The example selects client delegation; your backend must handle delegated work.
jq -n \
  --arg username "$SIP_USERNAME" \
  --arg password "$SIP_PASSWORD" \
  '{
    "session": {
      "model": "gpt-live-1",
      "instructions": "Help the user schedule an appointment.",
      "audio": { "output": { "voice": "marin" } },
      "delegation": { "type": "client" }
    },
    "transport": {
      "type": "sip",
      "destination": "+14155550123",
      "trunk": {
        "provider_url": "sips:sip.example.com:5061",
        "auth": {
          "type": "digest",
          "username": $username,
          "password": $password
        },
        "caller_number": "+14155550100"
      }
    }
  }' | curl https://api.openai.com/v1/live/sessions \
    -H "Authorization: Bearer $OPENAI_API_KEY" \
    -H "Content-Type: application/json" \
    --data-binary @-
The request returns 200 OK after the session initializes:
{
  "session": { "id": "live_123" },
  "transport": { "type": "sip" }
}
This response doesn’t mean the call has been answered. It contains no SDP or trunk credentials. Preserve session.id unchanged for the sideband connection and call controls. You don’t need an incoming-call webhook or an accept request for an outbound call.
Monitor and end the call
Attach your backend to wss://api.openai.com/v1/live/sessions/{session_id}/attach using your OpenAI API key. Don’t send session.start again. The sideband connection carries conversation events, delegated work, and call progress while SIP carries the audio.
Each call-progress event includes event_id and session_id. Attach immediately after creation: the sideband only replays events from the preceding 3 seconds, so a later attachment can miss earlier call progress. Replayed events retain their original event IDs. Deduplicate events by event_id.
Once connected, use the active call controls to send keypad tones, transfer the call, or hang up. Keep the sideband open for session.closed and final usage as described in Usage and graceful close.
Handle limits and errors
Ringing is limited to 3 minutes, and a connected call is limited to 2 hours. These limits aren’t configurable in the creation request.
Invalid session configuration is returned on the creation request. Transport setup failures can return 502, and initialization timeouts return 504. After creation succeeds, monitor transport.failed for asynchronous setup failures.
Manage an active call
These controls apply to both inbound and outbound SIP calls.
Send and receive keypad events
The sideband receives transport.dtmf.received when the caller presses a key. To send a DTMF tone to the SIP trunk, send a transport.dtmf.send client event over the sideband WebSocket. After the tone is successfully sent, the sideband receives transport.dtmf.sent.
If you supply an event_id, the notification includes it as client_event_id; failures return an error event with error.client_event_id.
Transfer or end the call
To transfer the call, send POST /v1/live/sessions/{session_id}/refer with { "target_uri": "sip:agent@example.com" } for your destination. To hang up, send POST /v1/live/sessions/{session_id}/hangup with no request body. Both return 200 OK with an empty body on success.
The transfer target becomes the SIP Refer-To header and can be a telephone URI such as tel:+14155550123 or a SIP URI such as sip:agent@example.com. Your provider handles the remaining transfer flow.
Keep the sideband open until session.closed supplies final usage. See Usage and graceful close for usage reporting and close reasons.
SIP network requirements
GPT-Live SIP calls use separate network paths for signaling and media. Configure your network to allow both:
- SIP signaling: sip.api.openai.com and sip-eu.api.openai.com are GeoIP-routed endpoints. Allow outbound TCP/TLS traffic to the addresses returned by DNS on port 5061.
- SRTP media: The API specifies a separate media IP address and UDP port in the negotiated SDP. Allow bidirectional SRTP traffic over UDP to and from the following CIDRs:
  - 13.79.45.80/28
  - 23.98.140.64/28
  - 40.67.149.176/28
  - 40.83.204.240/28

- Webhooks and server-side controls: manage a session from your backend.
- Delegation and tools: connect speech to your reasoning and tool backend.
- Managing sessions: handle transcripts, session state, and close.
- Latency and cost: understand latency and usage.
- GPT-Live partner integrations: find provider-specific setup guides and examples.
SIP is a protocol used to make phone calls over the internet. With SIP and the Realtime API you can direct incoming phone calls to the API.
Overview
If you want to connect a phone number to the Realtime API, use a SIP trunking provider (e.g., Twilio). This is a service that converts your phone call to IP traffic. After you purchase a phone number from your SIP trunking provider, follow the instructions below.
Start by creating a webhook for incoming calls, through your platform.openai.com settings > Project > Webhooks.
Then, point your SIP trunk at the OpenAI SIP endpoint, using the project ID
for which you configured the webhook, e.g., sip:$PROJECT_ID@sip.api.openai.com;transport=tls.
For European data residency, use sip:$PROJECT_ID@sip-eu.api.openai.com;transport=tls instead.
To find your $PROJECT_ID, visit settings > Project > General. That page will display the project ID, which
will have a proj_ prefix.
When OpenAI receives SIP traffic associated with your project,
your webhook will be fired. The event fired will be a
realtime.call.incoming event,
like the example below:
POST https://my_website.com/webhook_endpoint
user-agent: OpenAI/1.0 (+https://platform.openai.com/docs/webhooks)
content-type: application/json
webhook-id: wh_685342e6c53c8190a1be43f081506c52 # unique id for idempotency
webhook-timestamp: 1750287078 # timestamp of delivery attempt
webhook-signature: v1,K5oZfzN95Z9UVu1EsfQmfVNQhnkZ2pj9o9NDN/H/pI4= # signature to verify authenticity from OpenAI
{
  "object": "event",
  "id": "evt_685343a1381c819085d44c354e1b330e",
  "type": "realtime.call.incoming",
  "created_at": 1750287018, // Unix timestamp
  "data": {
    "call_id": "some_unique_id",
    "sip_headers": [
      { "name": "From", "value": "sip:+142555512112@sip.example.com" },
      { "name": "To", "value": "sip:+18005551212@sip.example.com" },
      { "name": "Call-ID", "value": "03782086-4ce9-44bf-8b0d-4e303d2cc590"}
    ]
  }
}
From this webhook, you can accept or reject the call, using the call_id value from the webhook.
When accepting the call, you’ll provide the needed configuration
(instructions, voice, etc) for the Realtime API session.
Once established, you can set up a WebSocket and monitor the session as usual. The APIs to
accept, reject, monitor, refer, and hangup the call are documented below.
Accept the call
Use the Accept call endpoint to
approve the inbound call and configure the realtime session that will answer it.
Send the same parameters you would send in a
create client secret
request, i.e., ensure the realtime model, voice, tools, or instructions are set before bridging the
call to the model.
curl -X POST "https://api.openai.com/v1/realtime/calls/$CALL_ID/accept" \
  -H "Authorization: Bearer $OPENAI_API_KEY" \
  -H "Content-Type: application/json" \
  -d '{
        "type": "realtime",
        "model": "gpt-realtime-2.1",
        "instructions": "You are Alex, a friendly concierge for Example Corp."
      }'
The request path must include the call_id from the
realtime.call.incoming
webhook, and every request requires the Authorization header shown above. The
endpoint returns 200 OK once the SIP leg is ringing and the realtime session
is being established.
Reject the call
Use the Reject call endpoint to
decline an invite when you do not want to handle the incoming call, (e.g., from
an unsupported country code.) Supply the call_id path parameter
and an optional SIP status_code (e.g., 486 to indicate “busy”) in the JSON
body to control the response sent back to the carrier.
curl -X POST "https://api.openai.com/v1/realtime/calls/$CALL_ID/reject" \
  -H "Authorization: Bearer $OPENAI_API_KEY" \
  -H "Content-Type: application/json" \
  -d '{"status_code": 486}'
If no status code is supplied the API uses 603 Decline by default. A
successful request responds with 200 OK after OpenAI delivers the SIP
response.
Monitor call events
After you accept a call, open a WebSocket connection to the same session to
stream events and issue realtime commands. Note that when connecting to an existing
call using the call_id parameter, the model argument is not used (as it has already been configured
via the accept endpoint).
WebSocket request
GET wss://api.openai.com/v1/realtime?call_id={call_id}
Query parameters
Headers
- Authorization: Bearer YOUR_API_KEY
The WebSocket behaves exactly like any other Realtime API connection. Send
response.create,
and other client events to control the call, and listen for server events to
track progress. See Webhooks and server-side controls
for more information.
import WebSocket from "ws";
const callId = "rtc_u1_9c6574da8b8a41a18da9308f4ad974ce";
const ws = new WebSocket(`wss://api.openai.com/v1/realtime?call_id=${callId}`, {
  headers: {
    Authorization: `Bearer ${process.env.OPENAI_API_KEY}`,
  },
});
ws.on("open", () => {
  ws.send(
    JSON.stringify({
      type: "response.create",
    })
  );
});
Redirect the call
Transfer an active call using the
Refer call endpoint. Provide the
call_id as well as the target_uri that should be placed in the SIP Refer-To
header (for example tel:+14155550123 or sip:agent@example.com).
curl -X POST "https://api.openai.com/v1/realtime/calls/$CALL_ID/refer" \
  -H "Authorization: Bearer $OPENAI_API_KEY" \
  -H "Content-Type: application/json" \
  -d '{"target_uri": "tel:+14155550123"}'
OpenAI returns 200 OK once the REFER is relayed to your SIP provider. The
downstream system handles the rest of the call flow for the caller.
Hang up the call
End the session with the Hang up endpoint when your application should disconnect the caller. This endpoint can be used to terminate both SIP and WebRTC realtime sessions.
curl -X POST "https://api.openai.com/v1/realtime/calls/$CALL_ID/hangup" \
  -H "Authorization: Bearer $OPENAI_API_KEY"
The API responds with 200 OK when it starts tearing down the call.
SIP signaling and media IP ranges
Realtime SIP calls use separate network paths for signaling and media. To ensure proper operation, configure your network to allow signaling and media traffic as described below.
SIP signaling
sip.api.openai.com and sip-eu.api.openai.com are GeoIP-routed endpoints. Your network must allow
outbound TCP/TLS traffic to the addresses returned by DNS on port 5061.
SRTP media
The API specifies a separate media IP address and UDP port in the negotiated SDP. Your network must allow bidirectional SRTP traffic over UDP to and from the following CIDRs:
- 13.79.45.80/28
- 23.98.140.64/28
- 40.67.149.176/28
- 40.83.204.240/28
Server examples
The following is an example of a realtime.call.incoming handler. It accepts the call and then logs all the events from
the Realtime API.
For the Ruby example, set the OPENAI_API_KEY and OPENAI_WEBHOOK_SECRET
environment variables, then install the required dependencies with
gem install openai webrick async-websocket.
from flask import Flask, request, Response, jsonify, make_response
from openai import OpenAI, InvalidWebhookSignatureError
import asyncio
import json
import os
import requests
import time
import threading
import websockets
app = Flask(__name__)
client = OpenAI(webhook_secret=os.environ["OPENAI_WEBHOOK_SECRET"])
AUTH_HEADER = {"Authorization": "Bearer " + os.environ["OPENAI_API_KEY"]}
call_accept = {
    "type": "realtime",
    "instructions": "You are a support agent.",
    "model": "gpt-realtime-2.1",
}
response_create = {
    "type": "response.create",
    "response": {
        "instructions": ("Say to the user 'Thank you for calling, how can I help you'")
    },
}
async def websocket_task(call_id):
    try:
        async with websockets.connect(
            "wss://api.openai.com/v1/realtime?call_id=" + call_id,
            additional_headers=AUTH_HEADER,
        ) as websocket:
            await websocket.send(json.dumps(response_create))
            while True:
                response = await websocket.recv()
                print(f"Received from WebSocket: {response}")
    except Exception as e:
        print(f"WebSocket error: {e}")
@app.route("/", methods=["POST"])
def webhook():
    try:
        event = client.webhooks.unwrap(request.data, request.headers)
    except (InvalidWebhookSignatureError, ValueError) as e:
        print("Invalid signature", e)
        return Response("Invalid signature", status=400)
    if event.type == "realtime.call.incoming":
        requests.post(
            "https://api.openai.com/v1/realtime/calls/"
            + event.data.call_id
            + "/accept",
            headers={**AUTH_HEADER, "Content-Type": "application/json"},
            json=call_accept,
        ).raise_for_status()
        threading.Thread(
            target=lambda: asyncio.run(websocket_task(event.data.call_id)),
            daemon=True,
        ).start()
    return Response(status=200)
if __name__ == "__main__":
    app.run(port=8000)

Now that you’ve connected over SIP, use the left navigation or click into these pages to start building your realtime application.
