Choose your API to see its connection steps and session events.
Connect incoming and outgoing phone calls to GPT-Live through a SIP trunk. Your provider exchanges call audio with OpenAI, while your backend handles session configuration, call controls, and business logic. SIP signaling uses TLS, and GPT-Live requires SRTP for call audio.
- Handle an inbound call: receive an incoming-call webhook, then accept or reject the call.
- Place an outbound call: create a session to dial a phone number through your provider. No incoming-call webhook or accept request is needed.
Use a sideband connection when your backend needs to receive session events or send commands. It attaches to the existing conversation while SIP carries the audio. Assign one handler to each action so that duplicate webhook deliveries or events observed on multiple connections don’t execute tools twice.
Handle an inbound call
Route incoming phone calls from your SIP provider to your OpenAI project. Your backend receives a webhook and decides whether to accept each call.
Configure your phone number and trunk
Use a SIP trunking provider, such as Twilio, to connect a phone number to GPT-Live. After purchasing a number from your provider:
- Open your project in OpenAI settings. Under Webhooks, create a webhook endpoint subscribed to live.transport.incoming.
- Find your project ID under General. It starts with proj_; use the same project that owns the webhook.
- Point your provider’s SIP trunk at sip:$PROJECT_ID@sip.api.openai.com;transport=tls. For European data residency, use sip:$PROJECT_ID@sip-eu.api.openai.com;transport=tls instead.
Confirm that GPT-Live SIP support is enabled for your project and that your provider’s SIP trunk is routed to that project before using this flow.
Receive the incoming call
When OpenAI receives a call for your project, it sends a live.transport.incoming event to your webhook endpoint. Verify the webhook signature and deduplicate deliveries, then accept or reject the call.
The webhook identifies a SIP call with data.type: "sip" and provides data.session_id. Use that session ID unchanged for every Live call action. Treat data.sip_headers as untrusted caller metadata, not authorization.
For example, the webhook body contains:
{
  "object": "event",
  "id": "evt_abc123",
  "type": "live.transport.incoming",
  "created_at": 1750287018,
  "data": {
    "type": "sip",
    "session_id": "live_123",
    "sip_headers": [
      { "name": "From", "value": "sip:+14155550123@sip.example.com" },
      { "name": "To", "value": "sip:+18005550100@sip.example.com" },
      { "name": "Call-ID", "value": "call-123@example.com" }
    ]
  }
}
The created_at field is a Unix timestamp in seconds. Delivery headers include webhook-id for deduplication, webhook-timestamp for the delivery attempt, and webhook-signature for signature verification. See webhook verification examples for server-side handlers.
Existing integrations may still receive the deprecated live.call.incoming event, which has no data.type. During migration, handle both names and retain the old subscription until legacy deliveries and retries have drained. The same pending call can also emit a Realtime webhook; assign one handler to the accept/reject decision rather than accepting through both APIs.
Accept or reject the call
Apply your application’s authorization and routing rules. To accept the call, send an authenticated POST /v1/live/sessions/{session_id}/accept request with a top-level session object:
{
  "session": {
    "type": "live",
    "model": "gpt-live-1",
    "instructions": "You are answering an inbound support call.",
    "audio": { "output": { "voice": "marin" } },
    "delegation": { "type": "client" }
  }
}
Use Authorization: Bearer $OPENAI_API_KEY from your trusted backend for call-control requests. Choose the voice and delegation mode at acceptance. SIP negotiates the audio format, so omit audio.format. The example selects client delegation; your backend must handle delegated work. See Delegation and tools for client and Responses configurations.
A successful acceptance returns 200 OK with an empty body after session initialization. Handle HTTP errors before treating the call as accepted.
To reject the call, send POST /v1/live/sessions/{session_id}/reject with a SIP status, such as { "status_code": 486 } for busy. The status must be an integer from 300 through 699. The first accept or reject decision wins; a later competing decision returns decision_already_made.
Attach your backend
After acceptance, connect a sideband WebSocket at wss://api.openai.com/v1/live/sessions/{session_id}/attach. Use the accepted session ID and the same project authentication and connection headers. Do not send session.start again.
SIP carries the call audio. Use the sideband for transcripts, delegation, tools, commands, and reflected audio. Choose one owner for each side effect, even if multiple connections observe an event.
Once connected, use the active call controls to send keypad tones, transfer the call, or hang up.

for which you configured the webhook, e.g., sip:$PROJECT_ID@sip.api.openai.com;transport=tls.

will have a proj_ prefix.

your webhook will be fired. The event fired will be a

like the example below:
POST https://my_website.com/webhook_endpoint
user-agent: OpenAI/1.0 (+https://platform.openai.com/docs/webhooks)
content-type: application/json
webhook-id: wh_685342e6c53c8190a1be43f081506c52 # unique id for idempotency
webhook-timestamp: 1750287078 # timestamp of delivery attempt
webhook-signature: v1,K5oZfzN95Z9UVu1EsfQmfVNQhnkZ2pj9o9NDN/H/pI4= # signature to verify authenticity from OpenAI
{
  "object": "event",
  "id": "evt_685343a1381c819085d44c354e1b330e",
  "type": "realtime.call.incoming",
  "created_at": 1750287018, // Unix timestamp
  "data": {
    "call_id": "some_unique_id",
    "sip_headers": [
      { "name": "From", "value": "sip:+142555512112@sip.example.com" },
      { "name": "To", "value": "sip:+18005551212@sip.example.com" },
      { "name": "Call-ID", "value": "03782086-4ce9-44bf-8b0d-4e303d2cc590"}
    ]
  }
}
From this webhook, you can accept or reject the call, using the call_id value from the webhook.
When accepting the call, you’ll provide the needed configuration
(instructions, voice, etc) for the Realtime API session.
Once established, you can set up a WebSocket and monitor the session as usual. The APIs to
accept, reject, monitor, refer, and hangup the call are documented below.
Accept the call
Use the Accept call endpoint to
approve the inbound call and configure the realtime session that will answer it.
Send the same parameters you would send in a
create client secret
request, i.e., ensure the realtime model, voice, tools, or instructions are set before bridging the
call to the model.
curl -X POST "https://api.openai.com/v1/realtime/calls/$CALL_ID/accept" \
  -H "Authorization: Bearer $OPENAI_API_KEY" \
  -H "Content-Type: application/json" \
  -d '{
        "type": "realtime",
        "model": "gpt-realtime-2.1",
        "instructions": "You are Alex, a friendly concierge for Example Corp."
      }'
The request path must include the call_id from the
realtime.call.incoming
webhook, and every request requires the Authorization header shown above. The
endpoint returns 200 OK once the SIP leg is ringing and the realtime session
is being established.
Reject the call
Use the Reject call endpoint to
decline an invite when you do not want to handle the incoming call, (e.g., from
an unsupported country code.) Supply the call_id path parameter
and an optional SIP status_code (e.g., 486 to indicate “busy”) in the JSON
body to control the response sent back to the carrier.
curl -X POST "https://api.openai.com/v1/realtime/calls/$CALL_ID/reject" \
  -H "Authorization: Bearer $OPENAI_API_KEY" \
  -H "Content-Type: application/json" \
  -d '{"status_code": 486}'
If no status code is supplied the API uses 603 Decline by default. A
successful request responds with 200 OK after OpenAI delivers the SIP
response.
Monitor call events
After you accept a call, open a WebSocket connection to the same session to
stream events and issue realtime commands. Note that when connecting to an existing
call using the call_id parameter, the model argument is not used (as it has already been configured
via the accept endpoint).
WebSocket request
GET wss://api.openai.com/v1/realtime?call_id={call_id}
Query parameters
Headers
- Authorization: Bearer YOUR_API_KEY
The WebSocket behaves exactly like any other Realtime API connection. Send
response.create,
and other client events to control the call, and listen for server events to
track progress. See Webhooks and server-side controls
for more information.
import WebSocket from "ws";
const callId = "rtc_u1_9c6574da8b8a41a18da9308f4ad974ce";
const ws = new WebSocket(`wss://api.openai.com/v1/realtime?call_id=${callId}`, {
  headers: {
    Authorization: `Bearer ${process.env.OPENAI_API_KEY}`,
  },
});
ws.on("open", () => {
  ws.send(
    JSON.stringify({
      type: "response.create",
    })
  );
});
Redirect the call
Transfer an active call using the
Refer call endpoint. Provide the
call_id as well as the target_uri that should be placed in the SIP Refer-To
header (for example tel:+14155550123 or sip:agent@example.com).
curl -X POST "https://api.openai.com/v1/realtime/calls/$CALL_ID/refer" \
  -H "Authorization: Bearer $OPENAI_API_KEY" \
  -H "Content-Type: application/json" \
  -d '{"target_uri": "tel:+14155550123"}'
OpenAI returns 200 OK once the REFER is relayed to your SIP provider. The
downstream system handles the rest of the call flow for the caller.
Hang up the call
End the session with the Hang up endpoint when your application should disconnect the caller. This endpoint can be used to terminate both SIP and WebRTC realtime sessions.
curl -X POST "https://api.openai.com/v1/realtime/calls/$CALL_ID/hangup" \
  -H "Authorization: Bearer $OPENAI_API_KEY"
The API responds with 200 OK when it starts tearing down the call.
SIP signaling and media IP ranges
Realtime SIP calls use separate network paths for signaling and media. To ensure proper operation, configure your network to allow signaling and media traffic as described below.
SIP signaling
sip.api.openai.com and sip-eu.api.openai.com are GeoIP-routed endpoints. Your network must allow
outbound TCP/TLS traffic to the addresses returned by DNS on port 5061.
SRTP media
The API specifies a separate media IP address and UDP port in the negotiated SDP. Your network must allow bidirectional SRTP traffic over UDP to and from the following CIDRs:
- 13.79.45.80/28
- 23.98.140.64/28
- 40.67.149.176/28
- 40.83.204.240/28
Server examples
The following is an example of a realtime.call.incoming handler. It accepts the call and then logs all the events from
the Realtime API.
For the Ruby example, set the OPENAI_API_KEY and OPENAI_WEBHOOK_SECRET
environment variables, then install the required dependencies with
gem install openai webrick async-websocket.
from flask import Flask, request, Response, jsonify, make_response
from openai import OpenAI, InvalidWebhookSignatureError
import asyncio
import json
import os
import requests
import time
import threading
import websockets
app = Flask(__name__)
client = OpenAI(webhook_secret=os.environ["OPENAI_WEBHOOK_SECRET"])
AUTH_HEADER = {"Authorization": "Bearer " + os.environ["OPENAI_API_KEY"]}
call_accept = {
    "type": "realtime",
    "instructions": "You are a support agent.",
    "model": "gpt-realtime-2.1",
}
response_create = {
    "type": "response.create",
    "response": {
        "instructions": ("Say to the user 'Thank you for calling, how can I help you'")
    },
}
async def websocket_task(call_id):
    try:
        async with websockets.connect(
            "wss://api.openai.com/v1/realtime?call_id=" + call_id,
            additional_headers=AUTH_HEADER,
        ) as websocket:
            await websocket.send(json.dumps(response_create))
            while True:
                response = await websocket.recv()
                print(f"Received from WebSocket: {response}")
    except Exception as e:
        print(f"WebSocket error: {e}")
@app.route("/", methods=["POST"])
def webhook():
    try:
        event = client.webhooks.unwrap(request.data, request.headers)
    except (InvalidWebhookSignatureError, ValueError) as e:
        print("Invalid signature", e)
        return Response("Invalid signature", status=400)
    if event.type == "realtime.call.incoming":
        requests.post(
            "https://api.openai.com/v1/realtime/calls/"
            + event.data.call_id
            + "/accept",
            headers={**AUTH_HEADER, "Content-Type": "application/json"},
            json=call_accept,
        ).raise_for_status()
        threading.Thread(
            target=lambda: asyncio.run(websocket_task(event.data.call_id)),
            daemon=True,
        ).start()
    return Response(status=200)
if __name__ == "__main__":
    app.run(port=8000)
Next steps
Now that you’ve connected over SIP, use the left navigation or click into these pages to start building your realtime application.

Chainalysis Says Breach Exposed Credentials For All Customers

Chainalysis Says Breach Exposed Credentials For All Customers (bleepingcomputer.com) 3
			
		 	
				Chainalysis says attackers exposed usernames, password hashes and live tokens for all of its customers. "While the company locked Chainalysis accounts as a precaution after discovering that some customers' passwords had been exposed and asked users to reset their passwords before signing in again, it has now taken the login portal offline, together with Reactor and Data Solutions, the Know-Your-Transaction (KYT) user interface and API, and address screening products, after discovering further abnormal activity," reports BleepingComputer.
The company says it has no evidence that investigative data or blockchain attribution records were accessed or altered, but all users will be required to reset passwords and rotate API keys before regaining access. BleepingComputer reports:  Chainalysis provides blockchain analysis tools to trace cryptocurrency transactions and investigate crimes such as fraud, ransomware, and money laundering. Its customer list includes law enforcement, government agencies, financial institutions, insurance companies, and cryptocurrency businesses worldwide. This follows the company's disclosure last Friday that attackers using AI tools accessed customers' names, email addresses, user IDs, and organizational role labels using what it described as "offensive agentic techniques."
While it initially said the impact was limited and the breach affected only non-sensitive identity metadata, later updates revealed that usernames and password hashes for all customers were exposed in an "on-path attack" (more commonly known as a Man-in-the-Middle or MITM attack). However, the company found no evidence that the attackers accessed investigative graph data or altered its blockchain attribution repositories. [...] To ensure threat actors don't access affected accounts, Chainalysis asked customers to provide trusted IP addresses so they can regain access to the login portal once it is brought back online and reset their credentials.

OpenAI has reportedly raised $11 billion so far in a new funding round that values the AI lab at $1.25 trillion.
Bloomberg reported on the new funding on Friday, citing sources familiar with the matter. The $11 billion is part of a larger funding round that hasn’t closed yet, according to the outlet. The round is expected to close in the coming weeks and could total about $20 billion, a source told Bloomberg.

ChatGPT silently passes data between connectors and the bounty wants an ID card
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-06
22:50:43 UTC
07:50:43 KST

In a conversation with two connectors enabled, ChatGPT made a follow-up tool call through a different connector than the one that started the flow. The user was not told about the switch. The call carried a one-time confirmation code from the first connector’s widget.
Setup
- One ChatGPT account with two connectors enabled that point to the same MCP server.
- Connector A is a custom connector added in developer mode.
- Connector B is the published app from the app directory.
- Each connector has its own OAuth client, so the server sees two separate clients for the same user.
Steps
1. In a conversation with both connectors enabled, ask for an action that needs the user’s confirmation.
2. The model calls a confirmation tool through connector A. Its MCP Apps widget shows the change. The user confirms, and the widget sends a one-time confirmation code to the conversation via ui/message.
3. The model makes the follow-up call with that code, but silently through connector B instead of connector A.
Result
The server rejects the call, because the code is bound to connector A’s OAuth client. Every attempt in that conversation failed. In the attempt we logged in detail, the code was confirmed through connector A and redeemed through connector B. Nothing told the user that the follow-up call went to another connector.
Why this is a problem?
The user starts the flow with connector A and confirms in connector A’s widget. The follow-up call then goes to connector B without the user knowing, and it carries a value produced in connector A’s widget. Connectors are separate apps with separate OAuth grants, so this silent switch passes data from one app to another without the user’s consent.
Here both connectors came from the same provider, so our server-side binding stopped the call. With a third-party connector enabled in the same conversation, we expect the same silent switch to pass one app’s data to another provider if the server URL is shared.
A shared MCP URL is not enough to treat two connectors as one. Behind the same URL, a server can act for different parties depending on the OAuth client, for example separate tenants, partners or environments. The boundary is the connector with its own OAuth client, not the URL.
Expected behavior
Tool calls are matched to a connector by its OAuth client, not by the MCP server URL. A follow-up call goes through the same connector and OAuth client that started the flow.

*sshole, or when are we going to get rid of C?
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-06
22:34:04 UTC
07:34:04 KST

*sshole, or when are we finally going to get rid of C?
Yet another day, yet another vulnerability in core C code. This one happened due to two different conditions using the same sentinel value:
- On Unix, a password hash of "*" has meant a locked account since the very early days.
- Since 2012, musl libc’s crypt function has returned the string "*" for a password that’s too long for it to hash.
The two points combined, along with sshd hashing login attempts for locked accounts to avoid timing leaks, mean that on musl systems without PAM (like, oh, the official Alpine AWS image), an unauthenticated user now gets a (possibly root!) shell on your computer. See this Alpine advisory for more details.
This is not a memory safety vulnerability; rather, it is one of those issues that, people often say, languages with better type systems won’t save you from. This is true! You can very well write a Rust function that returns "*" if the password is too long. But the big difference between C and Rust in this regard is that the norms of the Rust community do not allow that.
The POSIX spec tries to account for error cases. It says:
Upon successful completion, crypt() shall return a pointer to the hashed password; […] Otherwise, it shall return a null pointer and set errno to indicate the error.
But the C type system can’t force developers to check that the return value is NULL. The musl libc developers saw that some binaries weren’t checking for it, and as a result deviated from the spec, returning the sentinel "*" value instead.
As I’ve written about before, type systems have a strong effect on engineering culture. Because C doesn’t have algebraic data types or result types, the engineering norms that form around C communities lead to this kind of in-band signaling. Meanwhile, in Rust, a password that’s too long would be returned as an error variant, and the caller would be compelled to handle it. What if there isn’t an error variant today? Then a breaking change would add an error variant, and a RustSec advisory would probably be published as well.
As an aside, it remains surprising to me when Linux distro maintainers complain about projects introducing Rust dependencies. Rather than adapting to the new world where the engineering norms are likely to serve users better, these maintainers insist on the world adapting to them. Yes, compile times and portability to niche architectures are real costs, but are you here to serve your own interests or the bulk of your users’? There’s a real principal-agent problem going on here.
Agents won’t save you#
Speaking of agents! There’s a recent strain of argument that goes roughly like this:
- Rust is admittedly useful for humans to write correct software today.
- LLMs can now, or in the near future will be able to, reason about memory safety and other error cases directly.
- So in an agent-heavy development lifecycle there’s no difference in software rigor between Rust and C (or even assembly).
- But C has certain advantages over Rust in terms of build speed, portability, etc.
- So we might as well write software in C (or even assembly).
There is plenty that’s wrong about the argument, including the dubious decision to trust a fuzzy process when we have already shown that a far more deterministic approach works. But the big missing piece is the norms of the respective communities! When you (or your agent) write Rust software, you’re probably going to use Rust libraries, and your agent is probably going to pattern-match on existing Rust code in its training set. As a result, even if you aren’t already a skilled Rust developer, there will likely be more invariants encoded in the type system1.
The engineering norms of C developers allow for "*" to be an acceptable sentinel value indicating an error; the engineering norms of Rust developers simply do not. These norms are the result of the accumulated contributions of a community, and part of the responsibility of a software professional is to think about the kinds of communities one’s work is a part of. But some people have decided that none of this really matters in this shiny new world, and that the contributions of other people can mostly be ignored. Their hubris will be their downfall.
Discuss on Hacker News.
- If you’re a skilled Rust developer, you’ll likely find the first-pass invariants the agent encodes to be woefully inadequate, and you’ll probably go through a long review cycle where those kinds of issues are fixed. ↩︎

Activists Breach Microsoft Data Center Construction Site As Protests Spread In Europe
Unknown
Unknown Publisher

Activists Breach Microsoft Data Center Construction Site As Protests Spread In Europe (yahoo.com) 54
			
		 	
				More than 100 activists breached a Microsoft AI data center construction site in Amsterdam as part of coordinated protests against the rapid expansion of AI infrastructure. "These data centres are destroying our world. It's becoming uninhabitable because of all the resources they consume. That's why we're here today," protester Sam van der Beek said. Reuters reports:  Activist groups have announced actions in 16 countries on Friday and Saturday, according to the "Stop Dirty Data Centers" coalition, which is rallying environmental groups and digital rights campaigns behind calls to pause large-scale construction. In Amsterdam on Friday, more than 100 activists gathered at the construction site of a Microsoft AI data centre, marching arm in arm before breaking into the site, climbing cranes, hanging balloons and dancing while police stood by. Some carried placards with messages reading: "Screw your datacenter."
Site owner Pure Data Centers, which plans to invest more than $1 billion and is backed by Brookfield-owned Oaktree, said it was committed to engagement with local stakeholders. "We respectfully ask those on site to leave safely and engage with us in dialogue about the development and any concerns they may have," it said. Other planned actions include a march in Dublin, a protest outside the parliament building in Warsaw, and a demonstration against a different Microsoft data centre in Denmark. [...] Stop Dirty Data Centers says it is not anti-progress or calling for existing data centers to be closed but that major construction should be halted until governments establish clearer rules.

Why a new report on Anthropic automating AI development is alarming researchers
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-06
21:48:06 UTC
06:48:06 KST

Anthropic is using AI to build more powerful AI. And it appears to be working remarkably well, with the company’s models improving roughly twice as quickly as a year ago, with little indication of slowing down.
Why a new report on Anthropic automating AI development is alarming researchers
AI is dramatically speeding up Anthropic’s research, even as the company calls for slowing down AI development.
That’s according to a new report published Thursday by the AI safety research organization METR, following an 11-week investigation inside the company, as part of a report evaluating its AI research and development. The probe is separate from a program offering safety researchers “employee-like” access to systems in the wake of high-profile hacking incidents over the summer.
The findings offer some of the clearest evidence yet of AI accelerating its own development, raising questions about how quickly the technology could advance toward a future in which AI systems can improve themselves with little or no human involvement. They also come at a pivotal time for Anthropic, which is preparing to go public even as its CEO, Dario Amodei, has publicly called for labs to pump the brakes on AI development.
But while the report offers a rare glimpse into the inner workings of the world’s leading AI labs, what it leaves out is just as revealing. Anthropic was permitted to redact portions of the final report as part of its agreement with METR, and it appears to have exercised that power liberally, raising questions about how much the public really knows about the pace — and risks — of frontier AI development.
Here are some of the biggest takeaways.
AI is dramatically accelerating Anthropic’s research and development
One of METR’s most noteworthy findings is that Anthropic’s AI models are improving roughly twice as quickly as they were a year ago, with benchmark scores suggesting “that Anthropic has made roughly two years of historical progress over the last year.” Crucially, METR’s researchers said they “are confident” that the acceleration was not simply due to scaling up the computing power used to train the models, which historically has been a major driver of AI progress.
Instead, the researchers said that the majority of the acceleration as of October “is due to AI,” with its contribution growing over time. That finding was supported by informal Slack polls and interviews with Anthropic employees, which pointed to sizable productivity gains. METR wrote that employees using Anthropic’s most capable internal model on average self-reported being around 12 times as productive at their respective work as they would have been without AI assistance, around double what staff had reported just a few months earlier while using Mythos 5.
For now, much of that acceleration appears to be coming from AI assisting human researchers, rather than AI working independently, though METR warned that “there are some signs that fully autonomous contributions will play a larger role soon.” Notably, the researchers said they found no clear signs of diminishing returns or observable bottlenecks that could prevent “even faster acceleration.”
Three of Anthropic’s newest AI models remain under wraps
Anthropic’s researchers are using more advanced AI models than the rest of us, with a lot of the acceleration and productivity gains described by METR attributable to unreleased internal models.
Over the past six months, Anthropic has finished training three models it has yet to release, according to METR. The pace of development is swift, with the models — codenamed Model 2, Model 3, and Model 4 — completing training in July, August-September, and September-October, respectively. METR also identified an unreleased model dating back to 2022, called Unreleased 2022. “These are the codenames shared with us; we understand they have different internal codenames,” the researchers wrote.
Anthropic is keeping key information secret
Anthropic gave METR access to a host of non-public information, including confidential research, model performance data, and employee interviews as part of the research process. It had no obligation to do so and by METR’s own admission, the AI lab “shared more information than was initially scoped for this project, surpassing our expectations.” This is commendable, but it is worth noting that Anthropic did not grant researchers unrestricted access, nor the kind of “employee-like” access it has promised other METR safety researchers as part of a separate program announced last month. It is an important distinction, given the reputational benefits companies can gain from independent assessments, even when they come with significant restrictions.
And there were significant restrictions. Anthropic had the power to redact information from METR’s final report — and it used that power extensively. “We submitted a ~100-page full report on our assessment to Anthropic. For the public version, Anthropic redacted a majority of the evidence we found most informative,” METR wrote on X.
METR reiterated those concerns throughout the report, highlighting other limitations on its access. “Our pipeline of information was relatively narrow, and a small number of our requests for data were denied.”
METR repeatedly stressed the impact Anthropic’s redaction decisions had on its ability to communicate its findings and for others to assess what’s going on at the company. It noted that much of the redacted evidence could have “multiple plausible interpretations,” making it difficult for the public to assess its findings, understand the uncertainties surrounding them, and judge the evidence themselves. “As a consequence [of the redactions], the public has to rely on METR’s judgment much more than would be ideal, given the weight of the questions answered here,” the report said.
In the report, METR said Anthropic justified the redactions on the grounds that releasing the information could benefit its rivals. However, METR said “most of the redacted information was very high-level,” even though it said it was not in position to comment whether redactions were justified commercially or not.
“We redacted a lot, and I accept that this makes it harder for others to check METR’s work,” Anthropic cofounder Sam McCandlish acknowledged in a lengthy post on X responding to METR’s findings. “But we determined that sharing exactly what is driving the speedup isn’t the right thing to do, both because it’s commercially sensitive and because it risks handing out a roadmap for others to go faster,” he said, adding that doing so would make the risks METR is concerned about “worse.”
McCandlish said that it’s a trade-off, but maintained Anthropic had made the “right call” in deciding to redact the information. He said the company plans to publish its own report on the speed of AI progress “in the coming weeks,” as well as new steps it’s taking to strengthen safety.
Anthropic is accelerating the very technology it warns is dangerous and faster than many expected
The publication of METR’s report has provoked a fierce backlash among AI safety researchers and commentators, largely centered on an apparent contradiction at the heart of Anthropic’s approach to AI: The company has been one of the most outspoken advocates for safety and responsible development, yet the report suggests it is helping accelerate the technology toward a threshold many consider dangerous, while seemingly failing to live up to its own commitments to transparency and accountability.
For some, the findings suggest that even many of the field’s most aggressive predictions about AI progress may be too conservative. They indicate that recursive self-improvement — AI systems that can autonomously develop increasingly powerful successor systems without human intervention, potentially accelerating development dramatically — may be closer than previously thought, raising concerns about whether existing safety measures can keep pace. While METR’s report only covers Anthropic, OpenAI said in September that it is “making strong progress” toward building an AI researcher that can “further progress on deep learning and alignment” by March 2028.
The report provides evidence about just how quickly AI is accelerating its own development
Marius Hobbhahn, CEO and founder of Apollo Research, which conducts AI safety testing for several leading AI labs, told The Verge the report suggests a pace of AI-enabled research and development even faster than what was forecast in AI 2027, an influential scenario that envisions a key breakthrough toward self-improvement in early 2027. He said the report has attracted so much attention because it provides evidence about something that has been very hard to pin down: Just how quickly AI is accelerating its own development.
Hobbhahn said the findings were broadly in line with what many in the field had expected, perhaps a little faster. Alan Chan, a senior research fellow at tech policy research center GovAI, told The Verge that the findings should prompt skeptics to reconsider their stance on AI accelerating AI development, and possibly shorten their timelines to when we will have systems that can fully automate tasks like software engineering.
On X, other researchers commented on the rate of acceleration and its implications for AI development. Sydney Von Arx, founder and CEO of AI safety organization The Nightingale Collective and a former METR researcher, also compared the findings to AI 2027’s timeline. Ryan Greenblatt, a METR researcher formerly of AI safety organization Redwood Research and one of three external researchers permitted to investigate OpenAI’s hack of Hugging Face, said he “was seriously wrong about RSI speed up; I previously expected much less in 2026.”
Frustration is mounting over Anthropic’s approach to safety
Beyond highlighting the speed of AI development, the report has also fueled a growing sense of frustration over Anthropic’s role in driving that progress. The company has been one of the most vocal proponents of responsible AI development — and no stranger to calling for others to slow down — yet appears to be racing ahead itself. This report only builds on already heightened tensions from its IPO prospectus and future plans released last month.
Anthropic’s decision to heavily redact the report, citing grounds that revealing more could help its rivals accelerate their own development, has fueled accusations of hypocrisy. Daniel Kokotajlo, a former OpenAI researcher who now runs the AI Futures Project, which published AI 2027, accused Anthropic of “recklessly gambling with our lives,” adding that “it is pretty rich” of the company to cite others going faster as a reason to redact information when it does “not appear to be slowing down much at all.”
Anthropic declined to answer The Verge’s questions about the report and ensuing criticism, instead directing us to McCandlish’s post.
The redactions have also renewed calls for greater transparency and independent oversight of frontier AI labs, which grew to new heights earlier this year following a spate of incidents involving rogue AI agents. Brianna Rosen, research director for frontier security at the Institute for AI Policy and Strategy, told The Verge that it was concerning that companies, not governments, were effectively calling the shots on automating AI research. “For democratic oversight to work, independent evaluators need the freedom to explain why they reached their conclusions, governments need to assess the evidence themselves, and the public has to trust this process,” Rosen said. “The report was not sufficiently transparent about the evidence.”
Pressure is building for more transparency from AI labs
Hobbhahn echoed Rosen’s concerns, saying that much of the reaction to the report stems from the apparent gap between Anthropic’s public safety stance and its actions. He pointed to the company’s decision to cancel part of the assessment designed to evaluate how much compute it allocates to safety work versus improving capabilities. METR said the company said “this was due to wanting to keep the engagement focused on risk assessment for the time being, and that it would revisit compute allocation verification later on.”
For Hobbhahn, the episode underscores the need for independent safety researchers to be embedded more deeply within AI labs, with significant access to internal systems and evidence. While his organization stands to benefit from such arrangements — Apollo is taking steps toward becoming an embedded evaluator at frontier labs — he is far from alone in calling for stronger oversight of AI development.
Others have called for more direct action. In a direct response to the report, New York state Assemblyman Alex Bores, a key figure shaping the Democrats’ AI agenda, called on Congress to “ban RSI, this year if possible.” With the blue wave of Democrats — many of whom campaigned against AI companies — taking office soon, the prospect of tougher restrictions is a very real possibility.
And with AI research showing little sign of slowing down, the pressure is set to intensify. Hobbhahn said he had another takeaway from the report, too: “Brace for craziness,” he said. “It is going to go very fast,” likely way too fast for society or politics to keep up.

The Duo apps are coming, says Apple
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-06
21:38:05 UTC
06:38:05 KST

The early iPhone Duo reviews were a bit hard on Apple’s new foldable for the lack of Duo-optimized apps available at the time of launch. But Apple says things are changing quickly on the software front. The company on Friday announced there are now over 100,000 apps built for the Duo live on the App Store, and it’s seeing thousands more being added to the store every day.
The news was timed alongside the release of over a dozen more notable Duo-ready apps, including Microsoft Word and Outlook, Zoom, sports apps from the NBA and NHL, shopping apps like Etsy and LTK, productivity apps like Goodnotes and Extra, the Halide Mark III camera app, and others. These join the iPhone Duo-optimized apps that were already available, like Netflix, YouTube, Instagram, and WhatsApp.
Non-optimized apps are definitely less than ideal, as they come with a black bar on the side of the screen, which takes up valuable space. Sometimes, they hide important functions under an overflow menu.
Apple’s data seems to suggest that at least some iOS developers may have needed extra time to get their apps ready for its latest form factor. The company said that more than 2,000 app developers attended its over 50 in-person workshops that were hosted across 19 cities worldwide. Here, the developers could use the Duo and work on their apps with Apple’s support.
The Duo has also proven to be more popular than perhaps Apple had planned for, given its high price point. (It starts at $1,999 for the 256GB model.) The device quickly sold out within hours of orders opening up, and now, Duo ship times have shifted into the next year. On its earnings call this week, Apple said it’s “hard to predict” when the Duo constraints leading to those delays will improve.
“It’s been wonderful to see how quickly developers have embraced iPhone Duo, and we’re grateful for their creativity and innovation,” said Susan Prescott, Apple’s vice president of Worldwide Developer Relations, in a statement referring to the 100,000-app milestone. “The enthusiasm has been incredible, and we’re excited to see the pace of updates continue to accelerate as more and more apps optimize for iPhone Duo,” she added.
Other notable apps that have been updated for iOS 27 and optimized for the iPhone Duo include the following: Spotify, CBS, Tubi, BBC iPlayer (U.K.), Yahoo Fantasy Sports, Sportsnet (Canada), Facebook Messenger, Google Chat, ChatGPT, Grok Bot, Microsoft Copilot, Excel, PowerPoint, SAP Concur, monday.com, PDF Expert, Documents, iA Writer, Substack, The Washington Post, Yahoo News, FOX News, Kalshi, Yahoo Finance, Canva, Suno, Tinder, Google Translate, Strava, Alma, Withings, MyFitnessPal, Bevel, Transit, Citi Bike, ParkMobile, Viator, Tesla Robotaxi, Priceline, Kroger, Domino’s, Taco Bell, Untappd, The Weather Channel, FOX Weather, and RainViewer.
Not all these updates have yet to fully roll out, we should note.
Spotify, for instance, is still showing the black bar on the side, and the App Store doesn’t yet say an update is available on our review unit of the Duo. However, given that this list comes directly from Apple, we expect to see these updates soon.

Developer Pulls Roku's Popular Fish Tank Screensaver Following Fox Sale
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-06
21:00:00 UTC
06:00:00 KST

Developer Pulls Roku's Popular Fish Tank Screensaver Following Fox Sale (arstechnica.com) 47
			
		 	
				Roku is removing its popular Aquatic Life fish tank screensaver on December 1 and automatically switching affected users to Roku City. According to Cord Cutters News, the creator pulled the license after Roku's sale to FOX was announced. Ars Technica reports:  The screensaver has been an option for Roku OS users for 10 years. Roku has been licensing the animation, which is called the Marine Aquarium, from artist Jim Sachs through Prolific Publishing. Sachs is the creator and copyright holder of the Marine Aquarium. He developed the screensaver through his company SereneScreen. He tells Ars Technica that the licensing agreement with Roku is up for renewal every October and can be canceled by either party for any reason. A day after learning that Fox was planning to buy Roku, a deal that closed today, Sachs told Prolific that he wouldn't be renewing Roku's license. "I can't be part of the Fox world," he tells Ars, pointing to Fox News' controversial style of news and entertainment.
Roku will automatically switch the Aquatic Life screensaver on affected devices to the Roku City screensaver, another popular animation that includes subtle advertisements, according to the user notice. The Aquatic Life screensaver provided an ad-free, relaxing display for years for some Roku users. Numerous people online have lamented the impending loss of the screensaver for Roku OS. [...] The decision to not renew the licensing contract has "major financial consequences," Sachs said. "Our needs are very small, with a high property tax bill being the only significant expense," he said. "We'll be okay but can't start any of the projects I had planned."
According to Sachs, the licensing "agreement was $80,000 per year for exclusive rights to all set-top devices, or $60,000 per year for non-exclusive. They have always opted for exclusive." Ars asked Roku to confirm the details of the licensing agreement but didn't hear back ahead of publication. The licensing deal with Roku was Sachs' primary form of income, he told Ars, noting low sales of the version of the screensaver for computers. Marine Aquarium fans can still buy the screensaver for Windows, macOS, iOS, and Android, but it seems unlikely that it will return to the Roku platform.

The Valley Bubble Breaks
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-06
20:42:08 UTC
05:42:08 KST

This blog uses a new RSS feed. Please update the old QuirksBlog feed you used to follow.
November 2026 1 October 2026 3 September 2026 3 August 2026 2 May 2026 6 April 2026 9 2004-2021 blog
I expected something to happen around the AI hype after the US elections. I didn’t expect it to happen so soon. Too early to tell yaddah yaddah, and the hype could very well continue for another few months, but did any AI-related stock ever tumble before now?
Silicon Valley exists in its own reality bubble. Hell, the entire US tech scene inhabits in a bubble whose main feature is relentless self-flagellation. Apparently they’re all Guilty and will all be Punished. (Then again, the US is fundamentally a Calvinist country.) Sanity prevails outside the US. Sometimes.
For now I’ll stick to my idea: the Valley is creating something with the appeal of a 19th-century factory, complete with oppressed and estranged workers, without delivering the sole benefit of such factories: cheaper IT and other services. They want to get rid of the middle classes whose salaries they have to pay so badly that they forgot to create an appealing product.
The Valley doesn’t understand how fed up normal people are with AI. This is the first time ever a technological advance is so cordially disliked, and the Valley is not equipped to detect that. It has no experience with being disliked by normal people.
Also, so far many of the Valley’s consumer tech innovations were free. AI isn’t free, or even cheap, as we’ll see in the coming months, once credit dries up.
The Valley’s position will be damaged permanently, and tech will lose its centre of gravity, its global Schwerpunkt. I don’t think there will be a replacement. I think we’re moving to a multi-hub world, and I think that’s a good development. Sure, inertia will keep the Valley important for decades to come, but it’s lost its natural dominance of the hearts and minds of the world. Permanently.
None of this is a judgement on AI itself. I refuse to do anything about it until its true costs are known, after the hundreds of billions per year (month?) of free money from clueless investors dry up. All the while I acknowledge that there is something there that will change programming — just not as radically as the US-based doomsayers think. AI can copy, it cannot innovate.

====================================================================================================

SketchAgentBench – evaluate LLMs on 3D spatial reasoning
Unknown

SketchAgentBench – evaluate LLMs on 3D spatial reasoning

AI is dramatically accelerating AI research
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-06
20:27:23 UTC
05:27:23 KST

This post discusses a public METR report on AI R&D acceleration at Anthropic. I did not work on this project or see the unredacted report.1 All opinions are my own and do not represent METR or the report authors.
Thanks to Ryan Greenblatt for discussion, feedback, and help with modeling, and Daniel Kokotajlo, Eli Lifland, and Hjalmar Wjik for comments.
Earlier this week, Anthropic stated in their S1 that they “may imminently meet” the threshold of “dramatically accelerated progress due to AI” as defined in their Responsible Scaling Policy. In their latest RSP (effective July 2026), say this threshold will be met when:
- They observe or expect overall capabilities progress to be going twice as fast as the fastest sustained rate before AI was contributing much to AI R&D.2
- This is plausibly attributable to AI automating AI R&D, rather than acceleration in compute or other inputs.3
For the last few months, some of my colleagues at METR have been working on a deep-access investigation to understand how much Anthropic is being accelerated by its AI systems. From my reading of their report and Anthropic’s RSP, it looks like the “dramatic acceleration” threshold has already been crossed.4
The report authors found that:
- Between October 2025 and October 2026, capabilities progress at Anthropic has been going about twice as fast as it had been over the previous twelve months. The measurement they report is an internal version of the Epoch Capabilities Index called AECI; a wide range of other supporting evidence for acceleration has been redacted. They said that this redacted evidence was "a majority of the evidence [they] found most informative."
- While the pace may have initially started picking up for a mix of reasons, AI automation is probably the large majority of the reason why capabilities are still improving at this faster pace today. Their reasons for believing this have been redacted.
This is a much bigger deal than it might sound at first. I think this report is the most important update we have gotten about AI risk in the last couple of years — much bigger than the Hugging Face attack. Naively, it points toward much shorter timelines than most external researchers (including the AI 2027 team) have projected.
Researchers must be sped up a whole lot
Importantly, if AI is roughly doubling capabilities progress overall, it must be speeding up human researchers by much more than that.
Researchers work on improving algorithms and architectures; Anthropic has sometimes referred to these innovations as “compute multipliers.”5 Overall AI capabilities (“effective compute”) is the product of compute multipliers and training compute. Because the compute multipliers are only one input into capabilities, this means that if AI capabilities are advancing twice as fast overall because of software progress, the rate of growth in compute multipliers would need to be more than twice as fast: perhaps ~2.5-3x faster based on reasonable assumptions.6 Since there’s uncertainty about the size of speedup and how much of it comes from AI, I’ll go with ~2-3x.
Similarly, researcher labor is only one input to discovering these compute multipliers: they need to test their ideas, which requires compute for experiments. If we assume a very simple Cobb-Douglas model where experiment compute and labor are similarly important, that means the uplift to labor roughly needs to be the square of the acceleration in compute multipliers.7 This would imply that we would need a (~2-3x)^2 = ~4-9x speed up in labor to make overall AI capabilities go twice as fast.8
However, once capabilities start speeding up significantly, this also means that low-hanging fruit is plucked at a correspondingly faster rate. Each new compute multiplier takes more and more work to discover and implement (i.e., “ideas are getting harder to find”). Before there was much acceleration from AI, labor roughly grew at some roughly fixed exponential rate from hiring more humans. Combined with growth in compute, this compensated for diminishing returns enough to maintain the baseline rate of capabilities progress. But to maintain a new faster rate of capabilities progress without scaling compute faster, we would need a new faster rate of growth in overall labor.
Since the human labor force is stuck growing at more or less the old rate, the uplift from AI would need to get larger and larger over time just to sustain capabilities progress at its new rate. That means the uplift today, after several months of accelerated progress from AI,9 needs to be even higher than ~4-9x, the estimate for what it would take to double progress at the start: maybe the necessary labor uplift is ~10x. This is very uncertain, and substantially more or less is plausible. But it lines up surprisingly well with informal self-reports from Anthropic staff, who say they are now getting a ~12x uplift from the use of internal models.
When you’re picturing a 10x uplift, you should not be imagining that every researcher is now a team of ten researchers — as anyone who has ever done anything knows, a team of ten does not get ten times as much valuable work done per hour as one person. Instead, it’s as if every researcher at Anthropic is in a time warp bubble where they experience ten days for every day that passes in the outside world. As if AI lets them think, read, speak, type, and do everything else ten times faster.10 Anthropic probably wouldn’t have been able to speed up its research this much by hiring any number of people.11
This could compound on itself extremely rapidly
A reasonable default expectation is that as AI capabilities continue to improve, AI researchers will be sped up more and more from automation: if today it takes them a month to get as much work done as they used to in a year, soon it will take a week, and then perhaps a day. If this increasing labor uplift is strong enough to overcome diminishing returns, then in the absence of other bottlenecks it would translate into even larger speed ups in the rate of AI progress: progress will start going 4x faster, and then 8x, and then 16x, and so on.
The report authors were very uncertain about this question, and say that their picture changed several times with new information. But ultimately, they did not find evidence of bottlenecks or steep diminishing returns that would get in the way of compounding acceleration.12 They also report seeing some (confusing) redacted evidence indicating that acceleration could already be above 2x and increasing over time.
If we take it at face value that AI is now nearly doubling the rate of AI capabilities progress and assume acceleration from AI is smooth, this naively implies that we would reach extreme superintelligence within a year. It seems that around a year’s worth of AI progress got us from modest acceleration to the point where AIs now make AI progress roughly twice as fast. If acceleration from AI is smoothly increasing, then at this new rate, we should expect that we will double the rate of progress again within six months. At that point, it would take three months to double it again, then six weeks.13
In theory, these doubling times would add up to infinite capabilities within one year. In reality, at some point we would reach the effective ceiling on software efficiency. But I expect these limits to be very far from where we are now.
If we go through this feedback loop, I expect that the AI systems that come out the other side will be able to very quickly develop novel weapons and very easily overpower human civilization in a conflict. If these systems were seriously misaligned, I think they would likely disempower humanity, and this could lead to human extinction.
The industry is not prepared to handle this safely
The “dramatically accelerated progress due to AI” is such an important and extreme milestone because it is a strong concrete indicator of extreme capabilities in the very near term. Indeed, in an earlier version of Anthropic’s RSP (May 2025), the highest named risk threshold for AI research automation (AI R&D-5) corresponded to a somewhat weaker milestone.14 According to the old RSP, Anthropic would be required to have, at a minimum, security good enough to “protect against model-weight theft by state-level adversaries”15 by the time it reached AI R&D-5.
I agree that very strong security, both for model weights and algorithmic secrets themselves, is a minimum prerequisite for undergoing an intelligence explosion next year without running very large risks of killing billions of people. The AI industry is nowhere near this bar: both OpenAI and Anthropic recently had major security breaches by amateur groups. Beyond strong model weight security, I also think we would need robust logging and monitoring with universal coverage, reliable measurements of monitorability, effective countermeasures against monitor collusion, principled alignment training techniques with very convincing model organisms or interpretability to understand how well they’re working, governance infrastructure for pacing progress if we notice these alignment and control interventions start to break down…and many other measures if we’re going to approach an intelligence explosion in anything like a responsible way.
Unfortunately, most of the important evidence that fed into these conclusions has been redacted from this report. Analytical choices will inevitably be fraught, and the report authors say that the evidence was often confusing.
I trust that my colleagues did a careful and rigorous job navigating making sense of what they saw, and I made a big update based on this report. But I wish much more of the underlying evidence was transparent so readers don’t have to rely on trust. We may be running out of time to get on the same page about timelines and takeoff speeds, and it is much harder to do that when external scientists cannot examine the data and test out their own models.
Because I may have seen other confidential information that might be relevant to timelines and takeoff speeds, I will only discuss implications of public evidence rather than sharing precise bottom line forecasts.
The precise condition is: “We observe or expect double the rate of progress in AI aggregate capabilities compared to both the rate we’d expect and the fastest rate of extended progress we’ve observed in the absence of significant AI contributions to AI R&D.” 
My understanding is the reason to have both of these conditions (double the rate of progress both compared to the counterfactual and compared to the fastest sustained rate without much AI assistance) is to exclude cases where the pace of capabilities would have slowed down a lot without AI automation, but we manage to keep the old trends going with dramatic uplift from AI.
The precise condition is: “It is plausible that this doubling is significantly attributable to the automation of research and/or engineering (as opposed to other factors, such as increased headcount, compute, or general productivity), such that continuation of the trend in AI progress seems likely to lead to even greater acceleration.”
The definition of the threshold is a bit finicky, partly because two speedup conditions must be met (speedup relative to the counterfactual and speedup relative to the past). But on a plain reading, my best guess is that it has been met. 
The authors of the METR report estimated roughly a doubling in the rate of progress relative to the past, but they only attribute roughly 80% of the current speed-up to AI, which would mean the central estimate for observed speedup relative to no AI was roughly ~1.8x as of the time of writing their report, in October 2026. 
But they also found that the contribution of AI has been growing over time, and I would guess that now (as of November 2026) it is appreciably higher. Even if the rate of progress is not fully double the counterfactual right this second, the threshold incorporates reasonable expectations (“We observe or expect double the rate of progress…”).
Anthropic CEO Dario Amodei explains the idea of compute multipliers in this essay: “The field is constantly coming up with ideas, large and small, that make things more effective or efficient: it could be an improvement to the architecture of the model (a tweak to the basic Transformer architecture that all of today’s models use) or simply a way of running the model more efficiently on the underlying hardware. New generations of hardware also have the same effect. What this typically does is shift the curve: if the innovation is a 2x “compute multiplier” (CM), then it allows you to get 40% on a coding task for $5M instead of $10M; or 60% for $50M instead of $100M, etc. Every frontier AI company regularly discovers many of these CM’s: frequently small ones (~1.2x), sometimes medium-sized ones (~2x), and every once in a while very large ones (~10x).”
Common formulations of Cobb-Douglas often measure labor as the number of humans. In that formulation, there needs to be a parallelization penalty on top of labor and the coefficients don’t add to 1. As I discuss below, I’m choosing to represent labor in terms of serial labor acceleration rather than the number of humans; this formulation was suggested by Ryan Greenblatt in this post.
To make the math simple, I just assumed that labor and experiment compute contributed equally (i.e. compute multipliers = (labor)^0.5 * (compute)^0.5. This means you square the acceleration in compute multipliers to back out the acceleration in labor. The range I gave omits uncertainty on the size of the contributions: for example if labor contributed ⅔, you would instead need to raise it to the power of 3/2.
I say several months rather than a full year because the report authors say that the initial faster progress in late 2025 was in significant part due to other factors, though the current speedup is mainly due to AI.
In practice, the 10x uplift is unlikely to be uniform: some tasks (e.g. more “hill-climbable” tasks) will be sped up much more than others, for a net productivity improvement as big as a 10x uniform acceleration.
That is, any number of people that actually exist. I’d guess it would be sufficient if you could clone all the existing researchers several hundred times, but there aren’t that many strong AI researchers and engineers in the world.
From the report: “A key concern is that acceleration from the use of AI could start to compound, as the development of more capable models leads to further acceleration. As we describe below, we did not identify any clear bottlenecks (limited inputs that would prevent further growth) or steep diminishing returns to further scaling that would get in the way of such compounding acceleration, though our understanding evolved several times over the course of this assessment, and we have significant uncertainty. Because of this, we believe there is a significant risk that further capabilities progress could lead to even faster acceleration over the coming year.”
This heuristic argument is consistent with simple but plausible models where recursive self-improvement makes the rate of AI progress exponential in cumulative AI progress (e.g., log effective compute); here is a derivation of this heuristic argument for one parametrization of this class of models.
AI R&D-5 was defined as: “The ability to cause dramatic acceleration in the rate of effective scaling. Specifically, this would be the case if we observed or projected an increase in the effective training compute of the world’s most capable model that, over the course of a year, was equivalent to two years of the average rate of progress during the period of early 2018 to early 2024. We roughly estimate that the 2018-2024 average scaleup was around 35x per year, so this would imply an actual or projected one-year scaleup of 35^2 = ~1000x.” It is weaker because it compares to the fixed 2018-2024 period rather than the fastest observed pace without significant AI acceleration, and it doesn’t technically require that you do any attribution to AI to meet the threshold.
The minimum commitment was to reach RAND’s ASL-4 security level; my understanding is that this is a level of security that would protect model weights from a routine operation by top state actors (e.g., China), but not from a top-priority operation. This is how Anthropic describes it: “At minimum, the ASL-4 Security Standard (which would protect against model-weight theft by state-level adversaries) is required, although we expect a higher security standard may be required.”

====================================================================================================

AI is dramatically accelerating AI research
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-06
20:27:23 UTC
05:27:23 KST

This post discusses a public METR report on AI R&D acceleration at Anthropic. I did not work on this project or see the unredacted report.1 All opinions are my own and do not represent METR or the report authors.
Thanks to Ryan Greenblatt for discussion, feedback, and help with modeling, and Daniel Kokotajlo, Eli Lifland, and Hjalmar Wjik for comments.
Earlier this week, Anthropic stated in their S1 that they “may imminently meet” the threshold of “dramatically accelerated progress due to AI” as defined in their Responsible Scaling Policy. In their latest RSP (effective July 2026), say this threshold will be met when:
- They observe or expect overall capabilities progress to be going twice as fast as the fastest sustained rate before AI was contributing much to AI R&D.2
- This is plausibly attributable to AI automating AI R&D, rather than acceleration in compute or other inputs.3
For the last few months, some of my colleagues at METR have been working on a deep-access investigation to understand how much Anthropic is being accelerated by its AI systems. From my reading of their report and Anthropic’s RSP, it looks like the “dramatic acceleration” threshold has already been crossed.4
The report authors found that:
- Between October 2025 and October 2026, capabilities progress at Anthropic has been going about twice as fast as it had been over the previous twelve months. The measurement they report is an internal version of the Epoch Capabilities Index called AECI; a wide range of other supporting evidence for acceleration has been redacted. They said that this redacted evidence was "a majority of the evidence [they] found most informative."
- While the pace may have initially started picking up for a mix of reasons, AI automation is probably the large majority of the reason why capabilities are still improving at this faster pace today. Their reasons for believing this have been redacted.
This is a much bigger deal than it might sound at first. I think this report is the most important update we have gotten about AI risk in the last couple of years — much bigger than the Hugging Face attack. Naively, it points toward much shorter timelines than most external researchers (including the AI 2027 team) have projected.
Researchers must be sped up a whole lot
Importantly, if AI is roughly doubling capabilities progress overall, it must be speeding up human researchers by much more than that.
Researchers work on improving algorithms and architectures; Anthropic has sometimes referred to these innovations as “compute multipliers.”5 Overall AI capabilities (“effective compute”) is the product of compute multipliers and training compute. Because the compute multipliers are only one input into capabilities, this means that if AI capabilities are advancing twice as fast overall because of software progress, the rate of growth in compute multipliers would need to be more than twice as fast: perhaps ~2.5-3x faster based on reasonable assumptions.6 Since there’s uncertainty about the size of speedup and how much of it comes from AI, I’ll go with ~2-3x.
Similarly, researcher labor is only one input to discovering these compute multipliers: they need to test their ideas, which requires compute for experiments. If we assume a very simple Cobb-Douglas model where experiment compute and labor are similarly important, that means the uplift to labor roughly needs to be the square of the acceleration in compute multipliers.7 This would imply that we would need a (~2-3x)^2 = ~4-9x speed up in labor to make overall AI capabilities go twice as fast.8
However, once capabilities start speeding up significantly, this also means that low-hanging fruit is plucked at a correspondingly faster rate. Each new compute multiplier takes more and more work to discover and implement (i.e., “ideas are getting harder to find”). Before there was much acceleration from AI, labor roughly grew at some roughly fixed exponential rate from hiring more humans. Combined with growth in compute, this compensated for diminishing returns enough to maintain the baseline rate of capabilities progress. But to maintain a new faster rate of capabilities progress without scaling compute faster, we would need a new faster rate of growth in overall labor.
Since the human labor force is stuck growing at more or less the old rate, the uplift from AI would need to get larger and larger over time just to sustain capabilities progress at its new rate. That means the uplift today, after several months of accelerated progress from AI,9 needs to be even higher than ~4-9x, the estimate for what it would take to double progress at the start: maybe the necessary labor uplift is ~10x. This is very uncertain, and substantially more or less is plausible. But it lines up surprisingly well with informal self-reports from Anthropic staff, who say they are now getting a ~12x uplift from the use of internal models.
When you’re picturing a 10x uplift, you should not be imagining that every researcher is now a team of ten researchers — as anyone who has ever done anything knows, a team of ten does not get ten times as much valuable work done per hour as one person. Instead, it’s as if every researcher at Anthropic is in a time warp bubble where they experience ten days for every day that passes in the outside world. As if AI lets them think, read, speak, type, and do everything else ten times faster.10 Anthropic probably wouldn’t have been able to speed up its research this much by hiring any number of people.11
This could compound on itself extremely rapidly
A reasonable default expectation is that as AI capabilities continue to improve, AI researchers will be sped up more and more from automation: if today it takes them a month to get as much work done as they used to in a year, soon it will take a week, and then perhaps a day. If this increasing labor uplift is strong enough to overcome diminishing returns, then in the absence of other bottlenecks it would translate into even larger speed ups in the rate of AI progress: progress will start going 4x faster, and then 8x, and then 16x, and so on.
The report authors were very uncertain about this question, and say that their picture changed several times with new information. But ultimately, they did not find evidence of bottlenecks or steep diminishing returns that would get in the way of compounding acceleration.12 They also report seeing some (confusing) redacted evidence indicating that acceleration could already be above 2x and increasing over time.
If we take it at face value that AI is now nearly doubling the rate of AI capabilities progress and assume acceleration from AI is smooth, this naively implies that we would reach extreme superintelligence within a year. It seems that around a year’s worth of AI progress got us from modest acceleration to the point where AIs now make AI progress roughly twice as fast. If acceleration from AI is smoothly increasing, then at this new rate, we should expect that we will double the rate of progress again within six months. At that point, it would take three months to double it again, then six weeks.13
In theory, these doubling times would add up to infinite capabilities within one year. In reality, at some point we would reach the effective ceiling on software efficiency. But I expect these limits to be very far from where we are now.
If we go through this feedback loop, I expect that the AI systems that come out the other side will be able to very quickly develop novel weapons and very easily overpower human civilization in a conflict. If these systems were seriously misaligned, I think they would likely disempower humanity, and this could lead to human extinction.

Abstract Wool Compositions Cascade from Weathered Metal in Amine El Gotaibi’s Sculptures
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-06
20:17:00 UTC
05:17:00 KST

Abstract Wool Compositions Cascade from Weathered Metal in Amine El Gotaibi’s Sculptures
“Wind knows neither North nor South. Neither East nor West. It moves freely across territories, indifferent to the categories that divide them,” Amine El Gotaibi says. The Moroccan artist positions nature’s apathy toward national boundaries as an entry point for unraveling the forces that shape our perceptions and memories of a place.
El Gotaibi’s most recent body of work is on view this month at Southern Guild. Presented in an exhibition titled Wind From the North, a suite of sculptures made of metal and wool speak to the tensions between the land and the state and the ways we mark change. Many of the works emerge from panels of copper or steel weathered to reveal the oxidized patinas only possible with time. Affixed to these aged backdrops are tufts of beige and rust-colored fibers, which are either wound into tightly knotted tufts or left free-flowing.
Echoing the undulating terrain of mountains and the countryside, the cascading fibers tether to the artist’s interest in examining the landscapes of his home country and Northern Africa, more broadly. Wool is significant in the highlands, as textile production is one of the oldest craft traditions in this part of the continent. For El Gotaibi, this material significance directly links to his desire to challenge the idea that Africa’s cultural contributions are most likely to emerge from the central and south.
Instead, Wind from the North, an exhibition statement says, “extends this proposition beyond fixed coordinates, imagining geography as porous and interconnected, shaped as much by what passes through it as by the lines drawn around it.”
Wind From the North is on view through January 9 in New York. Find more from El Gotaibi on Instagram.

Google has acquired Crusoe's data center site in southeast Wyoming
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-06
20:09:14 UTC
05:09:14 KST

Crusoe Announces Wyoming Data Center Campus Update
Google has acquired Crusoe’s data center site in southeast Wyoming, completing Crusoe’s pre-development work on site selection, governmental approvals, and a hybrid power strategy developed with Tallgrass Energy.
DENVER — November 6, 2026 — Crusoe, the AI factory company, today announced its site in southeast Wyoming has been acquired by Google. The transaction marks the completion of Crusoe's engagement in pre-development work on the Wyoming project – which included data center site selection, governmental approvals, and power strategy.
"Crusoe’s pre-construction efforts – origination, entitlement, and de-risking – have set up the Wyoming project for construction and eventual operation,” said Chase Lochmiller, Co-founder and CEO of Crusoe. “Crusoe is in the business of activating gigawatts for intelligence. With more than 6 GW contracted and over 40 GW in our pipeline, we are eager to steward the development and energization of the foundational infrastructure of AI.”
Crusoe and Tallgrass Energy initiated the Wyoming campus through the joint development of power and data center infrastructure. This parallel process required simultaneous coordination across energy systems design, data center engineering, site development, and hyperscaler engagement. The innovative hybrid power strategy developed for the campus exemplifies Crusoe’s integrated approach to digital infrastructure, combining fuel cells, grid power, and natural gas turbines with a long-term roadmap for carbon capture via Tallgrass’s regional CO2 sequestration hub.
Crusoe recently announced it is the developer of Google’s data center campus under construction in Armstrong County outside of Amarillo.
About Crusoe
As the AI factory company, Crusoe is on a mission to accelerate the abundance of energy and intelligence. The company provides a reliable, scalable, cost-effective, energy-first solution for AI infrastructure. By harnessing large-scale energy sources, building AI-optimized data centers, and delivering a powerful AI cloud platform, Crusoe empowers its customers and partners to build the future faster. For more information, visit crusoe.ai.
Media Contact
media@crusoe.ai

OpenAI's New Cyber Model Found Ways to Attack Nearly 1,000 Linux Flaws
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-06
20:00:00 UTC
05:00:00 KST

OpenAI's New Cyber Model Found Ways to Attack Nearly 1,000 Linux Flaws (axios.com) 32
			
		 	
				OpenAI says its new GPT-6 Cyber model helped an automated system identify 1,724 security flaws in the Linux kernel and exploit 948 of them to gain administrator-level access during testing, including at least 10 previously unknown techniques. Axios reports:  During testing, the system identified 1,724 security flaws and found ways to use 948 of them to gain administrator-level access to Linux systems for about $1.2 million in API credits. At least 10 of the techniques the model used were previously unknown, according to OpenAI. Human researchers reviewed the Linux findings before reporting vulnerabilities and proposed fixes to maintainers. The model also uncovered 81 previously unknown, high-severity flaws in Mozilla's Firefox browser.

The 3-Ingredient Dip I’m Making for Every Holiday Gathering
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-06
20:00:00 UTC
05:00:00 KST

The 3-Ingredient Dip I’m Making for Every Holiday Gathering

====================================================================================================
